  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when punishment is inflicted by the entire group, human subjects are more likely to cooperate (i.e., contribute to the public good) than when punishment is uncoordinated, even though the material cost to defectors is held constant. The authors attribute this to the norm-signaling function of group punishment, which is hypothesized to activate both instrumental and normative motivations for compliance. Agent-based simulations support the idea that norm salience, as modeled in the EMIL-I-A architecture, can explain the observed human behavior. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (contribution rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of punishment (specifically, whether it is coordinated by the group or uncoordinated) can significantly affect cooperation rates in public goods games, even when the material incentives are held constant. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that group punishment may lead to higher efficiency than uncoordinated punishment, but this is inferred from contribution rates rather than directly measured. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of group punishment structure and norm-signaling on cooperation, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency when combined with other sources.

- source: Zhou, X. Y., Alysandratos, T., & Naef, M. (2023). Rice Farming and the Origins of Cooperative Behaviour. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that (1) participants from wetland rice regions contribute more in both the no-punishment and punishment conditions of a repeated PGG, and (2) participants from rice regions assign more punishment points to free riders than those from non-rice regions. The difference in contribution is larger in the punishment condition. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The results are robust to a wide range of controls and IV strategies, and are interpreted as evidence of persistent cultural norms of cooperation and punishment rooted in historical rice farming.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment option increases contribution rates in a repeated PGG, and that the magnitude of this effect is moderated by cultural background (rice vs. non-rice regions). However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for predicting contribution behavior under different cultural backgrounds, but not for direct efficiency prediction as required by the downstream task.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2020). Heterogeneous groups cooperate in public good problems despite normative disagreements about individual contribution levels. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a linear public goods game with peer punishment and heterogeneous returns, experimentally induced normative disagreement within groups does not reduce cooperation (measured by average contributions) or increase punishment. Instead, the average normative view within a group predicts the group's contribution level. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that joint payoffs are maximized at full contribution, and that observed contribution levels are lower than this maximum, consistent with underprovision. The presence of punishment maintains cooperation at moderate levels, but does not eliminate the effect of group-mean norms. The study does not include a no-punishment control, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of normative disagreement in a linear PGG with peer punishment, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, nor does it include a no-punishment control. For prediction tasks, it shows that, in a 3-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGG with punishment (cost 1, impact 3), with full information about others' contributions and payoffs but anonymous punishment, group-mean normative views are a strong predictor of contribution levels. The presence of punishment maintains moderate cooperation, but the paper does not allow direct estimation of the efficiency gain from enabling punishment versus disabling it. The results suggest that, under these parameters, punishment supports cooperation even in the presence of normative disagreement, but the efficiency effect relative to no-punishment is not measured.

- source: Molleman, L., Kölle, F., Starmer, C., & Gächter, S. (2019). People prefer coordinated punishment in cooperative interactions. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that in a one-shot, three-player binary public goods game with a costly punishment stage, a substantial fraction of participants prefer to punish free riders only if another group member also punishes (coordinated punishment). This preference is especially prevalent among those who do not punish unconditionally. The presence of coordinated punishment is robust across two large samples and is not correlated with conditional cooperation preferences. The study provides detailed behavioral evidence on how punishment decisions are conditioned on others' actions, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment in public goods games, especially the prevalence and motivations for coordinated punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it compare efficiency between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether it is coordinated) may matter, but does not provide quantitative or comparative efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, which may help in mapping to other studies that do report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Mischkowski, D., Glöckner, A., & Lewisch, P. (2018). From spontaneous cooperation to spontaneous punishment - Distinguishing the underlying motives driving spontaneous behavior in first and second order public good games. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in both one-shot and iterated public goods games with a punishment option, individuals who decide to punish quickly invest more in punishment, especially if they contributed more than their peers and feel highly upset. The spontaneous punishment effect is not moderated by dispositional pro-sociality (SVO), but by situational factors (above-average contribution and negative affect). The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, so it does not provide evidence on whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on the psychological and situational determinants of punishment behavior in public goods games, but does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. However, it does confirm that punishment is used more by above-average contributors and those experiencing negative affect, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is 1:4. The game design is a standard PGG with continuous contributions, four players, two rounds (Study 1) or one round (Study 2), no chat, and full information about others' contributions. The absence of efficiency or payoff analysis limits the paper's direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Albrecht, F., Kube, S., & Traxler, C. (2018). Cooperation and norm enforcement - The individual-level perspective. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds substantial heterogeneity in both cooperation and punishment patterns. About half of subjects are pro-social punishers, and about 40% are non-punishers. There is only a moderate correlation between cooperation and punishment types: many conditional cooperators do not punish, and some free-riders do punish pro-socially. In repeated public goods games with punishment, groups with more pro-social punishers achieve higher and more stable contribution levels, even after controlling for the number of conditional cooperators. Regression analyses show that the number of pro-social punishers in a group is a stronger predictor of group contribution levels than the number of conditional cooperators. However, the paper does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in linear public goods games with punishment, the presence of pro-social punishers in a group increases contribution levels. This suggests that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoffs. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is informative about behavioral mechanisms (contributions and punishment) but not about the net payoff effects of punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the experimental setup matches the canonical PGG with punishment, so the findings are highly relevant for understanding how group composition and punishment opportunities affect cooperation rates, but not directly efficiency.

- source: Gallier, C. (2020). Democracy and compliance in public goods games. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a non-deterrent contribution rule in a public goods game increases contributions, regardless of whether it is imposed exogenously or chosen democratically. The aggregate increase in contributions is primarily due to the presence of the rule itself, not the democratic process. Democratic participation (voting) does not have a statistically significant effect on contributions at the aggregate level after accounting for the direct effect of the rule, self-selection, and information effects. At the individual level, participants who are overruled in the group vote tend to reduce their contributions, but this negative effect is counteracted by a small democracy premium if the rule is democratically chosen. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent automatic sanction, the introduction of the rule (punishment) increases contributions, but the effect is not due to democratic participation per se. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing a weak punishment mechanism on contribution rates, but not directly on efficiency or group payoff, as these are not reported. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contribution, etc.) are clearly specified and can inform parameterization of prediction models. However, since efficiency is not reported, the paper's quantitative results cannot be directly used for efficiency prediction, but the qualitative pattern (punishment increases cooperation even when non-deterrent) is robust.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Nettle, D., & McElreath, R. (2015). Interactions between personality and institutions in cooperative behaviour in humans. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the opportunity for decentralized punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions to the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect is especially pronounced for individuals high in extraversion, who tend to free-ride in the absence of punishment but increase their contributions when punishment is possible. Agreeableness is associated with higher initial contributions regardless of institution, while neuroticism is associated with lower contributions in both conditions. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in contributions with punishment suggests a likely increase in efficiency, subject to the cost of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions, particularly among certain personality types (notably extraverts). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be quantified from this paper alone. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the punishment mechanism is standard (costly, decentralized, 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio). The findings support the general prediction that punishment increases cooperation, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes for the downstream task.

- source: Weber, T. O., Weisel, O., & Gächter, S. (2018). Dispositional free riders do not free ride on punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the availability of punishment leads dispositional free riders (DFR) to increase their contributions to the public good to levels similar to dispositional conditional cooperators (DCC). Both DFR and DCC are equally likely to punish non-cooperators, and their punishment expenditures and motives are similar, regardless of whether punishment reduces payoff inequality (3:1 ratio) or not (1:1 ratio). The presence of punishment thus increases cooperation among DFR, but the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a one-shot public goods game increases contributions among individuals who would otherwise free ride, and that both free riders and conditional cooperators are willing to punish non-cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does, however, support the expectation that punishment increases cooperation even among those predisposed to free ride, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar game designs. The lack of direct efficiency data is a limitation for quantitative prediction.

- source: Vollan, B., Landmann, A., Zhou, Y. X., Hu, B. L., & Herrmann-Pillath, C. (2017). Cooperation and authoritarian values: An experimental study in China. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent punishment for under-contribution, exogenously imposed punishment rules (authoritarian/top-down) significantly increase average contributions compared to no law or endogenously chosen (democratic) punishment rules. This effect is strongest among participants (especially workers) with high deference to authority, and weaker or absent among those with low deference to authority (especially students). The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but since the game is linear and contributions are the main outcome, higher contributions imply higher efficiency. There is no evidence of a democracy premium in this Chinese sample; instead, authoritarian implementation is more effective at increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear PGG with a mild, non-deterrent punishment, enabling punishment via exogenous (authoritarian) imposition increases average contributions (and thus efficiency) compared to no punishment. The effect is context-dependent: in this Chinese sample, exogenous punishment is more effective than democratic (endogenous) punishment, especially among those with high deference to authority. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on cultural context and the method of rule implementation (exogenous vs. endogenous). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates.

- source: Zhang, Z. S., Song, F., & Song, Z. B. (2020). Promoting knowledge sharing in the workplace: Punishment v. reward. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulation results show that both punishment and reward mechanisms increase the fraction of knowledge contributors compared to no incentive, but punishment is more effective than reward. Mixed mechanisms (punishment plus reward) are less effective than either alone. The relationship between fine/bonus size and knowledge sharing is nonlinear: moderate fines/bonuses maximize knowledge sharing, while very high or low values are less effective. Peer pressure, time pressure, and coworkers' attitudes significantly affect knowledge sharing rates. The main outcome is the behavioral rate of knowledge sharing, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a PGG-like environment, introducing punishment increases the rate of knowledge sharing (contribution) more than reward or mixed incentives, but it does not report on efficiency or group payoff. The results are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical data. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates, not efficiency. The model details (e.g., player count, network structure, incentive parameters) are informative for mapping to design dimensions, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design dimensions.

- source: Bell, A., Zhang, W., & Nou, K. (2016). Pesticide use and cooperative management of natural enemy habitat in a framed field experiment. *Agricultural Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment uses a one-shot public goods game with three treatments: (1) Base (no punishment), (2) UCPun (conditional decision maker can be punished by an unconditional decision maker), and (3) CPun (conditional decision maker can punish an unconditional decision maker). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), but does lead to more sensitive punishment behavior. Responsibility effects (increased cooperation when given punishment power) are stronger than substitution effects. The overall effect of punishment on cooperation is positive, but the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment mechanisms affect conditional cooperation in a one-shot public goods game with four players, MPCR 0.4, and costless punishment. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that enabling punishment increases the responsiveness of cooperators to others' contributions and may increase overall cooperation, but also increases free riding. For prediction tasks, the results imply that punishment is likely to increase cooperation-related behaviors, but the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous due to the rise in free riders. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to quantitatively predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency, but the behavioral mechanisms identified are informative for modeling.

- source: Zhou, Y. X., Chen, S. W., Chen, Y. F., & Vollane, B. (2022). Does parental migration impede the development of the cooperative preferences in their left-behind children? Evidence from a large-scale field experiment in China. *China Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that (1) children's cooperation (measured by contribution in a public goods game) increases with age; (2) paternal migration significantly reduces children's cooperation, while maternal or both-parent migration does not; (3) both exogenous and endogenous punishment mechanisms significantly increase cooperation, with exogenous punishment being more effective; (4) exogenous punishment can offset the negative effect of paternal migration on cooperation. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are most directly about behavioral cooperation rather than payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing punishment mechanisms (especially exogenous punishment) increases cooperation (contributions) in one-shot public goods games with children, and can offset negative effects of paternal absence. However, because the outcome is contribution level rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be mapped to efficiency outcomes with caution. The game design dimensions are well specified (e.g., player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech), and the effect of punishment is robustly positive on contributions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect but informative evidence that punishment is likely to increase efficiency in similar settings, especially when baseline cooperation is low due to social factors.

- source: Zhang, B. Y., An, X. M., & Dong, Y. L. (2021). Conditional cooperator enhances institutional punishment in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary PGGs with three types of players (cooperators, defectors, conditional cooperators), the presence of conditional cooperators does not increase average contribution in standard PGGs or with institutional reward. However, with institutional punishment, conditional cooperators enable high contribution rates even for small punishment sizes, making punishment more effective than reward in promoting cooperation. These findings are robust to the inclusion of decision errors (bounded rationality). The main reported outcome is the stable contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in PGGs with institutional punishment, the presence of conditional cooperators can substantially increase the equilibrium contribution rate, especially for small punishment sizes. However, because the main outcome is contribution rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the direct transfer to efficiency prediction is limited. The model assumes infinite populations and evolutionary dynamics, which may differ from finite, repeated lab or field experiments. The results suggest that, all else equal, punishment is more effective than reward at increasing cooperation rates when conditional cooperators are present, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2015). Group Cooperation without Group Selection: Modest Punishment Can Recruit Much Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulations demonstrate that when punishment is possible and can recruit labor (i.e., induce free riders to cooperate in the future), even modest levels of punishment are sufficient to sustain high levels of cooperation in public goods games. The model relaxes several assumptions of prior models, allowing for probabilistic punishment, continuous cooperation probabilities, individual recognition, and group mixing. The results show that both punishment and cooperation can evolve by individual selection, even in large groups, and that second-order free riders do not prevent the evolution of cooperation. However, the outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation and punishment rates), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in public goods games can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is probabilistic and can recruit labor. The findings suggest that game designs allowing for individual recognition and repeated interactions (mixing ecologies) will see higher cooperation supported by lower punishment rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. Its value is in supporting the mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar empirical designs, but this is not directly quantified here.

- source: Wang, Q. L., Meng, H. R., & Gao, B. (2019). Spontaneous punishment promotes cooperation in public good game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that a group-based punishment mechanism, where the punishment fine is multiplied by the number of punishers, strongly increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. Higher punishment fines and higher enhancement factors both promote cooperation. The main reported outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators under different parameter settings. The model is a spatial PGG with binary (cooperate/defect) choices, and punishment is probabilistically assigned by cooperators to defectors. The results show that cooperation can be sustained at high levels when punishment is strong, but the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing punishment effectiveness (punishment fine) increases cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, especially when the enhancement factor is also high. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates as a function of punishment parameters, but less so for the downstream task of predicting efficiency. The model details (spatial structure, binary choices, group-based punishment) are informative for mapping to similar experimental designs.

- source: Shimao, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2013). Strict or Graduated Punishment? Effect of Punishment Strictness on the Evolution of Cooperation in Continuous Public Goods Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effectiveness of punishment strictness in promoting cooperation depends on the population structure. In spatially structured populations (lattice), strict punishment (high strictness parameter) leads to higher evolved cooperation levels than graduated punishment. In random-matching (well-mixed) populations, graduated punishment (low strictness parameter) is more effective at increasing cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameters for public goods efficiency and punishment effectiveness. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the average cooperation level as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) is moderated by the population structure: strict punishment is more effective in spatially structured settings, while graduated punishment is more effective in well-mixed settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension corresponding to population structure (e.g., fixed groups vs. random rematching) is a key moderator of punishment's effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as informative about contribution/cooperation rates, which are closely related but not identical to efficiency.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Supporting punishment via taxation in a structured population. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game with tax-supported punishment, the prevalence of cooperation, defection, and punishment strategies depends sensitively on the synergy factor, tax level, punishment cost, and fine. When the tax exceeds the cost of punishment, punishing cooperators can dominate. At low tax and high punishment cost, defectors dominate. Intermediate regimes can produce coexistence of multiple strategies, including cyclic dominance. The spatial structure allows for richer dynamics than well-mixed models, including new three-strategy coexistence states. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies depends on game design dimensions such as player group size, number of strategies, spatial structure, tax level, punishment cost, and fine. However, because it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding which parameter regimes are likely to support cooperation or complex coexistence, which may indirectly inform efficiency predictions if one assumes that higher cooperation rates correspond to higher efficiency, but this link is not established or quantified in the paper.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2023). Facilitating the evolution of cooperation through altruistic punishment with adaptive feedback. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing adaptive feedback in punishment intensity—where punishment increases as defection rises and decreases as cooperation rises—can facilitate the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in public goods games. The system can exhibit bistability (converging to either full cooperation with low punishment or full defection with high punishment), stable oscillations between cooperation and defection, or stable full cooperation/defection depending on the average per capita incentive and other parameters. Notably, full cooperation can be achieved and maintained with only low-intensity punishment under certain conditions. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of the replicator dynamics with feedback, not on empirical or experimental data. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so implications for efficiency must be inferred indirectly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that adaptive, feedback-based punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public goods games, sometimes with only low punishment intensity. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that under certain parameter regimes, adaptive punishment could lead to high efficiency (if cooperation is high and punishment costs are low). However, the absence of direct efficiency analysis means that predictions about efficiency effects must be made cautiously and indirectly, based on the reported cooperation dynamics. The model is most relevant for settings with institutional punishment, adaptive feedback, and infinite well-mixed populations, and less directly applicable to finite, experimental, or empirical settings.

- source: Xie, K., Liu, X. W., Wang, H. Z., & Jiang, Y. L. (2023). Multi-heterogeneity public goods evolutionary game on lattice. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a modified pool punishment mechanism and aspiration-driven updating in a multi-heterogeneity public goods game on a lattice can promote cooperation under certain conditions. Specifically, moderate punishment and low aspiration factor are most effective at increasing the fraction of cooperators. Too high punishment or aspiration factor leads to instability or loss of cooperation. The results are robust to different lattice sizes and initial conditions. While average payoffs for cooperators and defectors are reported, the main focus is on cooperation rates, not group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in public goods games with multiple heterogeneities, but only under moderate punishment and low aspiration factor. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic: moderate punishment may improve outcomes, but excessive punishment can reduce both cooperation and payoffs. The lattice structure and aspiration-driven updating are important contextual factors.

- source: Han, D., Yan, S. T., & Li, D. D. (2019). The evolutionary public goods game model with punishment mechanism in an activity-driven network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that increasing the maximal fine of punishment has a greater impact on defectors than on cooperators, but as the fine increases, the final density of cooperators decreases. The maximal cost of punishment mainly affects the prevalence of punishing strategies, with higher costs reducing the number of punishing cooperators. The initial density of cooperators has a counterintuitive effect: a higher initial density leads to a lower final density of cooperators, and vice versa. The results are reported in terms of the densities of strategy types, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies under different parameter settings. While the model structure and parameterization (e.g., punishment cost, fine, network dynamics) are informative for understanding mechanisms, the lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes means this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. It may be useful for understanding how punishment parameters affect cooperation rates, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., Wu, Y. E., Yan, M., & Xie, Y. Y. (2018). Tolerance-based punishment and cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that tolerance-based punishment, where cooperators only punish defectors after a threshold of tolerated defections, can significantly promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. The mechanism reduces the frequency of punishment and allows for higher levels of cooperation under adverse conditions. The effects of punishment fine (\alpha) and tolerance threshold (M) are systematically explored, showing that higher fines and lower tolerance thresholds promote cooperation. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment mechanisms with a tolerance threshold can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games. However, because the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, under similar spatial and parameter conditions, enabling punishment (especially with moderate tolerance) is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if the cost of punishment is not too high. However, the paper does not quantify efficiency or net group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible from this study alone.

- source: Quan, J., Cui, S. H., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Reputation-based probabilistic punishment on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing reputation-based probabilistic punishment into the spatial public goods game increases the frequency of cooperation and average payoffs. Higher reputation thresholds (meaning more defectors are likely to be punished), higher punishment intensity (lower b), and higher punishment fines (beta) all lead to higher cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model shows that cooperative and punishing strategies form spatial clusters with higher reputations and higher payoffs, while defectors cluster in low-reputation, low-payoff regions. The effect on efficiency is inferred from the increase in average payoff and the reduction of defectors, but efficiency as a normalized group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that, in spatial public goods games, enabling punishment (especially when it is reputation-based and probabilistic) increases cooperation and average payoffs compared to control conditions without punishment. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with higher intensity, lower cost, or higher fines) should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when the punishment mechanism targets low-reputation defectors. However, because the paper does not report efficiency as a normalized group payoff, quantitative predictions should be made with caution and may require mapping from average payoff or cooperation rate. The results are most applicable to spatial, agent-based settings with reputation dynamics and probabilistic punishment.

- source: Jaffe, K., & Zaballa, L. (2010). Co-Operative Punishment Cements Social Cohesion. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that co-operative punishment (where the cost of punishing free-riders is shared among group members) is much more effective at maintaining a high proportion of contributors in a simulated public goods game than either no punishment or altruistic punishment (where punishers pay the cost individually). The effectiveness of punishment depends on the ratio of cost to punished versus cost to punisher, and on the efficiency of detecting free-riders. Co-operative punishment allows pro-social behavior to invade and persist in populations of free-riders, while altruistic punishment does not. However, the primary outcome is the proportion of contributors, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the structure of punishment (co-operative vs. individual) is a critical moderator of cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, the maintenance of high contribution rates under co-operative punishment suggests that efficiency would likely be higher in such settings, especially when the cost of punishment is shared and detection of free-riders is effective. For prediction tasks, the results imply that enabling co-operative punishment mechanisms (where costs are distributed) should be expected to yield higher efficiency than individual punishment, all else equal. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Adami, C., & Hintze, A. (2018). Thermodynamics of evolutionary games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a one-dimensional PGG with three players per group, cooperation only emerges when the synergy parameter r exceeds the group size (r > 3). Introducing punishment (with a fine for defectors) shifts the critical point, allowing cooperation to emerge at lower r. The effect of punishment depends on the fine imposed on defectors, not on the cost to punishers. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results are derived analytically and confirmed by agent-based simulations, but neither approach reports group efficiency or welfare directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment increases the fraction of cooperators in a PGG-like environment and lowers the threshold for cooperation. However, it does not report or analyze group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The findings are useful for predicting when cooperation will emerge as a function of game parameters (e.g., punishment fine, group size, synergy), but do not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that, under the studied conditions, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may be associated with higher efficiency in standard PGGs, but this link is not explicitly demonstrated or quantified in the paper.

  findings: The study shows that egoistic punishment can evolve and promote high levels of cooperation in both peer and pool punishment modes, outperforming altruistic punishment in a wider range of parameter settings (especially at higher punishment costs and lower fines). The mechanism is robust to different strategy updating rules and population structures. However, the paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of cooperation and the survival of different strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of the punishment mechanism (egoistic vs. altruistic) and the parameterization of punishment cost and fine can substantially affect cooperation rates in public goods games. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that egoistic punishment mechanisms may be more effective at sustaining cooperation under a broader set of conditions, which could indirectly imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly measured or reported.

- source: Quan, J., Tang, C. X., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). Reputation evaluation with tolerance and reputation-dependent imitation on cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in a spatial public goods game with both punishment and exclusion available, making exclusion the primary sanctioning mechanism (with punishers acting as pure cooperators when excluders are present) leads to a larger parameter region where cooperation dominates, compared to the reverse. The average frequency of cooperation is higher and the threshold for the emergence of cooperation is lower when exclusion leads. The results are robust across variations in the punishment fine, exclusion cost, and synergy factor. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are based on behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a spatial PGG with both punishment and exclusion, exclusion as the leading sanctioning mechanism is more effective at promoting cooperation than punishment as the leading mechanism, across a wide range of parameter values. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that game designs enabling exclusion (rather than punishment) as the primary sanction may yield higher cooperation rates, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The findings are most relevant for settings with spatial structure, multiple sanctioning options, and evolutionary updating.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Effects of interconnections among corruption, institutional punishment, and economic factors on the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Corruption (bribery) reduces the level of cooperation when cooperators are more inclined to bribe, undermining the effectiveness of institutional punishment. (2) Both stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) and richer economic potential (higher pool multiplier) enhance cooperation, but their effects interact with the bribery tendencies of group members. (3) When defectors are more likely to bribe, strong leaders can sustain cooperation under poor economic potential, but in rich economic environments, weaker leaders may be more beneficial. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and the stability of cooperation/defection equilibria, not on direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, corruption, and economic potential interact to affect cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment's effectiveness in promoting cooperation is reduced by corruption, especially when cooperators are more likely to bribe. Stronger punishment and higher economic potential generally expand the basin of attraction for cooperation, but the effect can reverse depending on who bribes more. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of corruption and the relative strength of punishment and economic potential are key moderators of punishment's effect on cooperation, but direct translation to efficiency outcomes requires caution.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2017). Stochastic evolutionary voluntary public goods game with punishment in a Quasi-birth-and-death process. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment and non-participation strategies into a stochastic evolutionary public goods game changes the evolutionary dynamics and the likelihood of the system reaching cooperative, defecting, or non-participation equilibria. Higher punishment probability (alpha) and lower punishment cost (gamma) both increase the parameter regions where the system is likely to reach cooperative/punishing (C+P) equilibria. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it reports the probability of the system selecting each type of equilibrium as a function of the parameters. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under a wide range of conditions, but the effect is moderated by the cost and probability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the likelihood of cooperative/punishing equilibria increases with higher punishment probability and lower punishment cost, and that these effects are sensitive to the marginal per-capita return (r/N) and the fixed income for non-participants. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its findings must be mapped from the probability of cooperative equilibria to expected efficiency. The results suggest that, in parameter regions where the C+P equilibrium is likely, efficiency would be higher than in regions dominated by defection or non-participation. Thus, the paper supports the idea that punishment can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cong, R., Zhao, Q. C., Li, K., & Wang, L. (2017). Individual mobility promotes punishment in evolutionary public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the optimal mobility (migration tendency) of punishers depends on the cost of punishment. For high punishment costs, a medium tendency to migrate (rather than always punishing or always running) maximizes the prevalence of punishers. For low punishment costs, always punishing is optimal. The presence of cooperators (second-order free-riders) changes the dynamics: in harsh environments (low enhancement factor), punishers benefit from moderate migration, but in more favorable environments, cooperators can dominate, and the advantage of punishment diminishes. The model shows that mobility can help punishers survive, but only under certain cost and environmental conditions. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher prevalence of punishers and cooperators is interpreted as beneficial for cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for how punishment, its cost, and the mobility of punishers affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, especially when the cost is not too high and punishers have a moderate migration tendency. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when punishment is not too costly and when the game environment allows for spatial structure and mobility. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Spadaro, G., Graf, C., Jin, S. X., Arai, S., Inoue, Y., Lieberman, E., Rinderu, M. I., Yuan, M. L., Van Lissa, C. J., & Balliet, D. (2022). Cross-Cultural Variation in Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that the presence of sanctions (punishment and/or reward) in social dilemma experiments is associated with significantly higher cooperation rates. Other situational features, such as communication and lower conflict of interest, also increase cooperation. However, the outcome measured is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. No significant cross-societal or cross-cultural differences in cooperation are found after controlling for study characteristics. The effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff is not directly analyzed or reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment or reward mechanisms increases cooperation rates in PGG and PD experiments, across a wide range of societies and experimental designs. However, because the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The paper does not address whether increased cooperation from punishment leads to higher or lower efficiency, as it does not analyze payoff-based outcomes. For the prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment increases cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Wasieleski, D. M., & Hayibor, S. (2009). Evolutionary Psychology and Business Ethics Research. *Business Ethics Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that evolutionary psychology provides a useful framework for understanding cooperation, punishment, and norm enforcement in organizational and business ethics contexts. It reviews evidence from public goods games and related experiments (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, 2002) showing that the availability of punishment increases cooperation rates and reduces free-riding. The review also discusses the concept of strong reciprocity, where individuals are willing to punish norm violators at a cost to themselves, and suggests that such mechanisms are critical for sustaining cooperation in groups. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or directly analyze efficiency or group payoff; instead, it synthesizes existing theoretical and empirical work to propose directions for future research.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and contextual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods games and similar environments, referencing well-known experimental findings. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will increase cooperation and likely improve group outcomes, but it does not provide the detailed, dimension-specific evidence needed for precise prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Capraro, V. (2024). The Dual-Process Approach to Human Sociality: Meta-Analytic Evidence for a Theory of Internalized Heuristics for Self-Preservation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuitive processing (via time pressure, ego depletion, conceptual primes, etc.) increases negative reciprocity (i.e., punishment of norm violations) in economic games, including public goods games with punishment phases and ultimatum games. This is supported by a meta-analysis showing a small but significant increase in punishment/rejection rates when intuition is promoted. However, the review does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is not addressed; the focus is on the cognitive basis of the decision to punish, not on the downstream consequences for group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that intuitive processing increases the likelihood of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, but it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this review is not directly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or welfare outcomes. It is useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior (e.g., cognitive manipulation, individual differences), but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Use this paper to inform models of punishment frequency or behavioral response, not efficiency.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Kiyonari, T., Molenmaker, W. E., & van Dijk, E. (2019). Do people prefer leaders who enforce norms? Reputational effects of reward and punishment decisions in noisy social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across three experimental studies, the paper finds that both rewarding and punishing norm enforcement by leaders in public goods games (and an organizational scenario) generally improve third-party observers' perceptions of leader competence and morality, especially under conditions of perfect monitoring (no noise). Under behavioral noise (imperfect monitoring), the reputational benefits of punishment are reduced or eliminated, while rewarding continues to yield positive reputational effects. These effects extend to leader preference (i.e., observers are more likely to prefer leaders who reward or punish, especially reward, over those who do not enforce norms). However, the studies do not measure or report on actual group efficiency, payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to third-party observer perceptions of leader reputation and preference, not to actual behavioral or payoff outcomes among group members. While the design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost) are described, the outcomes are not efficiency-related. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may, however, inform hypotheses about the reputational incentives for leaders to use punishment or reward, which could indirectly affect norm enforcement behavior in other studies.

- source: Wang, X. F., & Perc, M. (2022). Replicator dynamics of public goods games with global exclusion. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a global exclusion mechanism (a centralized institution that can exclude defectors from the public good) into the public goods game can lead to higher efficiency and average group payoff, provided the cost of exclusion is not too high and the probability of successful exclusion is sufficiently large. The model predicts that, in these parameter regions, cooperation can emerge and be stabilized, sometimes through cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and excluders. The results are robust across a wide range of functional forms for the exclusion probability. When exclusion is too costly or too unlikely, the system reverts to defection and low efficiency. The paper does not analyze empirical data but provides theoretical and numerical phase diagrams supporting these conclusions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games, the addition of a sufficiently effective and not overly costly global exclusion (institutional punishment) mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency compared to the baseline game. The effect is robust to the details of how exclusion is implemented, as long as the exclusion probability is high enough and the cost is not prohibitive. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, when the game design includes a global exclusion mechanism with moderate or low cost and high effectiveness, one should expect a significant increase in efficiency relative to the control. The model also clarifies that if exclusion is too costly or ineffective, efficiency gains will not materialize. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of institutional punishment/exclusion in PGG-like environments.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2020). Evolutionary game dynamics in multiagent systems with prosocial and antisocial exclusion strategies. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in finite well-mixed populations playing a public goods game, prosocial pool exclusion (where cooperators can pay to exclude defectors from benefits) is more effective at promoting cooperation and group efficiency than costly punishment. Even when antisocial exclusion (where defectors exclude cooperators) is introduced, prosocial pool excluders maintain their evolutionary advantage and can dominate the population, especially when second-order sanctioning is allowed. When both exclusion and punishment (prosocial and antisocial) are available, prosocial pool excluders prevail and maximize group payoff/efficiency. Antisocial exclusion alone leads to defection and low efficiency, but does not undermine the effectiveness of prosocial exclusion when both are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with the option for prosocial exclusion, enabling such exclusion mechanisms leads to higher group efficiency and welfare than enabling costly punishment, even in the presence of antisocial sanctioning. The results are robust to the inclusion of second-order sanctioning and to moderate changes in the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). For prediction tasks, the presence of prosocial exclusion as a punishment-like mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, and to outperform standard costly punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to games with continuous contributions, no communication, and fixed group size and rounds, as modeled here.

- source: Xie, K., & Liu, T. J. (2024). The regulation of good and evi promotes cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to both punish defectors and reward cooperators (PEPG strategy) in a spatial public goods game, especially when combined with heterogeneous payoff allocation based on reputation, leads to higher cooperation rates and higher average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is sensitive to the enhancement factor (r), the importance of reputation in payoff allocation (a), and the strength of the bonus-penalty (gamma). Defectors' average payoffs are always lower than those of cooperators in the steady state. The model demonstrates that the union of PEPG and reputation-based heterogeneity is more effective than either alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides simulation evidence that combining punishment and reward (PEPG) with reputation-based heterogeneous payoffs increases cooperation and average payoffs in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (especially when combined with reward and reputation mechanisms) will increase efficiency, particularly when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr, via r), the effectiveness of punishment/reward (gamma), and the importance of reputation (a) are high. However, the model is not a direct empirical test and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative optimum, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Yokoyama, A. (2014). The Effect of Ostracism and Optional Participation on the Evolution of Cooperation in the Voluntary Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in voluntary public goods games, allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) candidates with low reputation (defectors) leads to the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation rates. Optional participation (where individuals can choose whether to join a group based on group reputation) does not sustain cooperation. The strictest exclusion rule (requiring unanimous acceptance for membership) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The model is evolutionary and simulation-based, and outcomes are reported as the proportion of cooperators, not as group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that costless ostracism (exclusion of defectors) is a highly effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, more so than optional participation alone. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the implication is that group efficiency would be higher when ostracism is enabled, since the share of cooperators is much higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costless and takes the form of exclusion, rather than costly point-based punishment. The results are less directly applicable to predicting the quantitative effect of standard costly punishment on efficiency, but they support the qualitative prediction that effective exclusion or ostracism can greatly increase group efficiency if measured.

- source: Sell, J., & Wilson, R. K. (1999). The maintenance of cooperation: Expectations of future interaction and the trigger of group punishment. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that requiring a grim trigger punishment rule (where defection locks a player into permanent defection) substantially increases cooperation rates in a repeated public goods game. The effect of the discount parameter (expected number of rounds) on cooperation is only significant when the grim trigger is imposed, and only between the highest and lower values. Without a mandated punishment phase, increasing the discount parameter does not increase cooperation. The main dependent variable is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that group-level punishment threats can coordinate expectations and sustain cooperation, but the value of future interaction alone is insufficient unless paired with credible punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the imposition of a group-level, long punishment phase (grim trigger) increases cooperation rates in repeated public goods games, especially in the early rounds. However, the outcome measured is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling a strong group punishment mechanism (even if not individual-targeted) will increase cooperation and likely improve efficiency, especially when players expect many rounds. The effect of expected future interaction (discount parameter) is only realized when punishment is credible and severe. For prediction tasks, the presence of a mandated, long punishment phase should be treated as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, but the absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative inference.

- source: Reuben, E., & Tyran, J. R. (2010). Everyone is a winner: Promoting cooperation through all-can-win intergroup competition. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing tolerance-based expulsion in a spatial public goods game can substantially increase the level of cooperation (measured as the fraction of cooperators and expellers). The effect is strongest at intermediate tolerance thresholds and when expulsion costs are high. The mechanism allows expellers to balance the cost of expulsion with the benefit of removing defectors, leading to higher and more stable cooperation levels. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that tolerance-based expulsion mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially when the cost of expulsion is high and the tolerance threshold is optimally set. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation levels rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that enabling punishment (in the form of expulsion) is likely to increase cooperation, but the impact on efficiency is not directly quantified and may depend on the costliness of expulsion and the structure of the population. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.

- source: Sun, X. P., Li, M. Y., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2023). Combined effect of pure punishment and reward in the public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that social exclusion is a more effective strategy than punishment for promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games. Introducing antisocial punishment (defective punishers) helps eliminate second-order free-riding by cooperators who do not bear exclusion costs. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the exclusion probability, antisocial punishment fine, and exclusion/punishment costs. High exclusion probability and low antisocial punishment fine promote cooperation. The model demonstrates that cooperative excluders can protect cooperators from invasion by defectors and defective punishers, and that the combination of social exclusion and antisocial punishment can sustain high cooperation levels. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms like social exclusion and antisocial punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, which may be associated with higher efficiency in practice. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how the structure and parameters of punishment and exclusion mechanisms affect cooperation dynamics, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in similar game designs. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results should be treated as informative about behavioral mechanisms but not as direct evidence.

- source: Liu, L. J., Xiao, Z. L., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Early exclusion leads to cyclical cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, the ability to exclude defectors early (after monitoring) leads to cyclical dominance among cooperators, defectors, and excluders, and maintains high average cooperation rates. If exclusion is delayed, defectors can dominate. The model shows that the timing of exclusion, monitoring cost, and discount factor are key moderators. While the model defines payoffs and uses them in evolutionary dynamics, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff.
