  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it shows that enabling a form of punishment (social exclusion) can sustain high cooperation rates if implemented early, and that the timing and cost of exclusion are critical moderators. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as informative-indirect evidence. The model's structure and parameterization (group size, rounds, exclusion cost, monitoring cost) can inform how similar design dimensions might affect the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated PGGs, but direct quantitative predictions about efficiency are not available.

- source: Gangadharan, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Normative conflict and the limits of self-governance in heterogeneous populations. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Allowing communication and rewards in a public goods game leads to near-maximal efficiency in homogeneous groups, but only partial efficiency in heterogeneous groups where returns from the public good differ. In homogeneous groups, communication increases group earnings to 94% of the maximum, while in heterogeneous groups, the increase is only 80% of the maximum. The main reason is that most heterogeneous groups prioritize equality or compromise between equality and efficiency, rather than maximizing group earnings. Rewards are not systematically used to offset inequality. The loss in efficiency in heterogeneous groups is due both to lower contributions and to under-use of rewards. The study demonstrates that normative conflict can limit the effectiveness of self-governance mechanisms in promoting efficiency in heterogeneous populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling communication and rewards in public goods games depend critically on group homogeneity. In homogeneous groups, such mechanisms can achieve near-maximal efficiency. In heterogeneous groups, even with communication and rewards, efficiency gains are much smaller due to normative conflict between equality and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity in returns (mpcr differences) is a strong moderator that limits the efficiency benefit of enabling communication and rewards. The absence of punishment means the results are most directly applicable to reward-based or communication-based interventions, but the findings on normative conflict are likely relevant for predicting the limits of punishment as well, especially in heterogeneous settings.

- source: Stewart, A. J., Parsons, T. L., & Plotkin, J. B. (2016). Evolutionary consequences of behavioral diversity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in iterated public goods games, increasing the number of available contribution levels (behavioral diversity) can either increase or decrease group efficiency, depending on the ratio of public benefit to individual cost (mpcr). With low mpcr, more choices allow for more robust cooperative strategies and higher mean payoffs. With high mpcr, the abundance of suboptimal robust strategies causes populations to get stuck at lower efficiency. The ability to punish (by reducing one's own contribution in response to defection) is necessary for two-choice strategies to be robust, but explicit costly punishment is not modeled. The paper provides analytic conditions for when simple strategies can resist invasion by more complex ones, and uses evolutionary simulations to show the impact of choice set size on mean payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the number of contribution options (all_or_nothing, player_count, mpcr, etc.), but it does not model explicit costly punishment as a separate mechanism. Instead, 'punishment' is operationalized as the ability to reduce one's own contribution in response to others' defection. The findings suggest that increasing the number of contribution options (behavioral diversity) can either help or hurt efficiency, depending on mpcr. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that more behavioral options do not always increase efficiency, and that the effect depends on the underlying game parameters. However, because explicit costly punishment is not present, the results are only adjacent to the core prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhang, C. Y., & Cao, M. (2015). How insurance affects altruistic provision in threshold public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing insurance for contributors in threshold public goods games increases the provision of the public good and can improve the efficiency (group payoff) of the system. The effect is strongest when the insurance compensation is high and the required threshold for provision is high. The model shows that insurance can shift the evolutionary dynamics to favor contributors over defectors, depending on parameter values. The allocation rule for surplus contributions (parameter omega) also moderates the effect. The paper does not analyze punishment or sanctions, but provides a theoretical basis for how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can promote cooperation and efficiency in threshold public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that risk-mitigation mechanisms (insurance) for contributors can increase efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the risk of loss is high and insurance compensation is generous. While it does not study punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in games where contributors face risk and can be protected from loss. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, adding insurance or similar mechanisms can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The model is most applicable to threshold public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, no communication, and no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of the threshold, insurance compensation, and allocation rules for surplus contributions.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2019). Cleverly handling the donation information can promote cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list partially (i.e., only for those who contribute above a threshold) increases total contributions to the public good compared to publishing all or none of the list. The optimal threshold for publication can be set dynamically as the mean of recent contributions, which is both effective and convenient. The model shows that this approach increases the sum of contributions and reduces the variance in contributions, indicating more uniform and higher cooperation. The effect is robust to changes in network connectivity and the window of historical data used. Gossip and self-assessment of face act as indirect social sanctions, motivating higher contributions. There is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism with costs and impact parameters as in standard PGG punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that indirect social sanctions (via partial publication of contribution information and gossip) can increase total group contributions in a PGG-like environment. While it does not study explicit costly punishment, it suggests that reputational mechanisms can substitute for or complement formal punishment in promoting cooperation and group welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that introducing a reputational/gossip-based sanction (by publicizing contributions above a threshold) can increase group payoff, especially if the threshold is set adaptively. However, the absence of explicit punishment cost/impact parameters means the results are not directly transferable to standard punishment treatments. The sum of contributions is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2011). An Institutional Mechanism for Assortment in an Ecology of Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that capacity constraints (limits on the number of players per game) are a simple and effective institutional mechanism for promoting positive assortment of cooperators in an ecology of public goods games. When group size is limited, cooperators can cluster together and achieve higher payoffs relative to defectors, even without punishment or reputation. Budget constraints (limits on the number of games per player) are less effective. The model does not include punishment, but discusses it as a more complex and potentially costly alternative. The main outcome is the relative cooperator payoff, not group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model punishment or sanctions. However, it is relevant for understanding how institutional design (e.g., group size limits) can promote cooperation and higher payoffs without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that capacity constraints can increase the relative payoff of cooperators, but does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Moreira, J. A., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2013). Evolution of collective action in adaptive social structures. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can adapt their social ties—specifically, break links with defectors more quickly than with cooperators—cooperation can be sustained at higher levels than in well-mixed or static networks. The evolutionary dynamics shift from defection dominance to a scenario with stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, depending on the group size, enhancement factor, and network adaptation rates. Stochastic effects (e.g., mutation, exploration) further help populations escape defection traps and maintain cooperation. However, the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how endogenous partner selection and adaptive network structure can promote cooperation in PGGs, which may indirectly inform predictions about efficiency in environments where such mechanisms are present. However, it does not address punishment or sanctions, nor does it provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that adaptive partner selection can increase cooperation rates, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other interventions.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Hendriks, A., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2016). Think global, act local: Preserving the global commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when participants can observe their neighbors' contributions to the public good and can condition their pairwise cooperation (in a PD) on this information, contributions to the public good are sustained at high levels, even in very large groups (up to 1,000 participants). In the control condition, where such information is not available, contributions decline over time. The mechanism is not direct punishment but rather the withholding of cooperation in a separate pairwise game, which acts as a social sanction. The effect is robust to group size and does not require participants to know the overall level of public good provision. The main outcome is sustained contribution, not measured efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for targeted social sanctions (withholding cooperation in a pairwise PD) based on observed public good contributions can sustain high contribution rates in large groups. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to punishment (withholding cooperation as a sanction) but does not involve costly punishment or explicit payoff reduction. The findings suggest that enabling targeted, observable social sanctions—even if not costly—can have a strong positive effect on contribution rates, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar designs, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Zhang, H. R., & Perc, M. (2016). Evolution of conditional cooperation under multilevel selection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with continuous contribution and multilevel selection, the presence of conditional strategies (modeled as piecewise linear response functions) enables the emergence and stability of high cooperation levels. The dominant strategy is robust to initial conditions and parameter changes. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on average contribution and strategy prevalence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional cooperation strategies can sustain high contribution levels in public goods games under multilevel selection, even without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related dimensions. Its findings are most relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of explicit sanctioning mechanisms, and for interpreting the role of conditional strategies as an alternative to punishment. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., Holt, C. A., Rodgers, M. S., & Thomas-Hunt, M. C. (2020). The impact of leader communication on free-riding: An incentivized experiment with empowering and directive styles. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that empowering leader messages reduce free-riding (increase contributions) more than directive messages when there is no two-way leader-follower communication. When two-way communication is allowed, both leadership styles lead to higher contributions, and the difference between styles disappears. The presence of two-way communication is especially beneficial for directive leadership. The main dependent variable is contribution rate; earnings are reported but not the focus. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that communication (especially two-way leader-follower communication) can increase contributions, which may be relevant for understanding the context in which punishment is or is not necessary. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, communication, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means the findings are only indirectly relevant for the prediction task.

- source: Shirado, H., & Christakis, N. A. (2020). Network Engineering Using Autonomous Agents Increases Cooperation in Human Groups. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing bots that facilitate the breaking of ties with defectors ('disengaged' intervention) in dynamic network public goods games leads to a substantial increase in cooperation rates among human participants, compared to controls (no bots, bots that always cooperate, or tit-for-tat bots). The effect is robust even when only a single bot is present and when humans are aware of the bots' presence. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative players per round. There is some supplementary evidence that, under the disengaged intervention, cooperators earn more than defectors, but group-level efficiency or total group payoff is not the main focus. The study does not involve punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the network exclusion of defectors is interpreted as a form of decentralized ostracism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Instead, it provides evidence about the effect of network engineering (ostracism via tie-breaking) on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that enabling players to break ties with defectors (or having bots facilitate this) can increase cooperation and, to some extent, the earnings of cooperators. However, because efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is not directly measured or reported, and because punishment is not present, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited. It may be useful as adjacent evidence for the effects of exclusion or ostracism mechanisms.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Indirect exclusion can promote cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect exclusion—where a game organizer probabilistically selects cooperators for future participation—can promote the stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors or even lead to the dominance of cooperation in repeated public goods games. The effectiveness of indirect exclusion increases with higher identification probability and more repeated rounds. The model avoids the second-order free-rider problem associated with costly direct exclusion. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium fraction of cooperators and the stationary distribution of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that indirect exclusion mechanisms can increase the equilibrium level of cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially as the number of rounds and the probability of correctly identifying cooperators increase. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment (or exclusion) on efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting changes in cooperation rates rather than efficiency, and the exclusion mechanism studied is costless and indirect, differing from standard costly punishment treatments.

- source: Zhou, T. W., Ding, S., Fan, W. J., & Wang, H. (2016). An improved public goods game model with reputation effect on the spatial lattices. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that embedding a reputation effect into the utility function of agents in a spatial PGG model leads to a substantial increase in the stationary fraction of cooperators, especially as the reputation factor increases. The critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge is reduced, and the region of parameter space supporting full cooperation is expanded. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are based on Monte Carlo simulations and are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. For the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency effects of punishment—this paper is only weakly informative. It suggests that non-monetary social mechanisms (like reputation) can increase cooperation, but does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects efficiency, nor does it report group payoff or welfare. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the reputation mechanism, but not for punishment or efficiency.

- source: Zheng, J. J., Ren, T. Y., Ma, G., & Dong, J. H. (2021). The emergence and implementation of pool exclusion in spatial public goods game with heterogeneous ability-to-pay. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing pool exclusion, where excluders pay a cost to exclude defectors from the public good, can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of exclusion depends on parameters such as the enhancement factor (r), the probability and effectiveness of exclusion, and the contribution rate to the exclusion pool. Cooperation is more likely to prevail when exclusion is effective and not overinvested. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators and excluders, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusionary sanctions (pool exclusion) can increase cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because the main outcome is cooperation level (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the sanction is exclusion rather than classic costly punishment, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that effective, not overly costly exclusion can help sustain cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2017). Publishing the donation list incompletely promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list incompletely (only for those who contribute above a threshold) leads to higher total contributions and more equal contributions among players in a public goods game on a network. There is an optimal threshold for publication that maximizes these effects. The per capita number of neighbors and the amount of historical data players consider also moderate the effect, but the main result is robust: partial transparency outperforms full transparency or secrecy. The model is agent-based and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on social reputation and face loss as motivators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that social information mechanisms (such as partial publication of contribution lists) can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not study monetary punishment or its effect on efficiency. The findings are relevant for predicting the effect of information disclosure or reputation-based interventions, not for predicting the effect of adding costly punishment. The sum of contributions is a proxy for group welfare, but efficiency (as defined by payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported. Use this paper as informative for the effect of social/reputational incentives, not for monetary punishment treatments.

- source: Yang, Z. H., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2013). Effects of adaptive dynamical linking in networked games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that adaptive dynamical linking—where agents lengthen or shorten social ties based on whether their partners cooperate or defect—can dramatically increase the prevalence of cooperation in both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods game. The effect is stronger when agents adjust their links more rapidly (higher adjustment rate, Δ), and under weak selection. The mechanism does not involve any explicit punishment, reward, or reputation system. The outcome measured is the frequency of full cooperation (all-cooperator absorbing state) in repeated simulations. There is no direct measurement or discussion of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as a demonstration that network adaptation (partner switching) can promote cooperation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (partner choice) that can promote cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Jolly, E., & Chang, L. J. (2021). Gossip drives vicarious learning and facilitates social connection. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing private communication (gossip) in a repeated public goods game increases average group contributions and slows the decline of cooperation over rounds, compared to no-communication conditions. Gossip is especially important when direct observation of others' behavior is limited, as it enables vicarious learning and helps participants adjust their own contributions based on information received from others. Gossip also increases social bonding and alignment of impressions between conversation partners. The study does not include any formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication (gossip) in public goods games increases contribution rates and sustains cooperation, especially under incomplete information. However, it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that social information transmission (gossip) can substitute for formal sanctions in sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes or for games with punishment enabled. The design dimensions most directly addressed are player count, number of rounds, chat/communication, and information visibility. The absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, W. J., Li, X., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). Social exclusion with dynamic cost on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing reputation-dependent imitation and a strict reputation evaluation mechanism (low tolerance for defection) in a spatial public goods game leads to higher and more stable levels of cooperation (as measured by the frequency of cooperators). The effect is stronger when the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is higher. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings about reputation-dependent imitation and tolerance in reputation evaluation may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a punishment mechanism limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Messer, K. D., & Zarghamee, H. (2007). New hope for the voluntary contributions mechanism: The effects of context. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that context—specifically, the combination of cheap talk, voting, and a status quo of giving—can dramatically increase and sustain contributions and efficiency in a voluntary contributions mechanism (VCM) with a low MPCR. In the baseline (no context), efficiency starts at 82.3% and decays to 72.6% by round 10. In the full-context treatment, efficiency is 99.0% initially and 98.1% in the final round, with no significant decay. Cheap talk and status quo of giving each increase initial contributions, but only their interaction (and with voting) sustains high contributions. Voting alone has no effect. The results show that context can substitute for incentive-compatible mechanisms in achieving high efficiency, but the paper does not test or discuss punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly informative about the effects of communication, voting, and default framing, but not about punishment. For the downstream prediction task—predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency—this paper is not directly relevant. However, it does provide a strong benchmark for how non-punitive contextual factors can achieve high efficiency, which may be useful for comparative purposes or for understanding the limits of punishment's added value in similar settings.

- source: Schmidt, K. M., & Ockenfels, A. (2021). Focusing climate negotiations on a uniform common commitment can promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negotiation design is crucial for promoting cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. Uniform common commitments (UCC) result in group investments averaging 83% of the social optimum with enforcement, compared to 45% (individual commitments, IC) and 48% (complex common commitments, CCC). Even without enforcement, UCC achieves 52% of the optimum, while IC and CCC achieve only 34%. The UCC design is robust to asymmetries and to the absence of enforcement. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that negotiation design—specifically, requiring a uniform minimum contribution—can dramatically increase group efficiency in public goods games, even in the absence of enforcement. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding the impact of commitment and negotiation structure, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Bouma, J., & Ansink, E. (2013). The role of legitimacy perceptions in self-restricted resource use: A framed field experiment. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that label framing (contextualizing the game as protected area management) does not significantly reduce extractions (increase efficiency) on average. However, for participants who trust enforcement officers or feel their community can influence park management, framing leads to lower extractions (higher efficiency). The effect is not significant for the sample as a whole, but is significant for these subgroups. The explanatory power of legitimacy indicators is low, and the sample size is small. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms are present. However, it does show that contextual framing and legitimacy perceptions can moderate extraction (and thus efficiency) in PGG-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that institutional context and perceived legitimacy may interact with other design features to affect efficiency, but it does not inform the effect of adding punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in field or framed settings.

- source: Ren, T. Y., & Zheng, J. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics in the spatial public goods game with tolerance-based expulsion and cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a standard linear public goods game (VCM) with four players and 20 rounds per part, groups that start with unequal endowments (two rich, two poor) and then experience redistribution to equality do not increase their group contributions or efficiency after redistribution. Group contributions in the post-redistribution phase are indistinguishable from those in groups that remain unequal, and both are significantly lower than in always-equal groups. The experience of initial inequality creates a persistent negative spillover, locking groups into low cooperation and efficiency even after equality is restored. The mechanism is traced to the former poor mimicking the low-contribution behavior of the former rich after redistribution. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, simply reducing inequality via redistribution does not increase efficiency in public goods games if groups have previously experienced inequality. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, for games with similar design (no punishment, no reward, linear PGG, 4 players, 20+20 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, no identification, no summary of individual contributions), the efficiency in the treatment with redistribution will not exceed that of the control with persistent inequality, and both will be lower than in always-equal groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGGs without punishment or reward, but do not inform about the effect of punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Cooperation and competition between pair and multi-player social games in spatial populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary competition between 'alone' (pairwise) and 'together' (group) attitudes leads to complex phase behavior. At low public goods multiplication factors, pairwise interactions dominate; at intermediate values, both attitudes coexist but this can paradoxically lower average income; at high multiplication factors, group interactions dominate and cooperation can be sustained. The presence of the 'together' attitude at low multiplication factors can actually reduce average income, contrary to intuition. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding how the structure of interaction (pairwise vs. group) and the public goods multiplication factor affect efficiency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: van Soest, D., Stoop, J., & Vyrastekova, J. (2016). Toward a delineation of the circumstances in which cooperation can be sustained in environmental and resource problems. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game (VCM), average net group contributions are positive and significant, leading to substantial group payoffs and efficiency. In contrast, when the action space is extended to allow negative contributions (claims/taking from the public fund), average net group contributions are not significantly different from zero, and group payoffs are much lower. This holds even for subjects who initially display prosocial behavior. The ability to undo others' contributions destroys cooperation and efficiency, and the effect is not explained by framing or subject pool differences. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the action space—specifically, whether players can undo others' contributions—has a dramatic effect on efficiency in public goods games. In standard VCMs (with only non-negative contributions), efficiency is much higher than in Claim Games (where negative contributions are allowed). However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, establish a strong baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is high in standard VCMs and very low in Claim Games. For prediction tasks, this paper helps set expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency under different action space designs, but does not inform the effect of punishment.

- source: Meloni, S., Xia, C. Y., & Moreno, Y. (2017). Heterogeneous resource allocation can change social hierarchy in public goods games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when players in a networked PGG can allocate their contributions unevenly—favoring games where they previously earned higher payoffs—this leads to increased cooperation, a more heterogeneous distribution of investments, and a shift in the structure of wealth production. Specifically, small clusters of cooperators become highly productive, and the overall wealth distribution approaches the Pareto law (80% of wealth produced by 20% of nodes) for sufficiently strong payoff-driven allocation. The study also shows that the classical PGG (equal allocation) does not reproduce these features. The model is robust to different evolutionary update rules and network structures. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in PGGs or their impact on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in resource allocation affects efficiency and wealth distribution in PGGs, but it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task—estimating the efficiency impact of punishment given game design dimensions—this paper is not directly informative, as punishment is not present in the model.

- source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2011). Competition for Trophies Triggers Male Generosity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public goods game, the introduction of a unique and displayable reward (a 'trophy' mug) leads to significantly higher contributions, especially among males, compared to both a baseline (no reward) and a control reward (ice-cream) that is not unique or displayable. The effect is driven by male competitiveness for the trophy. Female behavior is less affected by the trophy. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes. The main outcomes are behavioral: contribution rates, approval points, and star rewards.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the effects of non-pecuniary, status-based rewards (trophies) on cooperation, especially among males, but does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment and lack of efficiency or payoff analysis means this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Isler, O., Maule, J., & Starmer, C. (2018). Is intuition really cooperative? Improved tests support the social heuristics hypothesis. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time-pressure (intuitive decision-making) increases contributions to the public good compared to forced-delay (reflective decision-making), especially under certain instructional conditions. The effect is robust to compliance and understanding confounds. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment condition or analyze payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on contribution rates under different cognitive conditions may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with or without punishment. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, no punishment, no reward, no chat) may help contextualize the baseline, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2017). Social learning and the demise of costly cooperation in humans. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that providing social information about other groups' behaviors or payoffs does not increase cooperation in repeated public goods games; in most cases, it leads to a significant decline in cooperation. The decline is strongest when individuals can observe the behavior and payoffs of the most successful individuals, supporting the idea of payoff-biased or prestige-biased social learning. There is no evidence for conformity bias (copying common behaviors) or group-prestige bias (copying successful groups). The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning and information about others' behavior/payoff affect cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as not providing evidence about punishment effects, but may be informative about baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment.

- source: Lamba, S. (2014). Social learning in cooperative dilemmas. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals do not show a clear tendency to conform to the modal contribution or to copy the highest earner's contribution in public goods games. There is significant individual and village-level variation in social learning strategies, and these are influenced by individual and contextual factors. Overall, contributions decreased in the second round, and there is no evidence that social learning increases cooperation. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present in the experimental design. The findings are relevant for understanding baseline behavior in the absence of punishment, especially regarding the role of social learning, but do not inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help characterize control conditions (no punishment, no reward, no communication), but does not provide treatment effect estimates or moderators for punishment.

- source: Horita, Y., Takezawa, M., Inukai, K., Kita, T., & Masuda, N. (2017). Reinforcement learning accounts for moody conditional cooperation behavior: experimental results. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that moody conditional cooperation (MCC) patterns are present in both repeated multiplayer PDG and PGG, though the specific form differs from previous literature. In the PGG, conditional cooperation is observed in both fixed and mixed group treatments. Reinforcement learning models (Bush-Mosteller and Roth-Erev) fit the observed behavioral data as well as or better than CC and MCC models, especially when direct reciprocity is absent. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics and the explanatory power of behavioral models in repeated PGGs with and without stable group composition. For the prediction task, this paper may inform expectations about baseline (control) cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment efficacy.

- source: Gong, X., & Sanfey, A. G. (2017). Social rank and social cooperation: Impact of social comparison processes on cooperative decision-making. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social comparison, operationalized as being assigned a high or low rank in a prior task, significantly affects subsequent contribution decisions in a standard public goods game. High rank leads to increased contributions, low rank to decreased contributions, compared to a baseline with no ranking. These effects are robust to whether the PGG group is the same as the ranking group, and are more pronounced in individuals with high social comparison orientation. The experiment does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are limited to how social comparison affects contribution behavior. While the game design (standard PGG) is relevant, the absence of punishment means it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior or the impact of social comparison, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Lotz, S. (2015). Spontaneous Giving under Structural Inequality: Intuition Promotes Cooperation in Asymmetric Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that priming participants to use intuition (vs. reflection) increases their contributions to the public good in a one-shot, four-player PGG with asymmetric MPCRs (structural inequality). The effect of intuition is robust to controls for age, gender, comprehension, and paragraph length. There is no significant main effect of MPCR (high vs. low) on cooperation, nor an interaction between intuition and MPCR. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant as a baseline for understanding cooperation rates in asymmetric, one-shot PGGs without punishment or reward. The findings may inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar asymmetric games, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on contribution rates (not payoffs) limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Zhang, L., Zhang, L. M., & Huang, C. W. (2022). Defectors in bad circumstances possessing higher reputation can promote cooperation. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputation rule—where reputation is based on matching one's strategy to one's neighbors—combined with heterogeneous investment allocation (parameterized by α), strongly promotes cooperation in spatial PGGs. Higher heterogeneity (larger α) lowers the critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the region of parameter space where full cooperation is stable. The results are robust to changes in network structure (square lattice, Moore neighborhood, scale-free network), total investment, noise, and initial cooperation proportion. However, the study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and heterogeneous investment can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Chen, Y. S., Yang, H. X., Guo, W. Z., & Liu, G. G. (2018). Promotion of cooperation based on swarm intelligence in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of swarm intelligence (where players update their strategies based on both their own best past strategy and the best current strategy in their neighborhood) into a continuous spatial public goods game leads to a strong promotion of cooperation. The level of cooperation depends on the multiplication factor (r) and the weight given to personal vs. neighborhood best strategies (c1). For low c1 (neighborhood-focused), cooperation can dominate at high r, but is vulnerable at low r. For high c1 (personal best-focused), cooperation is more stable even at low r, but the system is more diverse in strategies. The main reported outcome is the average cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how alternative strategy updating rules (swarm intelligence) affect cooperation rates, not payoffs or efficiency. It does, however, confirm that cooperation can be promoted by non-punitive mechanisms, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

- source: Stivala, A., Kashima, Y., & Kirley, M. (2016). Culture and cooperation in a spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that cooperation (measured as the fraction of conditional cooperators) only survives in multicultural states with high cultural diversity and very low or zero noise. In monocultural states or with any significant noise, cooperation dies out. The number of participants in public goods games is inversely related to cultural diversity. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it does provide contextual evidence that group structure, diversity, and noise can strongly affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding the role of cultural diversity and conditional cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, M., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2019). Dynamic scale return coefficient with environmental feedback promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of a dynamic scale return coefficient, governed by the difference between a group's average reputation and the population average, promotes the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. Higher reputation amplitude (the parameter controlling the impact of reputation differences) lowers the threshold for the enhancement factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the stable level of cooperation. The effect is robust across lattice and small-world network structures. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is focused on a different cooperation-promoting mechanism (reputation-based environmental feedback) and does not include or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Quan, J., Zhou, Y. W., Zhang, M., Tang, C. X., & Wang, X. J. (2019). The impact of heterogeneous scale return coefficient between groups on the emergence of cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient between groups (i.e., making the returns to investment nonlinear and group-specific) generally increases the average cooperation level compared to a homogeneous (linear) setting. The effect depends on the type of heterogeneity (uniform, exponential, power-law) and its amplitude. Uniform heterogeneity is most conducive to high and stable cooperation in most parameter ranges, but exponential heterogeneity can outperform in some cases. Stronger heterogeneity (higher amplitude) generally promotes higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency-related outcomes. Its findings are limited to how heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient affects average cooperation (contribution) levels in a spatial, continuous-contribution PGG. It is not relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be contextually useful for understanding how nonlinearities and heterogeneity in returns affect cooperation rates.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Pan, Q. H., Ju, X. X., & He, M. F. (2021). Public goods game with the interdependence of different cooperative strategies. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games on coevolving networks where agents disconnect from neighbors with similar payoff and connect to those with different payoff, the maintenance of cooperation depends on the balance between these two tendencies. Cooperation is best sustained when the rates of disconnection and connection are moderate and well-matched. Too much network time-dependence (frequent rewiring) generally undermines cooperation compared to the classic static network case. The emergence and stability of cooperation are associated with changes in network cohesion, but the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how network coevolution (rewiring based on payoff similarity/difference) affects cooperation rates, not efficiency. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Zhang, L., Xie, Y., Huang, C. W., Li, H. H., & Dai, Q. L. (2020). Heterogeneous investments induced by historical payoffs promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in investment, where individuals allocate more to groups that have yielded higher payoffs in the recent past, significantly increases the fraction of cooperators in spatial PGGs. Shorter memory (small M) and lower weight on earlier payoffs (small or negative omega) further enhance cooperation. Stronger heterogeneity (higher alpha) also promotes cooperation. These effects are robust to changes in network topology (square lattice vs. ER random networks). However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that certain forms of heterogeneity in investment can promote cooperation rates, but the translation to efficiency or payoff outcomes is not addressed. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only adjacent, not direct, evidence.

- source: Zhong, L. X., Xu, W. J., Shi, Y. D., & Qiu, T. (2013). Coupled dynamics of mobility and pattern formation in optional public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that both population density and agent mobility (free-floating probability) have non-monotonic effects on the frequency of cooperation in the OPGG. At low density and low mobility, cooperation is high; increasing either density or mobility generally reduces cooperation by making it easier for defectors to exploit cooperators. There is an optimal population density for cooperation, and the relationship between mobility and cooperation follows a power law. The results are explained in terms of agent collisions and cluster formation. However, the paper does not consider punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how population density and mobility affect cooperation rates in optional participation settings without sanctions. It should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Huang, C. W., Han, W. C., Li, H. H., Cheng, H. Y., Dai, Q. L., & Yang, J. Z. (2019). Public cooperation in two-layer networks with asymmetric interaction and learning environments. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the structure of the interaction and learning networks (specifically, their overlap) can significantly affect the steady-state level of cooperation in a spatial PGG. For low synergy factor r, higher overlap between interaction and learning networks increases the fraction of cooperators; for high r, lower overlap increases cooperation. The results are robust to changes in network topology and node degree. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how network structure (specifically, the overlap between interaction and learning networks) affects cooperation rates in PGGs without punishment. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Kim, J., Chae, H., Yook, S. H., & Kim, Y. (2015). Spatial evolutionary public goods game on complete graph and dense complex networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The SEPGG with voluntary participation exhibits distinct stationary states depending on the multiplication factor (r), group size (N), and network density (mean degree). On complete graphs (all-to-all), either defectors or loners dominate depending on r, with no stable cooperator-dominant state. On sparse networks, cooperation can dominate for sufficiently low mean degree. The paper provides scaling laws for the transitions between these states. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff; it focuses on the prevalence of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how group size and network structure affect cooperation and opting out in voluntary participation PGGs, but not for the effect of punishment. The results may be useful as a baseline for the behavior of voluntary participation PGGs without punishment, but cannot inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Yang, Y. Q., & Wang, T. (2015). Behavioral evolution in evacuation crowd based on heterogeneous rationality of small groups. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the degree of rationality (parameter alpha) in a subset of players leads to a higher fraction of cooperators in the population. When more players use rational allocation strategies, cooperation is promoted, and the phase transition points for cooperation occur at lower enhancement factors. The ratio of rational to conventional players also modulates cooperation: more rational players lead to more cooperation. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or direct efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in rationality and investment strategies can promote cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than payoffs limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Barr, A., Packard, T., & Serra, D. (2014). Participatory accountability and collective action: Experimental evidence from Albania. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is a robust positive correlation between cooperative behavior in a one-shot, binary public goods game and participation in both school-level and national accountability systems. At both the individual and district level, higher rates of cooperation in the game are associated with higher rates of participation in parent representative elections and higher voter turnout. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the public goods game, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or discuss punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are limited to the relationship between cooperative tendencies and real-world participation in collective action. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game environments.

- source: Nishi, A., Christakis, N. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Cooperation, decision time, and culture: Online experiments with American and Indian participants. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that American participants are more likely to cooperate than Indian participants in a repeated public goods game, both in the first round and in subsequent rounds, regardless of the social environment. Decision time for cooperation versus defection varies by culture: Americans decide faster when cooperating (except in non-cooperative environments), while Indians decide faster when defecting. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or efficiency outcomes are present. It does, however, provide contextual evidence about baseline cooperation rates in repeated public goods games with different cultural groups, which may be useful for understanding baseline control conditions in similar designs. However, it cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Lv, S. J. (2019). The roles of particle swarm intelligence in the prisoner's dilemma based on continuous and mixed strategy systems on scale-free networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The authors propose a method to predict the strategies of players whose current actions are hidden, based on the similarity of their past behavior to that of observed players. The method is tested on both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods games on a spatial lattice. The prediction method achieves high accuracy (success rate up to 0.9) in identifying hidden strategies, with performance depending on the length of historical data and game parameters (temptation to defect in PDG, enhancement factor in PGG). The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Its findings are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Dekel, S., Fischer, S., & Zultan, R. (2017). Potential Pareto Public Goods. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a linear public goods game with two majority players (who benefit from the public good) and one minority player (who is harmed by the public good), punishment institutions do not increase contributions or efficiency. Punishment is used ineffectively, with minority players sometimes punishing contributors, and majority players reluctant to punish free riders.
