As a result, efficiency is significantly lower in the punishment treatments compared to the baseline. Communication does not improve outcomes in the punishment condition. In contrast, when a reward institution is available and communication is allowed, majority players compensate the harmed minority, which increases majority contributions and efficiency. The presence of a harmed minority undermines the usual positive effects of punishment and communication on cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public goods games with a harmed minority, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and may even reduce it, relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of communication. The game design features—three players, 30 rounds, continuous contributions, known group composition, and a high punishment impact (3:1 ratio)—do not rescue the effectiveness of punishment in this context. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar settings (especially with harmed minorities), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency, even if control efficiency is moderate. The paper also shows that reward institutions, especially with communication, can increase efficiency by enabling compensation.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). When sanctions fail to increase cooperation in social dilemmas: Considering the presence of an alternative option to defect. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the authors find that introducing a sanctioning system (punishment for defection) increases collective outcomes (efficiency) in a standard two-option social dilemma (cooperate or defect). However, when a third option to defect (alternative defection, more harmful to the group) is available, sanctions do not increase efficiency and can even decrease it, particularly when participants have low expectations of others' cooperation. The negative effect is mediated by the failure of sanctions to increase trust or expectations of cooperation in the presence of the third option. Thus, the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is contingent on the decision structure and social expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is not uniform: it is positive in standard two-choice settings but can be null or negative when alternative, more harmful defection options are available and when group members have low expectations of cooperation. For prediction, this means that the presence of alternative defection options (i.e., more than two choices, with at least one being more harmful to the group) and low trust/expectations are critical moderators. If the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency and the game allows for alternative defection, enabling punishment may not improve and could even worsen efficiency, especially if group trust is low. In standard two-choice games, punishment is likely to increase efficiency.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2013). Empowering neighbors versus imposing regulations: An experimental analysis of pollution reduction schemes. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a non-point source pollution problem with heterogeneous agents, four treatments were compared: baseline (no intervention), group tax only, punishment only, and group tax plus punishment. The group tax mechanism (formal regulation) led to large and statistically significant increases in efficiency (from ~55% in baseline to ~87% with tax, and ~90% with tax plus punishment). Peer punishment alone increased efficiency only slightly and not significantly. When combined, punishment reduced variance and helped keep pollution below the target more often, but the efficiency gain was offset by the deadweight loss from punishment costs. Punishment was used less frequently and less intensely than in standard public goods games, limiting its effectiveness. The results suggest that formal regulatory mechanisms are more effective than informal peer punishment in increasing efficiency, but peer punishment can play a complementary role.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with a negative externality (pollution), enabling peer punishment alone yields only modest and inconsistent efficiency gains, while a formal group penalty (tax) mechanism yields large and robust efficiency improvements. Adding punishment to the group tax further reduces damages and increases the likelihood of meeting the target, but does not significantly increase efficiency due to the costliness of punishment. For prediction, the presence of a formal group penalty is the key driver of efficiency, and the marginal effect of enabling peer punishment is small or negligible unless punishment is much more frequent or severe than observed here. The game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, rounds, information) are well-documented, making this study highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar settings.

- source: Acemoglu, D., & Wolitzky, A. (2020). SUSTAINING COOPERATION: COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT VERSUS SPECIALIZED ENFORCEMENT. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal way to sustain cooperation in repeated public-goods-like games depends on the effectiveness of the punishment technology. If specialized enforcers can punish at low cost and high impact, then pure enforcer punishment (with immediate return to cooperation) maximizes efficiency. If specialized enforcement is less effective, then combining enforcer punishment with community enforcement (e.g., temporary withdrawal of cooperation or ostracism) is optimal. The paper provides explicit characterizations of the maximum sustainable cooperation (efficiency) in each regime, and shows that the presence of effective specialized enforcement can increase group welfare compared to pure community enforcement. However, if the punishment technology is very weak, community enforcement alone is nearly optimal. The results generalize to both small and large groups, and to settings with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of introducing punishment in public-goods-like games, conditional on the effectiveness of the punishment technology and other game design parameters. It shows that, when punishment is highly effective (i.e., the cost to punishers is low relative to the harm inflicted), enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency by supporting higher cooperation. However, if punishment is costly or ineffective, the efficiency gain is limited, and community enforcement may be nearly as good. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency as a function of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and discount factor. The results also highlight that the structure of punishment (one-time vs. repeated, enforcer vs. peer) and the possibility of ostracism or repentance are important moderators.

- source: Ozono, H., Jin, N., Watabe, M., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Solving the second-order free rider problem in a public goods game: An experiment using a leader support system. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when leaders are given the ability to punish followers using resources provided by follower support (support-present condition), both the frequency and effectiveness of punishment increase compared to a no-support condition. This leads to a higher probability of groups achieving high cooperation (as measured by contribution rates) and higher average profits for both leaders and followers, especially under 'linkage' punishment leaders who punish both non-cooperators and non-supporters. The no-support condition, where leaders have externally provided punishment resources, results in weaker punishment and lower group cooperation. The findings suggest that the structure of punishment funding and the type of leader punishment strategy are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling leaders to punish using resources provided by group members (rather than external resources) increases the efficiency of punishment in public goods games, as measured by group and individual profits and the frequency of high-cooperation groups. The effect is especially pronounced when leaders punish both first- and second-order free riders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader support system, the type of punishment strategy, and the source of punishment capital are key moderators. The control (no-support) condition provides a baseline for efficiency, and the support-present condition demonstrates a substantial positive shift in efficiency when punishment is enabled and properly incentivized.

- source: Tarui, N., Mason, C. F., Polasky, S., & Ellis, G. (2008). Cooperation in the commons with unobservable actions. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common property resource game with unobservable actions, a two-part symmetric group punishment strategy (harsh group punishment after a defection, then forgiveness and return to cooperation) can support full cooperation (first-best efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, provided certain conditions on the discount factor, resource growth rate, and group size are met. The analysis shows that the threat of the harshest possible group punishment (worst perfect equilibrium) is sufficient to deter defection, even when individual actions are not observable. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when this is possible and uses numerical simulations to illustrate how the sustainability of cooperation depends on group size and other parameters. Notably, the effect of group size is non-monotonic: increasing group size can initially make cooperation easier to sustain (by making punishment more credible), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect eventually dominates, making cooperation harder to sustain.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in public-goods-like environments with unobservable actions, the availability of credible, symmetric group punishment can enable full efficiency (relative to the first-best cooperative outcome), provided the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the parameter conditions (discount factor, resource growth, group size) are favorable. The analysis is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games where only aggregate outcomes are observable. The findings suggest that punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, but only if the punishment is credible and harsh enough to deter defection. The effect of group size is nuanced: increasing group size can initially make punishment more credible (and thus support efficiency), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect increases and efficiency becomes harder to sustain. The paper does not address other design dimensions such as chat, reward, or information feedback, so its predictive value is strongest for settings matching the model's assumptions (no chat, no reward, symmetric group punishment, unobservable individual actions, continuous contribution/harvest choices).

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2013). If Cooperation Is Likely Punish Mildly: Insights from Economic Experiments Based on the Snowdrift Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This study uses repeated group snowdrift games (a close variant of public goods games) with and without punishment to test the effects of mild versus severe punishment on cooperation and average payoff. In groups of five, with 25 rounds per session, the authors manipulate the cost-to-benefit ratio (r = 0.2 or 0.8) and the punishment fine (p = 2.0 or 4.0, with constant cost to punisher). When cooperation is likely (r = 0.2), both mild and severe punishment increase cooperation, but severe punishment does not further increase cooperation and reduces average payoff compared to mild punishment. When cooperation is unlikely (r = 0.8), only severe punishment substantially increases cooperation and average payoff; mild punishment is less effective and can even reduce average payoff compared to no punishment. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: mild punishment is optimal when cooperation is already likely, while severe punishment is only justified in highly defection-prone environments.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the underlying difficulty of cooperation (cost-to-benefit ratio, analogous to MPCR in PGGs). If the control game (no punishment) already achieves high efficiency, adding severe punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency; mild punishment is sufficient. If the control game has low efficiency (cooperation is rare), only severe punishment can increase efficiency. The cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the baseline cooperation rate, are critical moderators. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in snowdrift- or PGG-like games with peer punishment.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Shrestha, M. D. (2021). Evolving institutions for collective action by selective imitation and self-interested design. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of collective action with institutionalized punishment by leaders, both selective imitation and self-interested design (foresight) in leaders can lead to the emergence of effective institutions that increase cooperation, group production, and payoffs (efficiency). The presence of leaders who monitor and punish free-riders increases group effort and payoffs compared to groups without leaders or punishment. The effect is strongest when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high, punishment is not too costly, and leaders value future payoffs. The model also shows that the details of the updating mechanism (imitation vs. foresight) matter most when the benefit-to-cost ratio is intermediate and leaders heavily discount the future. In 'us vs. nature' games, increased monitoring and production lead to higher payoffs for all; in 'us vs. them' games, overproduction can reduce payoffs for subordinates. The model provides direct evidence that institutionalized punishment can increase efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling institutionalized punishment by leaders in public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and punishment is not too costly. The effect is robust to variations in group size, punishment cost, and the leader's share of rewards. For prediction tasks, the presence of leader-administered punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), with the magnitude depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio, punishment cost, and whether leaders value future payoffs. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is less positive or even negative if punishment is very costly or if leaders do not value future payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with institutionalized punishment, especially when the control efficiency is low due to free-riding.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Toriumi, F., & Sasaki, T. (2015). The Effect of Incentives and Meta-incentives on the Evolution of Cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game framework, cooperation and high efficiency (group payoff) can only be stably achieved if the incentive system includes second-order incentives for first-order rewards. Systems with only first-order punishment or reward, or with second-order incentives for punishment but not for reward, cannot maintain cooperation or efficiency in the long run due to the second-order free-rider problem. The cost-effect ratio of incentives (how much impact per unit cost) is a critical parameter: higher ratios make it easier for cooperation to be sustained. The model predicts that systems with second-order rewards for first-order incentives are more robust and can achieve high efficiency with fewer incentive-providers than systems with second-order punishments. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency but gives qualitative and parametric conditions for when efficiency is high or low under different incentive structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment and reward systems (including meta-incentives) on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient for high efficiency; the structure of incentives, especially the presence of second-order incentives for rewarding, is crucial. If a game design includes only first-order punishment, efficiency will not improve in the long run. If second-order incentives for first-order rewards are present and the cost-effect ratio is high, efficiency can be high. The model also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional on the broader incentive structure. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence or absence of second-order incentives, and the cost-effectiveness of punishment/reward, are key moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Wang, M., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). The role of alliance cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with probabilistic punishment of defectors, increasing the monitoring probability and punishment severity generally increases the proportion of cooperators and can help maintain higher resource levels (i.e., higher efficiency/welfare). However, if the initial resource level is too low or the resource's maximum growth rate is insufficient, the system will experience resource depletion (tragedy of the commons) regardless of punishment strength. The model demonstrates bistability: depending on initial conditions, the system may converge to full cooperation with resource depletion, or to a mixed/cooperative state with sustainable resources. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but only when ecological conditions allow for resource regeneration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource environments is conditional: punishment (higher probability and severity) can increase efficiency and resource sustainability, but only if the resource's regenerative capacity and initial abundance are sufficient. In settings where the resource cannot recover (low growth rate or low initial stock), punishment does not prevent collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by ecological parameters (resource growth, initial abundance) in addition to standard game design dimensions. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the efficiency outcome is directly modeled.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2013). The co-evolution of social institutions, demography, and large-scale human cooperation. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the co-evolution of institutions with sanctioning (punishment) and demography enables the stable emergence and maintenance of large-scale cooperation, resulting in a dramatic increase in group carrying capacity and efficiency. In the absence of institutionalized punishment, cooperation cannot be stably maintained, and group size/efficiency remains low. The model demonstrates that the optimal institution invests most, but not all, of the public good into technological improvement (increasing carrying capacity), while retaining enough for sanctioning to deter defectors. Sensitivity analyses show that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to changes in key parameters, though it is diminished by high migration rates or low benefits of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling institutionalized punishment in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency, especially in structured populations. The model directly links the presence and effectiveness of punishment to higher group payoffs and larger sustainable group sizes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing or strengthening punishment mechanisms should increase efficiency, provided the cost of punishment is not prohibitive and the benefit from cooperation is sufficient. The model also highlights the importance of population structure (limited migration) and the allocation of resources between sanctioning and productive investment. While the model is not an empirical experiment, its detailed mapping to public goods game structure and explicit focus on efficiency make it highly informative for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Nair, A., Blome, C., Choi, T. Y., & Lee, G. (2018). Re-visiting collaborative behavior in supply networks structural embeddedness and the influence of contextual changes and sanctions. *Journal of Purchasing and Supply Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated triadic supply network game with real managers, the introduction of sanctions (punishment) reduces both group allocation and average payoffs when the incentive structure favors cooperation (i.e., low temptation to defect). When the incentive structure is changed to increase the temptation to defect, sanctions increase group allocation compared to the no-sanction condition, but average payoffs remain lower than in the cooperative baseline. Sanctions are only effective at increasing group allocation when the underlying incentive structure is unfavorable to cooperation. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average payoffs are provided for each condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus negative in cooperative contexts and ambiguous (possibly less negative) in defect-prone contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like network games depends critically on the underlying incentive structure. When the baseline (control) efficiency is high (cooperative context), enabling punishment reduces efficiency. When the baseline efficiency is low (defection is tempting), enabling punishment can increase group allocation but does not fully restore efficiency/payoff to cooperative levels. For prediction, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is negative when control efficiency is high, and less negative or possibly weakly positive when control efficiency is low, but the overall efficiency with punishment rarely exceeds the cooperative baseline. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the payoff structure (temptation to defect), the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the information structure.

- source: Gao, L., Wang, Z., Pansini, R., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). Collective punishment is more effective than collective reward for promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective punishment is more effective than collective reward in promoting and stabilizing cooperation in multiplayer snowdrift games, especially for small groups or high initial cooperation. When the punishment-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold, a stable state of full cooperation emerges for both small and large groups, while such a state does not appear for large groups with only reward. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, which is closely related to group payoff but not a direct measure of efficiency. The results suggest that, in environments where individual-level discrimination is not possible, collective punishment can maintain high cooperation and thus higher group fitness or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in multiplayer public-goods-like environments (specifically, snowdrift games), enabling collective punishment (even when it cannot be targeted at individuals) can substantially increase the frequency and stability of cooperation, especially in small groups or when the punishment intensity is high. This implies that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of collective punishment should be expected to increase efficiency or group payoff, particularly in small groups. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation, so the findings should be interpreted as indicating a strong positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes via increased cooperation rates and stable cooperative equilibria.

- source: Lee, J. H., Iwasa, Y., Dieckmann, U., & Sigmund, K. (2019). Social evolution leads to persistent corruption. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This theoretical study shows that while institutional punishment can, in principle, sustain cooperation and high payoffs in public-goods-like settings, the possibility of corruption (i.e., punishment institutions accepting bribes) leads to cycles or stable states of reduced cooperation and lower average payoffs. The model demonstrates that transparency and monitoring are necessary to maintain honest punishment, but these are costly and thus not always sustained. As a result, the efficiency gains from punishment are not robust: corruption can cause efficiency to fall well below the cooperative optimum, and the system may oscillate between periods of high and low efficiency depending on the prevalence of corruption and monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: enabling punishment in a public-goods-like game does not guarantee increased efficiency if the punishment institution can be corrupted. The efficiency benefit of punishment depends critically on the integrity of the institution and the cost and prevalence of monitoring. In prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether corruption is possible and how transparency is maintained. If the game design allows for institutional corruption or lacks mechanisms for transparency and monitoring, enabling punishment may not improve—and may even reduce—efficiency relative to the control. The model also highlights the importance of considering dynamic and cyclical effects, not just static outcomes.

- source: Currie, T. E., Campenni, M., Flitton, A., Njagi, T., Ontiri, E., Perret, C., & Walker, L. (2021). The cultural evolution and ecology of institutions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that institutions, including punishment mechanisms, can alter the payoffs in collective action problems such as public goods games, making cooperation more stable and frequent. The agent-based model demonstrates that when agents can collectively set punishment levels, cooperation can emerge and be maintained, with higher payoffs for cooperators and a reduction in free-riders. The model is illustrative and not based on empirical data. Macro-scale analyses show that institutional complexity and inclusivity are associated with higher economic development, but these are not tested in controlled PGG-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment mechanisms in public goods games can increase cooperation and, in model terms, improve payoffs for cooperators. However, it does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in controlled experimental settings. The agent-based model suggests that the ability to set and adjust punishment can help overcome collective action problems, but the findings are illustrative and not directly transferable to quantitative prediction tasks. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing the role of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2016). The impact of personal responsibility on the (un)willingness to punish non-cooperation and reward cooperation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that people are more reluctant to punish non-cooperators when they feel personally responsible (i.e., when deciding individually), but this reluctance is attenuated when responsibility is shared in a group. This effect is specific to punishment and does not apply to rewarding cooperation. The effect is mediated by feelings of personal responsibility and entitlement, not by external accountability. The experiments use a common resource dilemma with sanctioning opportunities, but do not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs. Thus, the findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral moderators of punishment willingness, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish non-cooperation is higher when punishment decisions are made jointly by groups rather than by individuals, due to reduced personal responsibility and increased entitlement. However, since the study does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or common resource games. The results are useful for predicting when punishment will be more or less likely to be used, but not for predicting the efficiency consequences of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment decision (individual vs. group) may moderate the behavioral use of punishment, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2014). On the willingness to costly reward cooperation and punish non-cooperation: The moderating role of type of social dilemma. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that participants are more likely to reward cooperative behavior than to punish non-cooperative behavior, and that this tendency is stronger in public good dilemmas (where cooperation involves giving up private property) than in common resource dilemmas (where cooperation involves refraining from taking collective property). When given a choice, most participants prefer to reward rather than punish. The size of rewards is also larger than the size of punishments, especially in public good dilemmas. The study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that the preference for reward over punishment may have implications for collective welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good and common resource games. It shows that, when given a choice, people are more willing to reward than to punish, and that punishment is used less frequently and less intensely, especially in public good dilemmas. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the mere availability of punishment may not lead to high usage, especially if reward is also available, and that the type of social dilemma (public good vs. common resource) moderates willingness to punish. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in public good dilemmas if participants are reluctant to use it, particularly when reward is also an option.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2015). Punitive preferences, monetary incentives and tacit coordination in the punishment of defectors promote cooperation in humans. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when only one group member is needed to punish a defector (step-level public good), groups with punishment cost heterogeneity (one strong, low-cost punisher) achieve higher rates of punishment and lower rates of defection than symmetric groups. Tacit coordination on the strong punisher leads to more efficient punishment (i.e., punishment is more often carried out by only one group member). The presence of a penalty for defectors (i.e., punishment that also harms the defector) is necessary to reduce defection rates. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment cost heterogeneity can improve the effectiveness and efficiency of norm enforcement in such games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment cost heterogeneity (i.e., having a designated or focal punisher with lower costs) increases the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment in step-level public good games, as measured by behavioral outcomes (punishment and defection rates). While it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, the findings suggest that game designs enabling tacit coordination on a single, low-cost punisher are likely to yield higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, especially when a penalty for defectors is present. The results are most directly relevant for step-level or volunteer's dilemma variants of public goods games, rather than standard linear PGGs. The absence of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction, but the qualitative pattern is clear: punishment is more effective and less wasteful when cost heterogeneity allows for coordination on a single punisher.

- source: Delton, A. W., Nemirow, J., Robertson, T. E., Cimino, A., & Cosmides, L. (2013). Merely Opting Out of a Public Good Is Moralized: An Error Management Approach to Cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people who opt out of contributing to public goods are moralized and sometimes punished, even if they have not yet free ridden. This preventive moralization is stronger in public goods than in club goods, and is mediated by the perceived likelihood that the non-contributor will free ride in the future. The effect is robust across implicit, explicit, and behavioral measures, and is observed in both student and nationally representative adult samples. However, the studies do not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and moralization are triggered by non-contribution in public goods contexts, even before actual free riding occurs. It demonstrates that costly punishment is more likely in public goods than in club goods, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms can increase the likelihood of punishment being used in public goods games, especially when free riding is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence about how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to informing about the behavioral context in which punishment is likely to be applied.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., Vollan, B., & Nuppenau, E. A. (2015). Resource scarcity, and democratic elections in commons dilemmas: An experiment on forest use in Ethiopia. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that resource scarcity (lower initial stock) leads to less cooperative behavior (higher HR), and that the way sanctions are implemented matters: democratically elected sanctions increase cooperation (lower HR) both under abundance and scarcity, while imposed sanctions can reduce cooperation (higher HR) under abundance (crowding-out effect), but not under scarcity. The main outcome is behavioral (HR), not efficiency or group payoff. Theoretical efficiency levels are provided, but not empirically analyzed as outcomes. The findings suggest that participatory rule-making is more effective than top-down sanctions for sustaining cooperation, especially when resources are not extremely scarce.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how the method of sanction implementation (election vs. imposition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation in a common-pool resource game with real-world framing. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results must be mapped with caution to efficiency prediction tasks. Theoretical efficiency levels are given, but empirical results focus on HR. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, no explicit MPCR, probabilistic punishment, no rewards, summary info shown, anonymous punishment) are well described, supporting mapping to prediction models. The main implication is that democratically elected sanctions are more likely to increase cooperation (and thus efficiency) than imposed sanctions, especially under resource abundance. Under scarcity, the difference is smaller, but elections still outperform imposition.

- source: Seip, E. C., van Dijk, W. W., & Rotteveel, M. (2009). On Hotheads and Dirty Harries The Primacy of Anger in Altruistic Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that anger, rather than the perception of unfairness, is the primary driver of altruistic punishment in social dilemma games. Experimental evidence from a one-shot, three-person public goods game with punishment shows that lower contributions from others evoke more anger and lead to harsher punishment, and that the effect of others' contributions on punishment is fully mediated by experienced anger. Priming participants with anger increases their tendency to punish defectors. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. However, it does suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment are strongly influenced by emotional responses (especially anger) to defection or low contribution. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is likely to be used when players experience anger at norm violations, but the paper does not address whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is deployed, not its payoff consequences.

- source: Wu, J. H., Luan, S. H., & Raihani, N. (2022). Reward, punishment, and prosocial behavior: Recent developments and implications. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The review finds that both reward and punishment can promote prosocial behavior in public goods games and related social dilemmas, but their effectiveness depends on context. Punishment can sometimes reduce group welfare, especially when it leads to retaliation or is applied under noisy conditions, where it may mistakenly target cooperators and harm group payoffs. Reward is often found to be at least as effective as punishment in promoting cooperation and may be more efficient, particularly under noise. The legitimacy and source of punishment (e.g., third-party or democratic punishment) can moderate its effectiveness. Theoretical models suggest that reward is important for establishing prosociality, while punishment helps maintain it once established, but this dynamic has not been directly tested in experiments. The review also notes that indirect strategies like gossip may be more efficient than punishment in some real-world settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides useful qualitative guidance for predicting the effects of punishment and reward on prosocial behavior and, to a lesser extent, on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that punishment does not always increase efficiency and can sometimes reduce it, especially under noise or when perceived as illegitimate. Reward may be more efficient in some contexts, particularly when monitoring is imperfect. The review also highlights the importance of the structure and legitimacy of punishment mechanisms. However, because the review does not report quantitative efficiency outcomes or systematically analyze game design dimensions, its value for direct prediction of treatment efficiency is limited to qualitative boundary conditions and theoretical expectations.

- source: Tsvetkova, M., Wagner, C., & Mao, A. (2018). The emergence of inequality in social groups: Network structure and institutions affect the distribution of earnings in cooperation games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in networked cooperation games (including PGG-like games) increases inequality in the distribution of final wealth, as measured by the Gini coefficient. This is because the cost of punishment is disproportionately borne by poorer players, who become even poorer as a result. However, the paper does not find a systematic relationship between the presence of punishment and group average wealth (i.e., efficiency or total payoff). Thus, while punishment increases cooperation and inequality, it does not reliably increase or decrease group efficiency. The main focus is on the distributional consequences (inequality), not on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in PGG-like networked cooperation games increases inequality in final wealth, but does not provide evidence that punishment systematically increases or decreases group efficiency (total payoff relative to full cooperation). For the downstream prediction task, this means that the presence of punishment should not be assumed to improve or worsen efficiency based on this evidence alone. The main effect is on the distribution of payoffs, not their sum. If the prediction task is about efficiency, this paper suggests that punishment's effect is likely neutral or context-dependent, and that other design dimensions (e.g., network structure, fluidity) may matter more for efficiency than for inequality.

- source: Redhead, D., Dhaliwal, N., & Cheng, J. T. (2021). Taking charge and stepping in: Individuals who punish are rewarded with prestige and dominance. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that high-status individuals (prestige or dominance) are more likely to engage in costly punishment in collective action settings, such as public goods games, because their status lowers the net cost and risk of punishment. Punishment can further increase their prestige or dominance through reputational rewards. The review highlights that punishment is not always altruistic and may be motivated by self-interest, especially when reputational gains are possible. The paper also notes that the net payoff of punishment depends on the balance of reputational gains and costs (including risk of retaliation), and that these dynamics are context-dependent. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual background on the social and reputational mechanisms that may moderate the effects of punishment in public goods and collective action games. It suggests that the effectiveness and cost-benefit of punishment may depend on the status of the punisher, the risk of retaliation, and the cultural or institutional context. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions. Thus, while it is useful for understanding potential moderators and mechanisms, it offers limited direct support for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., Vogel, M., & Singer, T. (2017). Caring Cooperators and Powerful Punishers: Differential Effects of Induced Care and Power Motivation on Different Types of Economic Decision Making. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Inducing a Care motive increases a latent cooperation factor (which includes public goods contributions), while Power induction increases a punishment factor (punishment in 2nd/3rd party punishment games, ultimatum, impunity). The two factors are largely orthogonal. Care increases cooperation relative to control, but does not increase punishment. Power increases punishment relative to Care, but not relative to control. No direct efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and cooperation are distinct behavioral tendencies, and that motivational context (Care vs. Power) can differentially affect them. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does suggest that punishment behavior can be increased by Power motives, but this is not linked to group payoff or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by clarifying that punishment and cooperation are separable and can be independently manipulated.

- source: Bracht, J., Figuières, C., & Ratto, M. (2008). Relative performance of two simple incentive mechanisms in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper experimentally compares two incentive mechanisms for public goods provision: the Falkinger mechanism (which rewards/penalizes deviations from average contributions) and the compensation mechanism (where players can subsidize each other's contributions). Both mechanisms increase contributions and group efficiency compared to the voluntary contribution control. The Falkinger mechanism, particularly with higher tax-subsidy rates (closer to the efficient value), leads to significantly higher and more stable efficiency gains than the compensation mechanism. The compensation mechanism initially boosts contributions, but this effect decays over time, and average efficiency remains lower than under the Falkinger mechanism. The Falkinger mechanism's performance is robust to parameter changes and predicts average contributions well, while the compensation mechanism suffers from suboptimal subsidy rates and coordination issues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment-and-reward mechanism (Falkinger) in a two-player, repeated, continuous-contribution public goods game with full information and no communication substantially increases efficiency relative to the control. The effect is stronger and more reliable when the punishment/reward parameter is set closer to the efficient value. The compensation (reward-only) mechanism also increases efficiency, but less so, and the effect is less stable. For prediction, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment/reward mechanisms on efficiency, especially when the mechanism is well-calibrated. The results are most directly applicable to small, fixed pairs, continuous contribution, no chat, and full feedback settings.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2018). Managing and defending the commons: Experimental evidence from TURFs in Chile. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with insiders (who can communicate) and outsiders (who cannot), the introduction of poaching without monitoring or sanctions dramatically reduces insiders' earnings and resource sustainability, nearly eliminating the efficiency gains from cooperation. Adding weak external enforcement (low-probability monitoring and fines) or allowing insiders to invest in monitoring (with the same fine) both significantly reduce poaching and increase insiders' earnings and harvests, but do not fully restore efficiency to baseline (no-poaching) levels. Combining external and local monitoring does not further reduce poaching or increase efficiency. The main constraint is insiders' inability to coordinate sufficient monitoring investment. The results are robust across field (TURF members) and student samples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with communication among insiders, the introduction of punishment (via monitoring and fines for poaching) increases group efficiency relative to a poaching-only control, but the effect is partial and depends on the ability to coordinate monitoring. The effect is similar whether monitoring is provided externally or by insiders, and combining both does not yield additional efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment (even if weak) should be expected to increase efficiency above the poaching-only baseline, but not to the level of the no-poaching or full-cooperation baseline. The magnitude of the effect is limited by the monitoring probability and the coordination of monitoring investment.

- source: Griffin, C., & Belmonte, A. (2017). Cyclic public goods games: Compensated coexistence among mutual cheaters stabilized by optimized penalty taxation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a cyclic public goods game with three species, the system without penalty taxation is unstable and leads to the dominance of a single species, with the interior coexistence point being a point of minimal total population growth (i.e., lowest efficiency). Imposing a penalty tax (punishment) on population growth can stabilize the interior fixed point and allow for coexistence of all species, but this tax is not redistributed and thus reduces overall efficiency further. The optimal tax is the minimal one that achieves stability, but any positive tax reduces total population growth below the already minimal level at the coexistence point. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include empirical or simulation data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a cyclic public goods game with three types of mutual cheaters, introducing a penalty tax (punishment) can stabilize coexistence but always reduces total population growth (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment case. The penalty is not redistributed, so it is a pure cost. Thus, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (as a non-redistributive tax) is predicted to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most relevant for settings with cyclic/multispecies structure and non-redistributive punishment, and less so for standard two-strategy PGGs or for punishment that is redistributed. The results are directly informative for predicting the direction of efficiency change (less positive) when enabling punishment in such models.

- source: Kosfeld, M. (2020). The role of leaders in inducing and maintaining cooperation: The CC strategy. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Drawing on a range of lab and field experiments, the paper finds that: (1) Groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators achieve better efficiency and group outcomes; (2) Punishment of non-cooperation (especially when administered by leaders with prosocial motives) increases group efficiency and cooperation, as shown in field data from Ethiopian forest user groups and lab public goods games; (3) Antisocial punishment by leaders is associated with significantly worse group outcomes; (4) Trust by leaders (as opposed to control) can increase efficiency in principal-agent settings; (5) Self-selection mechanisms can help attract motivated types, further improving group efficiency. The paper provides strong evidence that punishment, when properly targeted and motivated, is a key mechanism for sustaining high efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games will increase group efficiency, especially when punishment is prosocially motivated and not antisocial. The evidence is drawn from both lab and field settings, including real-world resource management and controlled experiments. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the motives of the punisher (leader or group member), the presence of motivated types, and the structure of the group. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, particularly when the punishment technology is effective and antisocial punishment is minimized. The paper also highlights the importance of leader behavior and group composition, which may interact with the game design dimensions.

- source: Bhui, R., Chudek, M., & Henrich, J. (2019). How exploitation launched human cooperation. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative indirect reciprocity (NIR)—whereby individuals are allowed to exploit those with bad reputations—can suppress exploitation and support the emergence and maintenance of cooperation and public goods provision. The model shows that cooperation is most stable when exploitation is inefficient (harm to victim > gain to exploiter), and that as the frequency of opportunities for reputation improvement increases, the minimum benefit required for stable cooperation also increases. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable, and shows that NIR can serve as a foundation for more complex forms of cooperation. The overall effect of NIR is to increase group efficiency and welfare by reducing exploitation and supporting norm adherence, especially in early or cognitively simple societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, tolerated exploitation of ill-reputed individuals) can increase group efficiency and public goods provision, especially when exploitation is inefficient and when opportunities for norm adherence are not too frequent. The model gives explicit payoff-based conditions for when cooperation is stable, which can inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when the cost to the victim of exploitation is high relative to the benefit to the exploiter, and when errors in reputation assignment are low. The model also highlights that as norm adherence becomes more mandatory and frequent, the requirements for stable cooperation become stricter, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency in some parameter regimes.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2017). When is bigger better? The effects of group size on the evolution of helping behaviours. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that as group size increases, the marginal benefits of helping, the effectiveness of reciprocity, and relatedness all tend to decrease, making cooperation less likely to evolve or be stable in large groups. Punishment can stabilize cooperation, but its effectiveness also declines with group size unless institutional mechanisms are in place. The authors argue that human societies have overcome these limitations through the evolution of institutions that coordinate sanctioning and information sharing, thereby enabling large-scale cooperation. The paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding how game design features (such as group size, punishment, and information structure) interact to affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative predictions or new data on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and institutional punishment on cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that, all else equal, increasing group size will tend to reduce the effectiveness of both reciprocity and punishment in sustaining cooperation, unless institutional mechanisms are present to coordinate sanctioning and information. The paper highlights the importance of considering diminishing marginal returns, monitoring costs, and second-order free-riding in large groups. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in larger groups unless institutional features (e.g., centralized punishment, coordinated monitoring) are present. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates or experimental data for efficiency outcomes, so its support is primarily conceptual and mechanistic.

- source: Bonroy, O., Garapin, A., Hamilton, S. F., & Monteiro, D. M. S. (2019). FREE-RIDING ON PRODUCT QUALITY IN COOPERATIVES: LESSONS FROM AN EXPERIMENT. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the probability of exclusion (punishment) significantly reduces free-riding (shirking) in larger groups, and that increasing the base payoff (independent of collective returns) also reduces free-riding. In small groups, free-riding is low regardless of punishment. The main outcome is the frequency of free-riding, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment (exclusion) and higher base payoffs can facilitate cooperation, but the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment (in the form of exclusion) reduces free-riding in public-goods-like environments, especially in larger groups. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (frequency of free-riding) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be mapped to efficiency outcomes indirectly. The experimental design and manipulation of group size, punishment probability, and base payoff are informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data is a limitation. The results support the prediction that enabling punishment will increase efficiency in larger groups, especially when the probability of punishment is substantial.

- source: Zhou, K., & Ren, T. Y. (2021). Low-carbon technology collaborative innovation in industrial cluster with social exclusion: An evolutionary game theory perspective. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a social exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a cost to expel defectors from the public goods game) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a networked public goods environment. The effect is stronger when the cost of exclusion is low and the probability of successful exclusion is high. Sparse network structures (low average degree) are more conducive to sustaining cooperation. Voluntary participation (allowing loners) also helps prevent defection from dominating. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, social exclusion) can increase cooperation rates in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and the network is sparse. However, because the model and results focus on behavioral outcomes (cooperation level) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The model's structure and parameter explorations may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more likely to be effective, but do not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes for the prediction task.

- source: Song, Q., Cao, Z. H., Tao, R., Jiang, W., Liu, C., & Liu, J. Z. (2020). Conditional neutral punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that language and symbolic communication fundamentally alter the possibilities for collective action by simplifying coordination, reducing the costs of monitoring and punishing free riders, and amplifying reputation and signaling effects. It reviews theoretical models and empirical studies (from both ethnography and experiments) showing that communication (including chat, gossip, and feedback) increases cooperation rates and the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemmas. However, it does not report direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling communication (chat, language, gossip) in public goods games and collective action settings increases cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment, which should, in principle, improve group outcomes and efficiency. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of communication and norm establishment as moderators of punishment effectiveness.

- source: Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2017). Helping in humans and other animals: a fruitful interdisciplinary dialogue. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that mechanisms such as punishment and reward, studied extensively in human public goods games, are also relevant in non-human animal cooperation. It reviews how laboratory studies (often using public goods or n-player social dilemma games) have inspired comparative research, and how features like punishment, partner choice, and communication can affect cooperation rates. The authors emphasize the need for more ecologically valid experiments and for understanding how laboratory findings translate to real-world helping and cooperation. The review notes that while punishment and reward can increase cooperation in public goods games, most evidence is about behavioral outcomes, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for how punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, but does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators that may influence the effect of punishment on cooperation, but cannot be used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of ecological validity, the role of communication, and the need to consider real-world payoff structures.

- source: Vollan, B., Prediger, S., & Frölich, M. (2013). Co-managing common-pool resources: Do formal rules have to be adapted to traditional ecological norms?. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This field-lab experiment with communal farmers in southern Africa shows that the efficiency of formal, democratically chosen rules for managing a common-pool resource depends on their alignment with local ecological norms. The quota rule (limiting extraction) increases group earnings and resource availability in both high and low sustainability groups, but is unpopular. The rotation rule only increases efficiency in low sustainability groups (who previously failed to coordinate), but is ineffective or even counterproductive in high sustainability groups (who already tacitly coordinated for sustainability), due to high rates of rule breaking. The lottery rule is ineffective in both types of groups. Rule compliance is lower when rules conflict with local norms, and rule breaking is not mainly motivated by profit. Thus, the effect of rule-based interventions on efficiency is highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of introducing a formal rule (akin to a punishment or restriction mechanism) on efficiency in a common-pool resource game, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect depends on the compatibility of the rule with pre-existing group norms. Rules that align with local sustainability norms (e.g., quotas in groups that value sustainability) can increase efficiency, while rules that conflict with these norms (e.g., rotation in high-norm groups) may reduce efficiency due to non-compliance. The presence of a sanctioning mechanism (imperfect monitoring and penalty) is not sufficient to ensure compliance or efficiency gains. Thus, the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency is moderated by group-level norms and prior behavior, not just by the formal game design. This paper is highly informative for settings where the main intervention is a formal rule or restriction, but less so for classic peer-punishment PGGs.

- source: Pal, S., & Hilbe, C. (2022). Reputation effects drive the joint evolution of cooperation and social rewarding. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputation effects are crucial for the evolution of cooperation and social rewarding in PGG-like environments. When individuals can observe or infer others' rewarding strategies (i.e., reputation is possible), social rewarding and cooperation co-evolve and are stable, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. Without reputation, cooperation and rewarding do not emerge, and antisocial rewarding may arise under certain conditions (e.g., high assortment or mutually beneficial rewards). In multiplayer (PGG) settings, similar results hold: reputation enables stable cooperation and social rewarding, while its absence leads to defection and low efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation and rewarding are payoff-maximizing, and shows that the presence of reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling reputation effects (e.g., making rewarding behavior observable) in PGG-like games will increase efficiency when social rewarding is possible. The model directly links game design dimensions such as group size, marginal per-capita return, reward cost and effectiveness, and information structure (reputation) to the emergence of efficient cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of observable rewarding (reputation), sufficiently high reward effectiveness relative to cost, and the ability to target rewards to cooperators are critical for efficiency gains from reward mechanisms. The absence of reputation or the presence of antisocial rewarding can undermine efficiency. While the paper does not model punishment directly, it provides indirect evidence that the structure and observability of incentives (including punishment, by analogy) are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Gross, J., & Böhm, R. (2020). Voluntary restrictions on self-reliance increase cooperation and mitigate wealth inequality. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can solve a collective action problem either individually (self-reliance) or collectively (public good), the availability of self-reliance crowds out cooperation and increases inequality, especially when self-reliance is relatively cheap. However, when groups are given the opportunity to vote to abolish the individual solution, they overwhelmingly do so under medium and high interdependence, which leads to higher rates of public goods provision, lower resource waste, higher group earnings, and reduced inequality. The effect is particularly strong when there is a risk of miscoordination (medium interdependence) or when group members differ in their ability to be self-reliant (wealth heterogeneity). The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the standard sense, but self-reliance by richer group members can function as a coercive device, pressuring poorer members to cooperate.
