  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional design features that restrict individual opt-out/self-reliance options can substantially increase efficiency and group earnings in public-goods-like environments, especially when the efficiency gains from cooperation are significant (i.e., when self-reliance is costly relative to the public good). While the study does not manipulate punishment, it shows that endogenous institutional restrictions (analogous to mandatory participation or exclusion of exit options) can have large positive effects on efficiency, similar to or greater than those observed for punishment in standard PGGs. For prediction, the presence or absence of self-reliance/exit options, and the ability to restrict them, should be considered as key moderators of efficiency, alongside standard game design dimensions.

- source: Nyborg, K. (2018). Reciprocal climate negotiators. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that reciprocal preferences can, under certain conditions (strong and widespread reciprocity), stabilize the grand coalition in a public-goods-like coalition game, leading to high group welfare (efficiency). If only a minority of countries are reciprocal, or if reciprocity is weak, the only stable coalitions are small and provide little welfare improvement over the non-cooperative baseline. The model demonstrates that the presence of reciprocal preferences can shift the game from a pure Prisoner's Dilemma to a coordination game in utilities, but this does not guarantee high efficiency unless reciprocity is strong and common. The paper does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning institutions, but reciprocity can act as a form of endogenous social sanctioning. The welfare/efficiency effects of reciprocity are thus mixed: potentially large if conditions are right, but often minimal in realistic parameter ranges.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that social preferences (reciprocity) can, in principle, increase efficiency in public-goods-like coalition games, but only if they are strong and widespread. In the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms, reciprocity alone is not a reliable path to high efficiency unless the population is highly reciprocal. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling social preference mechanisms (like reciprocity) may only improve efficiency if the relevant parameters (strength and prevalence of reciprocity) are high. The model does not address explicit punishment, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled games is limited, but it does show that endogenous social preferences can sometimes substitute for formal sanctions in supporting cooperation and efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented via social preferences rather than institutionalized mechanisms.

- source: Schonmann, R. H., & Boyd, R. (2016). A simple rule for the evolution of contingent cooperation in large groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that contingent cooperation in large groups can invade and persist if relatedness is above a threshold that depends sensitively on the threshold fraction of cooperators required for cooperation to be sustained. The required relatedness increases rapidly as the threshold for cooperation increases. The number of repeated interactions (iterations) required for cooperation to invade also increases rapidly with the threshold and decreases with relatedness. The model shows that, under realistic levels of relatedness, only strategies that require cooperation from a small fraction of the group can invade; strategies that require near-universal cooperation are unlikely to evolve. The paper argues that contingent punishment (not formally modeled here) is a more plausible mechanism for the evolution of widespread cooperation, because even a small fraction of punishers can induce high levels of cooperation. The results are supported by mathematical derivations and numerical simulations of evolutionary dynamics in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, relatedness, and the threshold for contingent cooperation affect the evolutionary success (and thus efficiency) of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It does not directly model or analyze punishment, but its results suggest that punishment-based mechanisms are more likely to support high efficiency in large groups than pure contingent cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper's formulas can be used to estimate the conditions under which cooperation (and thus high efficiency) can be sustained, given the group structure and behavioral rules. However, because the paper does not model explicit punishment or compare efficiency with and without punishment, it provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Heintzelman, M. D., Salant, S. W., & Schott, S. (2009). Putting free-riding to work: A Partnership Solution to the common-property problem. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals form partnerships that share revenue equally, the resulting free-riding reduces excessive effort in common-property problems, potentially achieving the social optimum in aggregate payoff. The number of partnerships is a key design parameter: too many partnerships (everyone solo) leads to excessive effort, while too few (everyone in one group) leads to under-provision. An intermediate, endogenously determined number of partnerships can induce the efficient outcome. The model shows that this solution is stable under certain conditions (e.g., when solo production is sufficiently disadvantageous). The Partnership Solution can outperform standard regulatory interventions in terms of participant welfare. The paper generalizes the result to various sharing rules and to cases with heterogeneous agents.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional design—specifically, the structure of group formation and revenue sharing—can have large, predictable effects on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not study punishment per se, it shows that endogenous free-riding within partnerships can substitute for external sanctions in curbing over-extraction and maximizing welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that efficiency can be increased by mechanisms that induce free-riding when the baseline is excessive effort, and that the number and size of groups (player_count, partnership structure) are critical moderators. However, the absence of explicit punishment means the results are most directly relevant to settings where group structure, not punishment, is the main intervention.

- source: Richter, A., & Grasman, J. (2013). The transmission of sustainable harvesting norms when agents are conditionally cooperative. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of conditional cooperators in a common-pool resource setting can lead to complex and sometimes abrupt transitions between cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria. Efficiency, measured as resource stock and group profit relative to the maximum economic yield, is highly sensitive to social dynamics. When temptation to defect increases or when group size increases, the system can shift from high-efficiency (cooperative) to low-efficiency (defective) states, sometimes with hysteresis or limit cycles. The model highlights that social norm enforcement via moral persuasion can sustain cooperation, but is vulnerable to collapse if conditional cooperators lose motivation. The absence of explicit punishment means that norm enforcement is 'soft' and collapses are possible even without changes in external parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like common-pool resource games, the efficiency of group outcomes is highly sensitive to the social composition (intrinsic vs. conditional cooperators vs. defectors) and to the strength of norm enforcement mechanisms. While it does not model explicit punishment, it suggests that in the absence of strong enforcement (e.g., costly punishment), efficiency is fragile and can collapse due to endogenous social dynamics. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that enabling or strengthening norm enforcement (potentially via punishment) could stabilize high-efficiency outcomes, especially in larger groups or when temptation to defect is high. However, the model also warns that even with some enforcement, efficiency can be lost if social dynamics shift. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is absent or weak, and thus provide a baseline for understanding the potential value of adding punishment.

- source: Richter, A., van Soest, D., & Grasman, J. (2013). Contagious cooperation, temptation, and ecosystem collapse. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a closed community harvesting a renewable resource, cooperation can be sustained through social norm contagion, but is vulnerable to collapse if the temptation to defect becomes too strong (e.g., due to increased population or technology). The system can flip from a high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibrium to a low-efficiency (defective) equilibrium, with substantial welfare loss. Hysteresis is present: reversing the parameter change that caused collapse is not sufficient to restore cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for welfare under cooperation and Nash equilibrium, showing large efficiency losses after collapse. The presence of social norm diffusion (but not explicit punishment) can sustain cooperation up to a tipping point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the efficiency dynamics of common-pool resource games with social norm diffusion, but it does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is maintained by social pressure, persuasion, or contagion, rather than by costly punishment. The model shows that efficiency can be high and robust up to a tipping point, after which collapse is sudden and efficiency is lost. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence about the role of population size, technology, and social norm strength in determining efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of enabling or disabling punishment. It is best used as a theoretical baseline for environments without explicit punishment.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taylor, P. D. (2010). Joint evolution of multiple social traits: a kin selection analysis. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when social traits such as public good production and exploitation co-evolve, their interactions can lead to complex evolutionary dynamics, including bistability between efficient (social) and inefficient (asocial) outcomes. The efficiency of the group (measured as total payoff or welfare) depends on the joint equilibrium of these traits, which is sensitive to relatedness, the presence of independent public good production, and the nature of trait interactions (synergy or interference). In some cases, higher relatedness increases both cooperation and exploitation, with ambiguous effects on efficiency. The model highlights that multidimensional social dilemmas can have multiple stable outcomes, and small changes in parameters or initial conditions can lead to large differences in group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the design dimensions of a public goods game—especially those affecting trait interactions, relatedness, and the possibility of multiple social traits—can lead to non-linear and sometimes counterintuitive effects on group efficiency when punishment-like or enforcement traits are present. While the model does not directly analyze punishment in the standard PGG sense, it shows that enforcement or exploitation traits can interact with cooperation to produce bistability or runaway dynamics. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment or enforcement mechanisms may not always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the interaction structure, initial conditions, and whether the system is in a region of parameter space that supports social or asocial equilibria. The findings caution against simple extrapolation from one-dimensional models and highlight the importance of considering multidimensional trait interactions.

- source: Hashim, M. J., Kannan, K. N., Maximiano, S., & Ulmer, J. R. (2014). Digital Piracy, Teens, and the Source of Advice: An Experimental Study. *Journal of Management Information Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Advice from parents, especially when they are punished for their child's piracy, increases purchasing and group profit and reduces piracy compared to other advice sources. Advice from record labels is the least effective and can even reduce group profit. Advice from a regulator is largely ignored. The effect of advice is strongest when the adviser has both a social tie and a stake in the outcome. The study does not include standard peer punishment or reward mechanisms; the only 'punishment' is an exogenous penalty to parents in one treatment. The game is a close variant of a public goods game, but not a standard PGG. Efficiency-related outcomes (group profit, purchasing) are reported, but not as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that exogenous punishment of a third party (parents) for a player's free-riding can increase public good provision and group profit, especially when combined with a social tie. However, it does not test endogenous peer punishment or standard PGG punishment institutions. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms may be more effective when they are socially salient and affect someone with a close tie to the player, but direct transfer to standard PGG with peer punishment is limited. Control efficiency (group profit without punishment) is reported, and the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (parent penalty) can be inferred, but the mechanism differs from standard punishment. The absence of peer punishment, reward, or communication means the results are only adjacent for direct prediction of PGG punishment effects.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2015). Institutional Sources of Legitimate Authority: An Experimental Investigation. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that citizens are more willing to help salaried (fixed-compensation) authorities than those who personally benefit from collected penalties, but only when transparency is high. When transparency is low, support for salaried authorities collapses, while support for appropriator authorities is low regardless of transparency. Contribution rates and average earnings are reported, but do not differ significantly between treatments under full information; under limited information, contribution rates are lower for salaried authorities. The main outcome is a behavioral measure of legitimacy (citizen intervention), not efficiency or group payoff. The deterrence index (successful punishment of noncontributors minus punishment of contributors) is associated with higher contributions, but the causal effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly estimated.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how institutional features (transparency, authority incentives) affect citizen support for enforcement and, through deterrence, contribution rates. However, it does not directly estimate the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The design is a binary (all-or-nothing) public goods game with centralized, probabilistic punishment and a unique intervention stage. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of punishment (as measured by citizen support) is sensitive to transparency and authority incentives, which may in turn affect deterrence and contributions, but the direct impact on efficiency is not quantified. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative about mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct treatment effect estimates for efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). Benefits of asynchronous exclusion for the evolution of cooperation in stochastic evolutionary optional public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asynchronous exclusion (where excluders act sequentially and avoid redundant costs) is more effective at promoting cooperation than synchronous exclusion (where all excluders act simultaneously and pay redundant costs). This effect is strongest when the probability of exclusion success is high and the public goods multiplier is low (well-mixed) or intermediate (structured). The main reported outcomes are the probability of the system reaching a cooperative state and the frequency of different strategies at equilibrium. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effectiveness of exclusion (a sanctioning mechanism) in promoting cooperation in optional public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the increased probability of reaching cooperative states under asynchronous exclusion suggests that, under some parameter regimes, more effective or less costly sanctioning can increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not measured, and because exclusion differs from standard punishment (it removes defectors from benefit sharing rather than imposing a direct cost), the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs. The results are most relevant for settings where exclusion is the main sanction and where the cost structure of sanctioning can be varied.

- source: Lokhorst, A. M., van Dijk, E., & Staats, H. (2009). Public commitment making as a structural solution in social dilemmas. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that both dispositional trust and situational expectations interact to predict willingness to invest in a public commitment system in a public good dilemma. High trusters are more willing to install a commitment system when situational expectations are low, while low trusters are more willing when situational expectations are high. The motive to maximize collective outcomes partially mediates this effect. However, the study does not measure actual contributions, group payoff, or efficiency, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of commitment systems (or punishment) on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding when participants are likely to support or invest in structural solutions (like public commitment systems), but does not report on actual efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be useful for understanding the adoption of commitment or sanctioning systems, but not for predicting their efficiency effects.

- source: Mulder, L. B., & Nelissen, R. M. A. (2010). When Rules Really Make a Difference: The Effect of Cooperation Rules and Self-Sacrificing Leadership on Moral Norms in Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that installing a cooperation rule increases personal moral norms and cooperative behavior (less resource extraction, shorter breaks, fewer lottery tickets taken) only when the rule is installed by a self-sacrificing leader. If the leader is self-benefiting, rules only increase cooperation when behavior is monitored (i.e., when there is supervision). The effect of rules on cooperation is mediated by the instigation of moral norms, but only for self-sacrificing leaders. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are limited to behavioral and attitudinal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of rules (and by extension, possibly punishment) in increasing cooperation depends on the perceived legitimacy and self-sacrifice of the authority figure, and on the presence of monitoring. However, because the studies do not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The results suggest that rules (and possibly punishment) may be more effective in increasing cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) when installed by a self-sacrificing leader or when monitoring is present, but this is not tested in terms of payoff or efficiency. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence of rules, monitoring (supervision), and leader characteristics, but not punishment per se.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2016). How the expanded crowd-funding mechanism of some southern rural areas in China affects cooperative behaviors in threshold public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both publishing the list of cooperators and the presence of lobbyists can significantly increase the number of cooperators in a threshold public goods game, especially when the synergy factor is not too high. The effectiveness of these mechanisms depends on the population structure (proportion of unconditional cooperators vs. defectors), the synergy factor, and emotional influence parameters. When the synergy factor is high, publishing the list is always beneficial and the role of lobbyists becomes negligible. When the synergy factor is low, the decision to publish the list should consider the relative numbers of cooperators and defectors and the strength of emotional inhibition. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social recognition (publishing the list) and persuasive agents (lobbyists) can increase cooperation rates in threshold public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or similar mechanisms on efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which social mechanisms affect cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Hurlstone, M. J., Wang, S., Price, A., Leviston, Z., & Walker, I. (2017). Cooperation studies of catastrophe avoidance: implications for climate negotiations. *Climatic Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in collective-risk social dilemma games is strongly affected by perceived risk, inequality, uncertainty about thresholds, intergenerational discounting, and the possibility of reputation-based rewards or punishments. High perceived risk, communication (pledges), intermediate targets, and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation (i.e., the proportion of groups that avert catastrophe). Inequality and uncertainty about the threshold for catastrophe reduce cooperation. The paper discusses the need for credible enforcement mechanisms (punishment) in real-world climate agreements but does not review experiments that directly implement or vary punishment in the game. The main findings are about behavioral cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the importance of enforcement (punishment) mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in public-goods-like threshold games, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. It is useful for understanding moderators of cooperation and the conceptual role of punishment, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a public goods game. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that credible punishment mechanisms are likely to be important, especially under uncertainty, but does not provide data or effect sizes.

- source: Aktipis, A. (2016). Principles of cooperation across systems: from human sharing to multicellularity and cancer. *Evolutionary Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper identifies three general principles that facilitate cooperation: (1) the ability to 'Walk Away' from uncooperative partners, (2) need-based resource transfers, and (3) the necessity of increasingly sophisticated cheater suppression mechanisms as cooperation scales up. It reviews agent-based models (from prior work) showing that conditional movement (Walk Away) can stabilize cooperation in both dyadic and group contexts, including public goods game-like environments. The paper discusses how positive assortment (cooperators interacting more with each other) is key to the evolution of cooperation. It also highlights the challenges of cheater suppression in large-scale systems and draws analogies between human societies, multicellular organisms, and social insects. However, it does not provide new empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it directly analyze the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on mechanisms that support cooperation, such as partner choice (Walk Away) and cheater suppression, which are relevant to understanding the effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. The discussion of cheater suppression includes punishment as one possible mechanism but is not specific or detailed enough to inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The most relevant insight is that as cooperative systems scale up, more sophisticated and costly cheater suppression mechanisms (including punishment) are required, but the paper does not specify the efficiency consequences of these mechanisms in controlled game environments.

- source: Majolo, B., & Maréchal, L. (2017). Between-group competition elicits within-group cooperation in children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that simulated between-group competition (BGC) increases within-group cooperation among children aged 3-10, as measured by the number of stickers shared with group members (experiment 1) and the number of stickers contributed to a public good (experiment 2). The effect is robust to age (especially for children 6+), and is not explained by gender, kinship, or familiarity. However, the experiments do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main dependent variables are behavioral measures of cooperation, not payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does provide indirect evidence that between-group competition can increase cooperative behavior in children, but since punishment is not manipulated or measured, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are partially reported, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper should not be weighted heavily for the prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2016). Take One for the Team! Individual Heterogeneity and the Emergence of Latent Norms in a Volunteer's Dilemma. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated volunteer's dilemma games, efficient public good provision emerges through different behavioral patterns depending on group structure. In symmetric groups with fixed partners, turn-taking emerges, leading to high efficiency and equal payoffs. In asymmetric groups, especially when the lowest-cost actor is much stronger or when roles are randomized each round, solitary volunteering by the strong actor dominates, also leading to high efficiency. When distributional conflict is present (fixed roles), efficiency is somewhat lower due to reluctance of strong actors to always volunteer. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus provides no evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any condition. However, it is informative about baseline efficiency levels in repeated volunteer's dilemma games under various structural conditions (symmetry/asymmetry, matching protocol). For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions for step-level public good games with similar parameters, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Skatova, A., Spence, A., Leygue, C., & Ferguson, E. (2017). Guilty repair sustains cooperation, angry retaliation destroys it. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated social dilemma game without punishment, group efficiency (measured as group earnings and energy savings) declines over time due to increased defection and retaliation. Retaliation (increasing usage after being exploited) is associated with lower group payoffs, while repair (reducing usage after overusing) is associated with higher group payoffs. Groups with more prosocial members (who are more likely to repair and less likely to retaliate) achieve higher efficiency. Emotional responses (anger and guilt) mediate these behaviors: anger leads to retaliation, guilt leads to repair. The absence of punishment means that emotional self-regulation, especially guilt-driven repair, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated public-goods-like games without punishment, efficiency tends to decline over time due to retaliation and lack of repair. The presence of prosocial individuals who engage in guilty repair can partially sustain efficiency, but overall, the lack of punishment leads to lower group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be lower and decline over time, especially in groups with more selfish individuals. The findings are most relevant for control conditions (no punishment), and highlight the importance of emotional and social preference heterogeneity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot inform the incremental effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Efferson, C., Roca, C. P., Vogt, S., & Helbing, D. (2016). Sustained cooperation by running away from bad behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing players to move away from their current neighborhood (endogenous movement) in a spatial social dilemma leads to higher cooperation rates and, crucially, eliminates the average payoff disadvantage of cooperation compared to defection. This is achieved through positive assortment: cooperators cluster together, shielding themselves from defectors. In the control condition (assign location), where movement is exogenous, cooperation remains costly and less frequent. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so the results are about migration/partner choice as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Instead, it demonstrates that allowing migration/partner choice can eliminate the payoff disadvantage of cooperation, even without punishment. For prediction tasks about punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., migration) can also support efficiency. If a game design includes migration but not punishment, this paper is highly relevant; if the focus is on punishment, it is not.

- source: Feltovich, N., & Grossman, P. J. (2015). How does the effect of pre-play suggestions vary with group size? Experimental evidence from a threshold public-good game. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that pre-play cheap-talk communication (suggestions) significantly increases cooperation, the frequency of efficient (all-cooperate) outcomes, and group payoffs in a one-shot threshold public-good game, but only for small groups. As group size increases, the positive effect of communication on efficiency and payoffs declines rapidly, becoming statistically insignificant or negative for groups of size 7 and 15. This pattern holds for both one-way (random leader) and all-way (full broadcast) communication treatments. The results are robust to controls for risk preferences and other covariates. The study highlights the importance of group size as a moderator of communication's effectiveness in promoting efficient outcomes in public-good-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of communication (pre-play suggestions) on efficiency in threshold public-good games is highly dependent on group size. For small groups (n=2,3), enabling communication can substantially increase efficiency relative to control. For larger groups (n=7,15), the effect is minimal or even negative. When predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, group size should be treated as a key moderator of communication's impact. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means these results are most applicable to settings where only communication is varied. The findings suggest that extrapolating positive communication effects from small-group experiments to larger groups is not warranted.

- source: Hu, L. W., He, N. R., Weng, Q. F., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2020). Rewarding endowments lead to a win-win in the evolution of public cooperation and the accumulation of common resources. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: Rewarding endowments in a collective-risk social dilemma (a PGG variant with environmental feedback) can create a win-win for cooperation and resource accumulation, but only in a specific parameter region: low basic endowment and intermediate reward intensity. High reward intensity or high basic endowment can lead to resource exhaustion, even if cooperation is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctions. The main outcomes are the amount of common resources (group welfare) and a composite index of cooperation and resource accumulation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it studies only reward mechanisms and does not include punishment or sanctions. However, it provides evidence that reward mechanisms can increase both cooperation and group welfare, but only under certain parameter regimes. The findings suggest that the design of incentive mechanisms (reward intensity, basic endowment) can have non-monotonic effects on group efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of reward as a moderator or alternative.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taddei, F. (2007). The Durability of Public Goods Changes the Dynamics and Nature of Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that when the public good is durable (i.e., changes slowly relative to the frequency of cooperators), the dynamics of cooperation and group payoff can be substantially altered compared to classic models. For example, durable public goods can lead to multi-generational oscillations in cooperation and payoff, shift the thresholds for invasion and stability of cooperation, and even allow for transient increases in cooperation in settings where defection would otherwise dominate. The explicit modeling of public good dynamics reveals that group payoff and efficiency are not solely determined by current cooperation rates, but also by the history and durability of the public good. However, the paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how the dynamics and durability of the public good itself can affect efficiency and group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It highlights that efficiency is not always a simple function of current cooperation rates, especially when the public good is durable or slow to change. However, because the paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most relevant for understanding baseline dynamics and for considering how lagged or durable public goods might interact with other interventions in future models.

- source: Wang, X. M., Couto, M. C., Wang, N. Y., An, X. M., Chen, B., Dong, Y. L., Hilbe, C., & Zhang, B. Y. (2023). Cooperation and coordination in heterogeneous populations. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that endowment inequality is a significant obstacle to successful coordination in threshold public goods games, reducing the proportion of groups that reach the threshold and thus receive the group reward. Productivity inequality does not have a similar negative effect. Participants tend to coordinate on equal relative contributions, even when this is not payoff-maximizing. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or cooperation. The findings highlight the importance of focal points and fairness heuristics in equilibrium selection, and show that evolutionary game theory models may mis-predict outcomes unless they account for participants' initial expectations and heuristics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. However, it provides informative context on how inequality (in endowment or productivity) affects coordination and group success in threshold public goods games. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it suggests that baseline efficiency (without punishment) is lower under endowment inequality. This could be relevant for understanding the baseline to which punishment effects would be compared in similar asymmetric games.

- source: Chambers, C. R., & Baker, W. E. (2020). Robust Systems of Cooperation in the Presence of Rankings: How Displaying Prosocial Contributions Can Offset the Disruptive Effects of Performance Rankings. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing performance rankings tied to differential rewards causes a sharp and persistent decline in cooperation (measured as transfer rates) in a generalized reciprocity game. This effect is especially pronounced in groups that were highly cooperative before the intervention. When reputation information (about others' past giving) is also provided, the initial drop in cooperation is similar, but cooperation rates recover over time to pre-intervention levels. The mechanism appears to be that actors use reputation information to reward those who have been cooperative, especially when they perceive inequity. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding how performance rankings and reputation information affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but the outcome of interest is cooperation/transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for understanding the impact of competitive incentives and reputation mechanisms on cooperation.

- source: Xu, Z. J., Li, R. Y., & Zhang, L. Z. (2019). The role of memory in human strategy updating in optional public goods game. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a spatial optional public goods game, human players update their strategies primarily based on their own past experiences (memory), not by imitating neighbors. The probability of switching to a strategy is higher if it performed well for the individual in the past, regardless of its performance among neighbors. Imitation and mutation dynamics do not adequately explain observed behavior. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its main contribution is to the understanding of human strategy updating (memory vs. imitation) in repeated spatial PGGs with voluntary participation. It is not informative for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., & Jeong, J. (2015). Decoding covert motivations of free riding and cooperation from multi-feature pattern analysis of EEG signals. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that neural oscillations measured by EEG immediately after result presentation can predict whether a participant will cooperate or free ride in the next round, across three different payoff structures that manipulate motivations (fear and greed). Behaviorally, free riding rates are highest in the standard condition, lower in the 'no fear' condition, and lowest in the 'no greed' condition. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how payoff structure (fear/greed) affects cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., Chen, W. M., & Gu, C. L. (2022). Competition of punishment and reward among inequity-averse individuals in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that reputation-based indirect reciprocity can promote the spread of donation and cooperation behaviors in a donation game variant of the PGG, but this effect is limited by the costs of donation and cooperation, and by the presence of 'hypocrite' and 'realist' strategies. When only extreme altruists and egoists are present, cooperation rarely persists. The introduction of shunners (who conditionally cooperate based on reputation) allows for the spread of donation and cooperation under certain cost regimes. However, the addition of hypocrites (who donate for reputation but defect in daily interactions) and realists (who do not donate but cooperate in daily interactions) undermines the prevalence of donation, leading to more complex phase diagrams and sometimes the collapse of donation behavior. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of different strategies and the qualitative structure of phase transitions; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it explicitly excludes punishment mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of indirect reciprocity (reputation) as an alternative to punishment in promoting cooperation, and about how the presence of certain strategy types and cost parameters can affect the prevalence of prosocial behaviors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., reputation) can support cooperation under some conditions, but also that the presence of exploitative or non-donating cooperators can undermine these effects. The findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Otten, K., Frey, U. J., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2022). Human cooperation in changing groups in a large-scale public goods game. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the number of newcomers in a group is negatively associated with the average percentage of resources contributed to the public good. This effect is strongest in small groups and at later time periods. The lower group contribution is primarily due to newcomers contributing less than incumbents, though their contributions increase as they spend more time in the group. The difference between newcomers and incumbents is partially explained by differences in private good levels and public good levels, but remains significant even after controlling for these factors. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment, and there is no evidence of sanctioning mechanisms being central to the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative context about how group composition changes (newcomer entry) can reduce cooperation (as measured by contribution rates), especially in the absence of punishment or exclusion. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not report payoff-based outcomes or manipulate punishment. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in large, dynamic groups without punishment.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Excessive abundance of common resources deters social responsibility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a collective-risk social dilemma is most sustainable when the initial amount of common resources and the effectiveness of cooperative contributions are at intermediate levels. Excessive abundance of resources or overly effective cooperation allows defectors to free-ride, leading to the eventual collapse of cooperation and resource depletion. Conversely, too little resource or ineffective cooperation also leads to collapse. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fraction of cooperators and the sustainability of the common resource pool, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as defined in the prediction task. Its findings about resource abundance and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding resource dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Lima, F. W. S., & Stauffer, D. (2012). Evolution of tag-mediated altruistic behavior in one-shot encounters on large-scale complex networks. *Computer Physics Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that tag-mediated cooperation can emerge and temporarily dominate in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like games on complex networks, especially when agents can recognize and preferentially help those with similar tags. The dominance of specific cooperative strategies (altruist vs. ethnocentric) depends on network topology (rewiring probability), but over long timescales, all strategies tend to equilibrate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperation and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how tag-based recognition and network structure can support or suppress cooperation in the absence of punishment, but does not address payoff-based outcomes or the impact of sanctions. It may provide weak contextual information about baseline cooperation in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like environments without punishment, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Han, T. A. (2022). Institutional incentives for the evolution of committed cooperation: ensuring participation is as important as enhancing compliance. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional incentives (punishment or reward) can promote the evolution of cooperation and commitment compliance in a social dilemma with a prior commitment stage. Reward is generally more effective than punishment at promoting cooperation, especially when the cost of commitment is high or the benefit-to-cost ratio is large. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation in the commitment (not just compliance) further increases cooperation, particularly when participation is costly. The presence of noise in participation decisions can also stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are based on explicit payoff calculations and evolutionary stability analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in social dilemma games with a prior commitment stage, enabling punishment (or reward) increases group efficiency (cooperation frequency) compared to no incentives, provided the incentive budget is sufficient. Reward is more effective than punishment, especially as the cost of participation increases. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation (not just compliance) further increases efficiency, especially when participation is costly. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with a commitment or opt-in stage, and the model's explicit payoff calculations can be mapped to efficiency outcomes. However, the model is based on a two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a commitment stage, not a standard repeated public goods game, so predictions should be transferred with caution to multi-player, repeated settings.

- source: Han, T. A., Duong, M. H., & Perc, M. (2024). Evolutionary mechanisms that promote cooperation may not promote social welfare. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with peer or institutional incentives, punishment mechanisms (both peer and institutional) are often detrimental to social welfare, even when they increase cooperation. Peer punishment increases cooperation more than peer reward, but peer reward leads to higher social welfare. For institutional incentives, reward achieves positive social welfare across a broader range of parameters than punishment, even when both achieve similar cooperation levels. There is often an optimal, moderate level of incentive impact for maximizing social welfare, rather than maximizing cooperation or punishment strength. These findings are robust across mutation rates, selection intensities, and game configurations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments (here, the Prisoner's Dilemma) is likely to reduce efficiency (social welfare) compared to reward mechanisms, even if punishment increases cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of model parameters. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially with high impact-to-cost ratio) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency relative to control, unless the punishment is extremely cost-effective and moderate in impact. The findings suggest that maximizing cooperation via punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and that reward mechanisms are generally preferable for social welfare. The results are based on theoretical models, not empirical data, but are directly relevant to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Ahmad, M. G., & Loch, C. (2020). What do the followers want? The core functions of leadership. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated two-player strategic games, allowing players to choose a punishment option (where one can assign a low-value payoff to the other after decisions are made) does not increase group efficiency. Instead, punishment is associated with lower group payoffs due to retaliation cycles and reduced cooperation. In all games, the average group benefit under punishment is lower than under individual decision-making or leadership. Leadership (especially recommending leadership) increases efficiency, while punishment governance leads to the lowest efficiency. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across games with and without monetary incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated games with public-goods-like social dilemmas, enabling punishment as a governance option (without central leadership) reduces group efficiency compared to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across different game structures and incentive types. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment option (with moderate cost and impact, as in this design) should be expected to decrease efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is decentralized and not embedded in a leadership structure.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Lenaerts, T., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Mediating artificial intelligence developments through negative and positive incentives. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment (sanctions) and rewards can increase group welfare and promote safety compliance in a repeated innovation race game, but their effects depend on the underlying risk and incentive parameters. Punishment is generally more effective at suppressing unsafe (defective) behavior and increasing group welfare in the 'dilemma' region where individual and collective interests diverge, but if punishment is too strong or too efficient, it can lead to over-regulation and reduce innovation even when risk is low and unsafe behavior would be collectively optimal. Rewards can also promote safety and welfare, especially when highly cost-efficient, and do not suffer from the risk of over-regulation, but are generally less effective than punishment. The model provides explicit conditions for when each incentive is beneficial or harmful for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like innovation races is highly context-dependent. If the risk of disaster from unsafe behavior is high, punishment increases efficiency by promoting safe behavior. If the risk is low, strong punishment can reduce efficiency by suppressing innovation. The cost-effectiveness of punishment (punishment cost and impact) is critical: punishment is most beneficial when its cost to the punisher is low relative to its impact on the punished. Reward mechanisms can also increase efficiency, especially when highly cost-effective, and are less likely to reduce efficiency through over-regulation. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on the risk/benefit structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline efficiency of the control game. The findings are most directly applicable to innovation race or winner-take-all settings, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: Moser, S., & Musshoff, O. (2016). Ex-ante Evaluation of Policy Measures: Effects of Reward and Punishment for Fertiliser Reduction in Palm Oil Production. *Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, holding expected income effects constant, the design of incentives (reward vs. punishment, certain vs. uncertain) significantly affects both behavioral response and efficiency. Certain rewards are the most efficient (i.e., minimize profit loss for a given reduction in fertiliser use), while uncertain punishments are the least efficient. Certain punishments and uncertain rewards are intermediate. Punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty reduces efficiency. The findings suggest that policymakers aiming for efficient reduction of negative externalities should prefer certain rewards over punishments or uncertain incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-good-like field context, the efficiency impact of punishment (vs. reward) depends strongly on the design: punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty in the incentive (probabilistic punishment or reward) further reduces efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially uncertain punishment) will not increase efficiency as much as enabling certain rewards, even if the expected value is held constant. The results are most applicable to field settings with individual decision-making and externalities, but less so to classic multi-player PGGs with peer punishment. The efficiency outcome is measured as profit difference, directly supporting payoff-based prediction.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2020). Five rules for friendly rivalry in direct reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strategies combining partner and rival features (friendly rivals) exist in the repeated prisoner's dilemma, with the CAPRI strategy as a prime example. CAPRI achieves mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency) when both players use it, and punishes defection to prevent exploitation. Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI dominates other strategies in terms of abundance and average payoff across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios, error rates, and population sizes. The paper also shows that memory length (m >= 3) is crucial for such strategies. While the main results are for the two-player PD, the authors note that similar strategies exist for the three-person public goods game, suggesting potential generalizability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment strategies designed to be both efficient and defensible (like CAPRI) can substantially increase group efficiency and average payoff in repeated prisoner's dilemma settings, and by extension, in closely related public goods games. The findings suggest that, when punishment is implemented with sufficient memory and recovery mechanisms, it can robustly improve efficiency regardless of population size, error rate, or benefit-to-cost ratio. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that enabling well-designed punishment in repeated games will increase efficiency, especially when players can remember and respond to past actions. However, the results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated PDs, with only indirect evidence for multi-player PG games.

- source: Grimalda, G., Pondorfer, A., & Tracer, D. P. (2016). Social image concerns promote cooperation more than altruistic punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, two-player prisoner's dilemma, the presence of a local authority (Big Man) as an observer increases cooperation and leads to the highest average payoffs, exceeding baseline. Introducing a costly punishment stage (where players can spend to reduce the other's payoff) does not increase cooperation above the social image condition and results in lower average payoffs than baseline, due to the costs incurred by both punisher and punished. When both social image and punishment are present, efficiency is still lower than with social image alone and remains below baseline. The presence of an external (outgroup) authority does not increase cooperation or payoffs. Thus, punishment is not an efficient mechanism for promoting cooperation in this setting, and social image concerns are more effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot, two-player PD with costly punishment, enabling punishment reduces efficiency compared to baseline (no punishment). The effect is robust: both punishment alone and punishment combined with social image concerns yield lower payoffs than baseline. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related (two-player PD with all-or-nothing contribution). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (one-shot, small group, all-or-nothing, high punishment cost), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The control (baseline) efficiency is higher than the punishment treatment efficiency. This evidence should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2011). What makes people cooperate? Individual differences in BAS/BIS predict strategic reciprocation in a public goods game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with super cooperators (who both punish defectors and reward cooperators), the ability of speculative defectors to identify and cooperate with super cooperators (i.e., higher information level) generally increases the prevalence of super cooperators and raises average social payoff (efficiency), provided the cost of punishment/reward is not too high. When the cost is high, increasing information can instead destroy super cooperators. The alliance between speculative defectors and super cooperators can insulate super cooperators from exploitation by unconditional cooperators, further supporting cooperation and efficiency. The model demonstrates that the introduction of punishment/reward mechanisms and information about others' strategies can substantially improve group efficiency compared to the baseline with only unconditional cooperators and defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatially structured prisoner's dilemma environments, enabling punishment (and reward) mechanisms—especially when paired with higher information about others' strategies—can increase group efficiency, as measured by average social payoff. The effect is strongest when the cost of punishment/reward is moderate or low. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games with similar structure, though the model is not a standard public goods game but a spatial PD with three strategies. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient information) should increase efficiency relative to the control. However, if the cost of punishment/reward is high, the effect can reverse. The model's focus on spatial structure, information, and cost of punishment/reward provides indirect but informative guidance for analogous PGG-like environments.

- source: Bhaskar, V., & Thomas, C. (2019). Community Enforcement of Trust with Bounded Memory. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain efficient cooperation (trust/lending) in large societies with random matching and one-sided moral hazard depends critically on the information structure available to punishers (lenders). With perfect bounded memory, punishment unravels and efficiency collapses. With coarse information (binary flag for recent default), temporary exclusion can sustain high efficiency, with the exact level depending on the profitability of lending and the exclusion length. The model provides explicit formulas for borrower and lender payoffs under different regimes. When lending is very profitable, some inefficiency is unavoidable due to the need for randomization and endogenous adverse selection. The results generalize to other sequential-move games with one-sided moral hazard.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining efficiency depends on the information structure: coarse information (pooling recent defaulters) enables efficient punishment and high payoffs, while fine-grained information leads to unraveling and zero efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will only improve efficiency if the punishment mechanism is supported by an information structure that prevents cherry-picking of near-rehabilitated defectors. The model is not a PGG or direct PGG variant, but the logic applies to trust games and other one-sided moral hazard settings. The findings are most relevant for settings with random matching, temporary exclusion, and information constraints.

- source: Quan, J., Pu, Z. J., & Wang, X. J. (2021). Comparison of social exclusion and punishment in promoting cooperation: Who should play the leading role?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both keeping and reversing social norms can promote cooperation and increase social welfare (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games with punishment. The effect is moderated by the benefit-cost ratio: keeping rules are more effective at low benefit-cost ratios, while reversing rules are more effective at high benefit-cost ratios. Public reputation scenarios consistently support higher cooperation and welfare than private reputation scenarios. Punishment actions can help sustain cooperation, but their cost can reduce average welfare. The specific social norm in use (among 16 studied) determines whether the population reaches a cooperative or non-cooperative stable state, especially in private reputation settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of social norms and the structure of reputation (public vs. private) are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games. The results suggest that, in environments similar to pairwise donation games with indirect reciprocity, enabling punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but the net effect on efficiency depends on the cost of punishment and the social norm in use. Public reputation and appropriate social norms (keeping or reversing, depending on benefit-cost ratio) are important for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to pairwise donation games, not standard public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for prediction.

- source: Saak, A. E. (2012). Collective Reputation, Social Norms, and Participation. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that collective reputation mechanisms with local peer punishment can sustain higher efficiency (average payoffs, welfare) than individual reputation, as long as group size is not too small or too large and peer monitoring is present.
