Peer punishment deters shirking and allows for softer public punishment, increasing the duration of efficient cooperation. The optimal group size increases with the discount factor, the informativeness of public signals, and the quality premium, but decreases with the relative gain from shirking. Without peer monitoring, collective reputation is less efficient than individual reputation. Perfect global peer monitoring allows for arbitrarily large efficient groups. Mandatory traceability (which enables individual punishment) can reduce welfare by undermining group-based peer punishment. The model provides clear comparative statics and welfare implications for the design of collective brands and group-based sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling peer punishment (local or global) in repeated group settings with imperfect public monitoring increases efficiency and group payoffs, compared to settings without such punishment. The model is not a literal public goods game, but is adjacent: it is a repeated moral hazard game with group-level payoffs, endogenous peer punishment, and collective reputation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms (especially peer punishment) on efficiency, conditional on group size, monitoring structure, and information. The paper also provides comparative statics on how efficiency gains from punishment depend on group size, monitoring, and information, which can inform predictions about moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Han, T. A., Lenaerts, T. C., Santos, F. C., & Pereira, L. M. (2022). Voluntary safety commitments provide an escape from over-regulation in AI development. *Technology in Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated two-player (and generalizable to N-player) innovation race game, unconditional punishment of risk-taking (unsafe) behavior can reduce social welfare by over-regulating and suppressing beneficial innovation when risk is low. Introducing voluntary, binding commitments to safe behavior—where only those who commit and then defect are punished—enables the system to achieve high efficiency: safety compliance prevails when it is collectively optimal, and innovation is not stifled when risk-taking is preferred. Both peer and institutional punishment mechanisms are effective if they are targeted only at commitment violators. The model shows that the efficiency of punishment (cost to punisher vs. impact on punished) modulates these effects, but the voluntary commitment mechanism robustly improves outcomes across parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms—specifically, whether punishment is unconditional or conditional on voluntary commitment—has a major impact on group efficiency in public-goods-like innovation dilemmas. For prediction tasks, the presence of voluntary, binding commitments with targeted punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to unconditional punishment, especially in environments where the collective optimum varies with risk. The model also highlights the importance of punishment cost and effectiveness, and shows that allowing players to opt out of commitment (and thus avoid punishment) can prevent over-regulation and welfare loss. These insights are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in games with similar structure, even if the setting is not a classic PGG.

- source: Köster, R., Hadfield-Menell, D., Everett, R., Weidinger, L., Hadfield, G. K., & Leibo, J. Z. (2022). Spurious normativity enhances learning of compliance and enforcement behavior in artificial agents. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Introducing a punishment regime for norm violations (e.g., eating a poisonous berry) enables agents to learn to avoid harmful actions more quickly, resulting in higher group efficiency (collective return) than in the absence of punishment. Adding a second, arbitrary 'silly' rule (punishment for eating a harmless berry) further increases group efficiency during the learning process, as it provides more opportunities for agents to practice enforcement and compliance, leading to faster and more robust avoidance of genuinely harmful actions. The effect is strongest during the intermediate learning phase, with the silly rule condition yielding the highest group payoffs. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment, even when applied to arbitrary rules, can enhance the emergence of efficient, norm-compliant behavior in artificial agent societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a multiagent environment with norm violations increases group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust even when punishment is costly and decentralized. The design is not a standard public goods game, but the core mechanism—costly third-party punishment of norm violations—closely parallels PGG punishment treatments. The results suggest that, for similar environments, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the environment presents a credit assignment problem (delayed or opaque consequences of harmful actions). The effect is further enhanced if the environment provides more opportunities for agents to practice enforcement (e.g., via additional, even arbitrary, punishable actions). The findings are most directly applicable to settings where learning and credit assignment are challenging, and where punishment is visible and incentivized. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with immediate, transparent payoffs, but the qualitative direction (punishment increases efficiency) is likely to hold.

- source: Perry, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2020). Foresight in a Game of Leadership. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a two-player leadership/inspection game, standard rational or learning protocols do not support the emergence of punishment or cooperation, leading to inefficient outcomes (no production, no inspection). However, if leaders use foresight (weighting future payoffs and anticipating subordinates' best responses), then punishment and production can become stable equilibria, resulting in higher payoffs for both leader and group (i.e., higher efficiency). Selective imitation can also support this outcome if subordinates use best response. The effect depends on the weight placed on future payoffs (the foresight parameter) and the cost-benefit structure of punishment and production.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated or multi-round games where leaders (punishers) value future payoffs and anticipate others' responses, punishment can increase group efficiency by enabling cooperation/production that would not occur otherwise. The effect is sensitive to the cost of punishment, the benefit of production, and the degree of foresight. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in games with forward-looking or strategic agents (or mechanisms that mimic foresight/selective imitation) can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of punishment is not too high relative to the benefit. However, in the absence of such mechanisms, punishment may not emerge and efficiency remains low.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). Two ways to overcome the three social dilemmas of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with private reputation assessment, punishment can increase group efficiency and maintain stable cooperation if assessment norms are designed to (1) detect free riders, (2) justify punishment of bad actors, and (3) generously assess cooperation toward badly reputed recipients. Two main solutions are identified: one based on recipient-focused justified punishment (with generosity toward bad recipients), and another based on doer-focused justified punishment (with high cooperation efficiency required). The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c), with more generous norms required at lower efficiency. The presence of punishment, when justified and properly assessed, leads to higher average payoffs and stable cooperative regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the assessment norm and the efficiency of cooperation (b/c). If the punishment system is designed so that justified punishment is recognized and not misinterpreted (i.e., the intention is correctly communicated), and if generosity is applied in assessing cooperation toward bad recipients, then enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The benefit-to-cost ratio (mpcr analog) is a key moderator: at higher efficiency, more norms can sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence and design of punishment, the assessment norm, and the efficiency parameter are crucial for forecasting the efficiency gain from punishment.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2016). Evolution of cooperation by the introduction of the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism, where punishment is more likely and more severe when the payoff difference between punisher and target is larger, leads to a substantial increase in both cooperation and average payoff in spatial prisoner's dilemma simulations. This effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, and for different average degrees of connectivity. The model demonstrates that, contrary to some previous findings with standard peer-punishment, this form of punishment can increase group welfare (average payoff) rather than decrease it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, a punishment mechanism that is probabilistic and based on payoff differences can increase average payoff, in contrast to standard fixed-cost peer punishment which often reduces group payoff. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in adjacent game structures, especially where punishment is adaptive or conditional on payoff differences. The results suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (e.g., making it proportional to payoff differences and probabilistic) is a key moderator of its effect on efficiency. However, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution due to the difference in game structure.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). Characteristics of the evolution of cooperation by the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism—where the probability and severity of punishment are proportional to payoff differences—can increase both cooperation and the average payoff of all players in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and for varying average degrees of connectivity, though the parameter range for positive effects narrows as connectivity increases. The model avoids antisocial punishment by design. The results suggest that, with appropriate parameterization, punishment can enhance group efficiency, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments structurally similar to spatial prisoner's dilemma games, a well-designed probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff), especially when the punishment is neither too weak nor too strong. The effect is sensitive to the network structure: as the average number of connections increases, the range of punishment parameters that yield efficiency gains becomes narrower. While the model is not a public goods game, the payoff-based outcome and the punishment mechanism are closely related, making the findings informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like settings, especially where network structure and punishment calibration are relevant.

- source: Friedman, D., & Singh, N. (2004). Negative reciprocity - The coevolution of memes and genes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that negative reciprocity (punishment) can transform a standard social dilemma (Prisoner's Dilemma) from an inefficient all-defect equilibrium to a coordination game with a Pareto efficient all-cooperate equilibrium, provided the punishment motive is strong enough. However, individual willingness to punish is undermined by evolutionary free-rider problems, as the costs are borne individually but the benefits are shared. The key finding is that group-level enforcement of punishment norms (memes), using low-cost sanctions such as shunning or status reduction, can sustain the optimal level of negative reciprocity and thus maximize group fitness. The model predicts that the efficiency of cooperation increases with the strength and enforcement of punishment norms, and that these norms will coevolve with individual capacities and environmental hostility. The comparative statics suggest that efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly, more effective, and more strongly enforced by group norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when group-enforced) increases efficiency in social dilemmas, compared to control conditions without punishment. The model is based on a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, but the authors argue that the logic extends to n-person games like public goods games. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the rigor of group enforcement are key moderators of the efficiency gain from punishment. The model also predicts that efficiency gains from punishment are larger when punishment is less costly and more effective, and when group norms for enforcement are strong. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct evidence from public goods games.

- source: Wubs, M., Bshary, R., & Lehmann, L. (2016). Coevolution between positive reciprocity, punishment, and partner switching in repeated interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the evolutionary success of punishment, positive reciprocity, and partner switching depends on the number of rounds, group size, and the costs/effectiveness of punishment and switching. Punishment is favored in small and moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. Partner switching dominates in large groups or when the number of rounds is high, unless switching is costly. Positive reciprocity is rarely the dominant mechanism, except when punishment is less effective or more costly. The model does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but evolutionary success is determined by aggregate payoff, so the results indirectly inform about conditions under which punishment or other mechanisms would lead to higher average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like environments. It suggests that punishment is more likely to improve group outcomes (in terms of evolutionary success/payoff) in small or moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. In large groups or with many rounds, partner switching may be more effective. The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games, especially repeated dyadic interactions, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, but the evolutionary dominance of strategies is determined by payoff, so the results can be used to infer the likely direction of punishment's effect on efficiency under different design dimensions.

- source: Zhu, Y. Y., Zhang, J. L., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2017). Evolutionary dynamics of strategies for threshold snowdrift games on complex networks. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment in threshold snowdrift games increases the evolutionary advantage of cooperation over defection, especially when punishment cost is low and the threshold is moderate. Larger thresholds generally promote cooperation, but too high or too low thresholds can be detrimental. Lower punishment cost (to the punisher) and higher punishment impact (on the defector) both facilitate cooperation. These effects are robust across well-mixed and networked populations, including single-layer and double-layer (interconnected) networks. However, the main reported outcomes are cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in threshold public-goods-like games, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. Theoretical payoff difference (P_c - P_d) is analyzed, but simulation results focus on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective when the cost to punishers is low and the penalty to defectors is high, and that network structure and threshold size moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve cooperation under certain parameter regimes, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Carr, R. (2014). High strength-of-ties and low mobility enable the evolution of third-party punishment. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible third-party punishment (3PP) can evolve and sustain cooperation in structured populations when social-structural constraints are high (i.e., high strength-of-ties and low mobility). Under these conditions, clusters of responsible punishers can induce cooperation in their local neighborhoods, leading to higher local pay-offs and the spread of both cooperation and punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed or highly mobile populations, responsible 3PP does not evolve, and cooperation fails to emerge. The model shows that 3PP is pivotal for the emergence of cooperation and increased pay-off, as neither population structure alone nor direct punishment alone suffice. The findings are robust to variations in network structure and to the presence of antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and increasing pay-off (and thus efficiency) depends critically on the social structure of the population. Specifically, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency in games with high strength-of-ties (low average degree, i.e., players interact repeatedly with a small set of neighbors) and low mobility (players do not frequently change partners). In well-mixed or highly mobile settings, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs with fixed, repeated local interactions and low partner switching will see greater efficiency gains from punishment mechanisms, while designs with random matching or high mobility will not. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it uses a two-player cooperation game with third-party punishment), so the transfer to standard PGGs is not exact but likely informative.

- source: Helbing, D., Yu, W. J., Opp, K. D., & Rauhut, H. (2014). Conditions for the Emergence of Shared Norms in Populations with Incompatible Preferences. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (sanctioning) is a powerful mechanism for establishing shared norms in heterogeneous populations, even when preferences are incompatible. Behavior-based punishment is more effective than preference-based punishment in creating conformity. However, the resulting norm is not always efficient: sometimes a minority can establish an unpopular or suboptimal norm, and average payoff may not be maximized. Adaptive group pressure (where local majorities punish minorities) can lead to spontaneous norm emergence, but again, the content of the norm may be arbitrary and not welfare-maximizing. The model highlights the importance of path dependence, local interaction, and randomness in norm formation. Payoff (welfare) is explicitly tracked, and the model demonstrates that sanctioning can both increase and decrease average payoff depending on the scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (payoff) in public-goods-like environments is highly context-dependent. Punishment can increase conformity and create shared norms, but does not guarantee higher efficiency: the emergent norm may be suboptimal. The model suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its cost, effectiveness, and whether it is behavior- or preference-based. Local interaction and adaptive group pressure can facilitate norm emergence, but do not ensure welfare maximization. For prediction, this paper suggests that enabling punishment will often increase conformity but may have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially if the norm that emerges is not aligned with group welfare. The model's adjacent structure to PGGs means its findings are suggestive but not directly quantitative for standard PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Boyd, R., & Mathew, S. (2015). Third-party monitoring and sanctions aid the evolution of language. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper develops a repeated signaling game (a variant of the Sir Philip Sidney game) to model the evolution of honest communication. It finds that when only direct reciprocity is possible and lies are hard to detect, honest signaling is not evolutionarily stable except in special cases. However, when third-party monitoring and punishment are introduced—so that multiple community members can observe, aggregate evidence, and sanction dishonesty—the range of parameters supporting honest signaling expands greatly. This is because third-party monitoring increases the probability of detecting lies and reduces false accusations, making it more likely that group-beneficial honest signaling (analogous to high efficiency) is maintained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that adding third-party monitoring and punishment mechanisms to repeated social dilemmas (even when not strictly public goods games) can substantially increase the likelihood of group-beneficial outcomes, such as honest signaling or cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of third-party punishment and monitoring should be expected to have a strongly positive effect on efficiency or group payoff, especially when direct detection of defection is difficult. The model is not a standard PGG, but the logic is closely related and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where monitoring and punishment are possible.

- source: Zhao, K., Ferguson, E., & Smillie, L. D. (2017). Politeness and Compassion Differentially Predict Adherence to Fairness Norms and Interventions to Norm Violations in Economic Games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that politeness and honesty-humility predict fair allocations in the dictator game, while compassion predicts third-party recompensation (helping the victim) and, to a lesser extent, third-party punishment (punishing the offender). Most third-party punishers are 'prosocial' (they also behave fairly as dictators), and both punishment and recompensation increase with the degree of unfairness observed. However, the study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the game structure is not a public goods game but a dictator/third-party punishment/recompensation design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it offers insight into the psychological predictors of punishment and recompensation behaviors in third-party games, it does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze public goods game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or rounds. Its findings are only indirectly relevant for understanding the motivations behind punishment and helping behaviors, not their impact on group efficiency.

- source: Whitson, J. A., Wang, C. S., See, Y. H. M., Baker, W. E., & Murnighan, J. K. (2015). How, when, and why recipients and observers reward good deeds and punish bad deeds. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party observers are more likely to punish bad deeds (e.g., deception) than direct recipients, and that direct recipients are more likely to reward good deeds (e.g., honesty) than observers. These effects are mediated by feelings of obligation: observers feel a stronger obligation to punish, while recipients feel a stronger obligation to reward. The studies use one-shot, controlled scenarios with real monetary stakes, but do not involve repeated interaction, group provision, or public goods. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward assigned), not group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in one-shot, dyadic or triadic settings, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that the presence of observers may increase the likelihood and intensity of punishment for norm violations, but do not inform how this affects group payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about who is likely to punish and under what circumstances, not about the payoff consequences of punishment.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Power Asymmetries and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with Variable Cooperative Investment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with variable cooperative investment and a punishment option, punishment did not increase the likelihood that targets would increase their cooperation, regardless of whether the punisher was strong or weak, or whether the game was symmetric or asymmetric. Both weak and strong players retaliated against punishment, and the threat of punishment from strong players may have led to higher baseline cooperation from weak players, but actual punishment had no effect on subsequent cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player games with variable investment and a punishment option, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency, even when punishment is more effective for some players (power asymmetry). However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar two-player designs, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may provoke retaliation. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs (iterated PD with variable investment) and may be less transferable to standard public goods games or multiplayer settings.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). The Effect of Power Asymmetries on Cooperation and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with punishment, punishment did not increase cooperation rates. Instead, cooperation from a partner was more effective at eliciting cooperation than punishment. Punishment, especially from strong players, sometimes provoked retaliation and further defection. Weak players never retaliated against punishment, but both weak and strong players retaliated against strong punishers. Players were more likely to opt out after being punished or when facing a strong partner. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and the authors explicitly state their focus is on behavioral rather than payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma games with punishment, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation and may even have detrimental effects on cooperative behavior, especially when power asymmetries exist. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that, at least in two-player, all-or-nothing, binary-choice games with possible opting out, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may worsen behavioral outcomes. Caution should be used in generalizing to multi-player or continuous-contribution public goods games.

- source: Makovi, K., Sargsyan, A., Li, W. D., Bonnefon, J. F., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Trust within human-machine collectives depends on the perceived consensus about cooperative norms. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a third-party punishment game followed by a trust game, humans earn less trust by sharing with or punishing bots than with humans. Trust-gain is attenuated but not eliminated in mixed human-bot collectives. Perceived consensus about cooperative norms predicts trust-gain, and experimentally making norm consensus salient increases trust-gain in some conditions. The main outcomes are trust and norm perception, not group efficiency or total payoff. The design is adjacent to public goods games, but the outcome focus is on trust and norm-following rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its design is adjacent (third-party punishment game with binary sharing), and the main outcomes are trust-gain and norm perception, not efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can increase trust in norm-followers, especially when norm consensus is salient, but do not quantify effects on group efficiency. The paper may inform mechanism design around norm salience and trust, but is not directly informative for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Rijkhoff, S. A. M., & van Dijk, E. (2013). Equality as a benchmark for third-party punishment and reward: The moderating role of uncertainty in social dilemmas. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishers and rewarders use the equality rule as a benchmark for sanctioning decisions only when the size of the common resource is certain. Under certainty, those who take more than an equal share are punished much more, and those who take an equal or smaller share are rewarded more. Under uncertainty, the amount of punishment or reward is linearly related to the amount harvested, with no sharp benchmark. The emotional basis for punishment is negative affect (anger), while for reward it is positive affect. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or common resource games, as it does not report any payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how the clarity of the cooperation norm (equality rule) and environmental uncertainty affect the application of punishment and reward. In settings where the cooperation norm is clear and known, punishment is more sharply targeted at norm violators; under uncertainty, punishment is more diffuse. This may indirectly inform predictions about the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation or efficiency, but the paper does not provide direct evidence for such effects.

- source: Ibanez, M., & Martinsson, P. (2013). Curbing coca cultivation in Colombia - A framed field experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that both increased risk of punishment (eradication) and increased legal profits (subsidies) reduce investment in illegal coca cultivation, but the effect is highly heterogeneous. About one third of participants have high moral costs and do not need external incentives, while two fifths are very hard to deter. Only about a quarter are responsive to policy levers. The supply of coca is inelastic to policy changes unless eradication risk is high (>30%). Carrots (subsidies) are more effective in areas with better market integration, while sticks (punishment) are more effective in depressed areas. The study provides cost estimates for both policies and suggests that optimal policy should mix both, tailored to local conditions. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (investment choices), not group efficiency or total welfare as in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence on how exogenous punishment (risk of eradication) and reward (subsidy) policies affect individual investment in a socially harmful activity, with a design adjacent to public goods games. The findings support that punishment can reduce undesirable behavior, but the effect is highly dependent on individual heterogeneity. The results are most useful for predicting how changes in punishment and reward parameters might affect behavioral outcomes (e.g., investment in the public bad), but less directly for predicting group efficiency as defined in PGGs. The cost-effectiveness analysis and elasticity estimates can inform models that link punishment/reward design to behavioral and, indirectly, payoff outcomes, especially in field-like or framed settings with real-world stakes.

- source: Guo, Z. Y., Guo, R., Xu, C. Y., & Wu, Z. (2022). Reflexive or reflective? Group bias in third-party punishment in Chinese and Western cultures. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both Chinese and Western participants show ingroup favoritism in third-party punishment under reflexive (fast, high cognitive load) conditions, punishing ingroup norm violators less than outgroup violators. Under reflective (slow, low cognitive load) conditions, Western participants—especially those with high group identity—reduce ingroup favoritism and may even punish ingroup violators more, while Chinese participants continue to show strong ingroup favoritism regardless of decision mode. Group identity amplifies these effects. The main outcome is the amount of punishment assigned, not efficiency or group payoff. The game is a third-party punishment mini-dictator game, not a standard public goods game, and does not report on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity, culture, and decision-making mode moderate the behavioral use of costly punishment in a third-party punishment context. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is adjacent to PGGs (mini-dictator/TPP), and the outcome is punishment behavior, not payoff. The findings are useful for understanding when and how punishment is used, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, X. H., Molleman, L., & van Dolder, D. (2021). Do descriptive social norms drive peer punishment? Conditional punishment strategies and their impact on cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that individuals' punishment of defectors is conditional on descriptive social norms: people punish more when cooperation or punishment is more common in a reference group. There is substantial heterogeneity: some punish independently, some increase punishment with higher cooperation (norm enforcement), and some with higher punishment (conformist). The dynamic model demonstrates that these conditional punishment strategies can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of high cooperation rates in populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment behavior is conditional on social context, and that such conditional punishment can promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. However, because the experiment is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with punishment and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games. The dynamic model suggests that conditional punishment can help sustain high cooperation rates, which may imply higher efficiency in analogous settings, but this is an indirect inference. The paper is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and heterogeneity in punishment, and for modeling the potential for punishment to support cooperation, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Spadaro, G., Molho, C., Van Prooijen, J. W., Romano, A., Mosso, C. O., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2023). Corrupt third parties undermine trust and prosocial behaviour between people. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across five experimental studies, participants exposed to a corrupt (dishonest) third-party punisher (TPP) trusted strangers less and cooperated less in subsequent economic games (trust game, prisoner's dilemma, dictator game) with TPP present. The effect is robust to both second-hand and first-hand exposure to corruption, and to whether the TPP's corruption harms only the experiment or also a collective good. The negative effect of perceived TPP corruption on cooperation is mediated by reduced interpersonal trust. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of TPP corruption on efficiency is not measured. The design always includes a TPP; there is no comparison to a no-punishment baseline, so the effect of enabling punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived integrity of a third-party punisher moderates the effectiveness of punishment institutions in promoting trust and cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment (vs. no punishment) on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it report efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that if punishment is administered by corrupt or untrusted third parties, its positive effect on cooperation may be undermined or even reversed, but the paper does not quantify efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of institutional trustworthiness, not about the baseline effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.

- source: Zhang, Y., Yu, H. B., Yin, Y., & Zhou, X. L. (2016). Intention Modulates the Effect of Punishment Threat in Norm Enforcement via the Lateral Orbitofrontal Cortex. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that the intention behind punishment threat critically shapes norm compliance: when a partner intentionally retains the right to punish, participants allocate less to the partner (reduced norm compliance), but when the partner intentionally waives the right, participants allocate more (increased norm compliance and positive affect). When the punishment threat is unintentional (computer-decided), these effects are weaker or absent. The rLOFC is shown to mediate these effects, as brain stimulation modulates the behavioral response to intention. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the social context and perceived intention behind punishment can strongly affect norm compliance (proxied by allocation), but does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where punishment is perceived as self-serving or intentional, punishment may reduce cooperation or norm compliance, while unintentional or third-party punishment may be less detrimental or even neutral. For prediction, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as legitimate or self-interested, but the lack of efficiency data and the use of a dictator game limit direct applicability to public goods game efficiency predictions.

- source: Chen, H. Z., Zeng, Z. J., & Ma, J. H. (2020). The source of punishment matters: Third-party punishment restrains observers from selfish behaviors better than does second-party punishment by shaping norm perceptions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) of unfair behavior in a dictator game leads observers to perceive higher descriptive and injunctive norms for sharing, and to share more themselves, compared to second-party punishment (SPP). Mediation analyses show that the effect on sharing is primarily through changes in descriptive norm perception. The effect is robust across two experiments with different manipulations (verbal blame and monetary punishment). However, the outcome is individual sharing behavior, not group efficiency or payoff, and the context is a dictator game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the source of punishment (third-party vs. second-party) can affect observers' prosocial behavior by shaping norm perceptions, especially descriptive norms. While the findings are robust for sharing behavior in dictator games, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment mechanisms that are perceived as norm-expressive (e.g., third-party punishment) may have stronger effects on prosocial behavior, but the transferability to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is indirect and should be treated with caution.

- source: D'Orsogna, M. R., Kendall, R., McBride, M., & Short, M. B. (2013). Criminal Defectors Lead to the Emergence of Cooperation in an Experimental, Adversarial Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that the availability of the Informant strategy (criminals who also report/punish other criminals) is necessary for the emergence and stability of high cooperation in an adversarial game. When Informants are allowed, the system reliably transitions to and maintains a high-cooperation state (Utopia), regardless of initial conditions or parameter profile. When Informants are not allowed, the system converges to high defection (Dystopia). The results are robust across different initializations, parameter profiles (costs of reporting), and levels of behavioral inertia. However, the study does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare as outcome variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of a punishment mechanism (specifically, the Informant strategy) is crucial for the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in adversarial games. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The game is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by defectors) can transform a high-defection environment into a high-cooperation one, which would likely increase efficiency if measured. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of this inference.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Moral labels increase cooperation and costly punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that moral framing (using labels like 'I cooperate' and 'I cheat') increases the likelihood of both moral and hypocritical punishment compared to neutral framing ('A' and 'B'). In Experiment 1, where only participants could punish, moral framing increased punishment but did not affect cooperation. In Experiment 2, where both participants and partners could punish, moral framing increased both punishment and cooperation. Antisocial punishment and general punishment bias were not affected by framing. The results suggest that the effect of moral framing on cooperation is contingent on the presence of punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and framing of punishment options can increase both punishment behavior and, under certain conditions, cooperation in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage, but the findings about behavioral responses to punishment and framing may be informative for predicting when punishment will increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) in similar environments. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to increase cooperation when it is symmetric (i.e., both players can punish), and that moral framing amplifies this effect.

- source: Bone, J. E., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). Human punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge and a desire for equality. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge (punishment occurs even when it cannot restore equality) and a desire for equality (when possible, punishment is tailored to equalize payoffs). Punishment is more likely when stealing creates disadvantageous inequality and when punishment is effective. However, the outcomes measured are behavioral (punishment frequency and amount), not efficiency or group payoff. The experiment is a two-player, one-shot, anonymous game with no communication, and does not use a public goods game or its direct variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about how punishment behavior is shaped by the effectiveness of punishment and the presence of inequality, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player settings. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be used and how it will be targeted, especially regarding the importance of punishment effectiveness (fee-to-fine ratio) and inequality, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not report efficiency or welfare, so its value for the prediction task is limited to qualitative insights about punishment motivation and targeting.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). Networks and Interethnic Cooperation. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of groups to enforce cooperation through peer punishment depends critically on the structure of their internal communication networks. Specifically, the 'reach' or 'integration' of the network—how widely and quickly information about defections can spread—determines how easily cooperation can be sustained. Denser networks are not necessarily better; networks with high integration (no highly peripheral or bottleneck nodes) are optimal for supporting cooperation. The model also shows that there is an upper bound on the proportion of interethnic (cross-group) interactions that can be kept cooperative, determined by the network's reach. Improvements in communication speed, longer punishment phases, and more integrated networks all make cooperation easier to sustain. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, but rather the theoretical conditions for sustaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how network structure moderates the effectiveness of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (and thus the likely efficiency gain from enabling punishment) depends not on network density but on integration/reach. However, the model is not a public goods game per se, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency. It is most useful for qualitative reasoning about the importance of network structure (especially communication reach) as a moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation.

- source: Morris, A., MacGlashan, J., Littman, M. L., & Cushman, F. (2017). Evolution of flexibility and rigidity in retaliatory punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment is costly, flexible (learning-based) victims are less likely to learn to punish, making them more vulnerable in repeated interactions. This leads to the evolution of rigid punishment strategies (i.e., always punish theft), even when such punishment is costly and not always effective. The learning dynamics of reinforcement learning agents show that thieves are more likely to adapt flexibly, while victims tend to persist in punishment. The evolutionary simulations confirm that the cost of punishment biases selection toward rigid punishment. The findings are primarily about the evolution and persistence of punishment strategies, not about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence about the evolution of punishment strategies in repeated dyadic games, focusing on the interplay between learning dynamics and evolutionary selection. While it does not directly address efficiency or group payoff in public goods games, it is informative about when rigid punishment is likely to evolve and persist, especially when punishment is costly. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the cost of punishment and the learning dynamics of participants may influence the prevalence of rigid punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be rigid or flexible, which may indirectly affect efficiency in related environments.

- source: FeldmanHall, O., Otto, A. R., & Phelps, E. A. (2018). Learning Moral Values: Another's Desire to Punish Enhances One's Own Punitive Behavior. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four experiments, participants who learned about another's strong preference for punishment increased their own endorsement of punitive options when responding to unfair offers. This effect was observed both when participants actively implemented another's preferences and when they passively observed them. The effect generalized across online and lab settings, and from a justice game (with compensatory and punitive options) to the ultimatum game (where only costly punishment is available). The learning process is best described by a reinforcement learning model sensitive to the magnitude of unfairness. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare; the focus is on individual behavioral change in punitive choices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that exposure to others' punitive preferences increases individuals' own willingness to punish in response to unfairness, even when punishment is costly. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is dyadic and focused on justice restoration, not multi-player public goods provision. The findings are most relevant for predicting changes in punitive behavior, not efficiency.

- source: Sun, K. T., Liu, Y., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Evolution of trust in a hierarchical population with punishing investors. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenously imposed collective punishment in a binary trust game significantly increases trustworthiness (from 38.5% to 82.3%) and somewhat increases trust (from 28.1% to 39.6%). When the adoption of the punishment mechanism is determined by majority vote, most subjects vote against it, and the endogenous adoption does not further increase trust or trustworthiness compared to exogenous imposition. Cognitive ability predicts voting for punishment. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, and the main focus is on behavioral measures (trust, trustworthiness, voting).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that collective punishment can increase trustworthiness and trust in a binary trust game, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it is a binary trust game rather than a multi-player PGG. The punishment mechanism is collective and endogenous in some treatments, but the main outcomes are behavioral. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can improve cooperative behavior, but does not allow direct inference about efficiency effects. The absence of efficiency or payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Sun, C. B., & Luo, C. (2020). Co-evolution of influence-based preferential selection and limited resource with multi-games on interdependent networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that female water point leaders (WPLs) in rural Namibia are younger and better educated than male WPLs, but are often related to traditional elites. In a trust game with third-party punishment, female WPLs punish less frequently (not statistically significant) and significantly less intensively than male WPLs. However, these differences in punishment behavior do not translate into differences in villager satisfaction with leaders. The experiment does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on gender differences in third-party punishment in a trust game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It shows that female leaders punish less, but this does not affect satisfaction. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design and findings may be useful for understanding how leader characteristics affect norm enforcement, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Berenji, B., Chou, T., & D'Orsogna, M. R. (2014). Recidivism and Rehabilitation of Criminal Offenders: A Carrot and Stick Evolutionary Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that neither punishment alone nor rehabilitation alone is optimal for reducing crime and recidivism. Instead, the best outcomes (highest ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) are achieved by balancing punishment severity and rehabilitation resources, subject to a fixed total resource constraint. There is an optimal allocation of resources between punishment and rehabilitation, and both too little and too much of either can worsen outcomes. The model also shows that the duration and intensity of rehabilitation matter, with intermediate durations being most effective. These findings are robust across different initial conditions and parameter values. However, the model does not report efficiency or group payoff in the sense of public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes (here, the ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) depends on the balance between punishment and rehabilitation, and that both are needed for optimal outcomes. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct predictive value for efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that punishment's effect is non-monotonic and context-dependent, especially when resources are constrained and when rehabilitation is possible. The findings suggest that in analogous PGG settings, enabling punishment without considering complementary positive interventions or resource tradeoffs may not maximize efficiency.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Gurven, M., & Tracer, D. (2011). The 'spiteful' origins of human cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that: (1) Small-scale societies are less generous in the Dictator Game and less likely to engage in third-party punishment, but are equally likely to engage in second-party punishment (Ultimatum Game) as large societies. (2) In large societies, third-party punishment is more common and even exceeds second-party punishment, suggesting a greater role for norm enforcement. (3) Generosity and punishment behaviors are correlated across games and societies. The findings challenge the view that strong reciprocity (including third-party punishment) is universal, suggesting that 'spiteful' second-party punishment is more fundamental in small-scale societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not use a PGG or report efficiency-related outcomes. However, it provides informative context about the prevalence and type of punishment (second- vs third-party) across societies, which may be relevant for understanding the social and cultural moderators of punishment effects in related game environments. The results suggest that the willingness to punish (especially second-party) is widespread, but the emergence of third-party punishment and higher generosity is more characteristic of large, complex societies. For prediction tasks, this implies that the social context (e.g., group size, societal complexity) may moderate the type and frequency of punishment, but the direct impact on efficiency in PGG-like games is not addressed.

- source: Schildberg-Hörisch, H., & Strassmair, C. (2012). An Experimental Test of the Deterrence Hypothesis. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that introducing small or intermediate punishment incentives can increase the amount stolen compared to no punishment, contrary to the deterrence hypothesis. Only very strong punishment incentives reduce stealing. The effect is driven by heterogeneity: 'selfish' subjects respond to incentives as predicted, but 'fair-minded' subjects increase stealing when incentives are introduced, possibly due to crowding out of fairness. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, as the design is not a public goods game but a dyadic stealing game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on norm-violating behavior (stealing) is non-monotonic: small and intermediate punishments can increase violations, while only strong punishments deter. However, because the design is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main lesson is that punishment can backfire under some conditions, especially when intrinsic motivation is present, but the mapping to efficiency outcomes in multi-player PGGs is indirect.

- source: Lo Gerfo, E., Gallucci, A., Morese, R., Vergallito, A., Ottone, S., Ponzano, F., Locatelli, G., Bosco, F., & Lauro, L. J. R. (2019). The role of ventromedial prefrontal cortex and temporo-parietal junction in third-party punishment behavior. *Neuroimage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that modulating neural activity in the VMPFC increases the tendency to punish unfair behavior (altruistic punishment), while modulating the TPJ increases the tendency to punish fair behavior (antisocial punishment) in a third-party punishment game. The behavioral outcomes are measured as the decision to punish and the amount of punishment, not as group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report on the effect of punishment on group welfare, efficiency, or aggregate earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are limited to individual punishment behavior in a third-party context, with no analysis of how punishment affects group outcomes or efficiency. The game is a modified Dictator Game, not a standard PGG or repeated PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as context for the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior.

- source: Ouss, A., & Peysakhovich, A. (2015). When Punishment Doesn't Pay: Cold Glow and Decisions to Punish. *Journal of Law & Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across a series of lab experiments, the authors show that individual punishment decisions are driven by 'cold glow' (private satisfaction from punishing) rather than by optimizing social efficiency. When punishment costs are shared by the group, individuals assign higher punishments than when they pay the cost themselves, resulting in over-punishment and excessive social cost. When the probability of apprehension is low, punishers do not increase punishment to compensate, so deterrence is insufficient and social efficiency is not achieved. When multiple punishers can assign punishment, their decisions are not crowded out by others' punishments, leading to excessive total punishment. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, taking behavior), but the implications for efficiency are discussed in terms of deviation from optimal deterrence and social cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on institutional details: (1) If punishment costs are externalized (group pays), enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to over-punishment and excessive social cost. (2) If the probability of apprehension is low, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency because punishers do not adjust their behavior, leading to under-deterrence. (3) When multiple punishers are present, lack of crowding out can lead to excessive total punishment and lower efficiency. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the effect is moderated by cost structure, probability of apprehension, and the number of punishers. For prediction, the presence of group-funded punishment, low apprehension probability, or multiple punishers should be expected to reduce or even reverse the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Conte, R., & Sabater-Mir, J. (2015). Self-Policing Through Norm Internalization: A Cognitive Solution to the Tragedy of the Digital Commons in Social Networks. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that agents capable of norm internalization (EMIL-I-A) achieve higher levels of cooperation (as measured by successful service transactions) than purely self-interested agents (IUMA) in a digital commons scenario. Internalizers flexibly adapt to environmental changes, internalizing and de-internalizing norms as conditions shift. The presence of punishment and sanction mechanisms supports norm salience and internalization, but the main driver of improved cooperation is internalization rather than external punishment alone. The model demonstrates that norm internalization can enable self-policing and robust cooperation in dynamic, decentralized environments. However, the outcomes are not reported in terms of group efficiency or total payoff as in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment and especially norm internalization mechanisms can increase cooperation and successful collective action in social dilemma-like environments. However, because the scenario is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that when agents internalize norms (potentially supported by punishment/sanction), cooperation is more robust and adaptive, but the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not isolated or quantified. The model's focus on norm salience and internalization may inform predictions about environments where norm-based motivations are strong, but it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Gambetta, D., & Przepiorka, W. (2019). Sharing Compromising Information as a Cooperative Strategy. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when a punishment mechanism is available (i.e., when norm-enforcement is possible), cooperation among less cooperative types ('hawks') increases, especially if both parties can mutually reveal their compromising information and thus make themselves vulnerable to punishment. In conditions without punishment, hawks rarely cooperate with each other. The effect is strongest when both the revelation of compromising information and the ability to punish are present and mutually chosen. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, defection, information sharing), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment opportunities can enable cooperation among otherwise uncooperative individuals, but the paper does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a trust-game-like environment can increase cooperation among less cooperative individuals, especially when mutual vulnerability is possible. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as indirect support for the prediction task.
