The game is a one-shot trust game variant, not a standard repeated public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured. The punishment mechanism is costly and only available in certain conditions. The results suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains (if any) is not reported.

- source: Wu, Y., Zhang, B., & Zhang, S. H. (2017). Probabilistic reward or punishment promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The introduction of a third-party agent who probabilistically rewards cooperators and punishes defectors increases the fraction of cooperators in both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games across different network structures. The effect is stronger as the reward/punishment magnitude increases and as the probability of encountering the third-party agent increases. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The results are robust across square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, and scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that probabilistic punishment (and/or reward) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, but it does not report on efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game, but a two-player prisoner's dilemma or snowdrift game, so direct prediction of efficiency in PGGs is not possible. The findings may be useful for understanding the general effect of third-party sanctions on cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2018). Heterogeneous groups overcome the diffusion of responsibility problem in social norm enforcement. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that diffusion of responsibility (lower individual sanctioning rates as group size increases) is present in symmetric groups but is largely overcome in heterogeneous groups where one member has a lower cost of sanctioning. Over repeated rounds, groups tacitly coordinate on the strong member to sanction, leading to higher and more efficient norm enforcement. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (sanctioning rates), not payoff-based efficiency or group welfare. The findings suggest that group heterogeneity can mitigate the bystander effect in norm enforcement, but do not provide direct evidence on how this affects group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative behavioral evidence about how group size and heterogeneity in punishment cost affect the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement in a volunteer's dilemma context. However, it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings suggest that introducing heterogeneity in punishment cost can increase the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement, especially in larger groups, but the translation to efficiency or welfare outcomes in standard PGGs is indirect.

- source: Vukov, J., Pinheiro, F. L., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). Reward from Punishment Does Not Emerge at All Costs. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured (scale-free network) populations, the introduction of peer punishment does not always promote cooperation. When the cost-to-impact ratio (q/p) is high, punishment can actually reduce the prevalence of cooperation compared to the no-punishment baseline. Only when the cost of punishing is sufficiently low relative to its impact does punishment enhance cooperation. The effect is mapped across a range of game parameters (T, S) and punishment parameters (p, q), showing regions where punishment is beneficial or detrimental for cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, but shows that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, on efficiency in adjacent games) is highly sensitive to the cost-effectiveness of punishment (q/p) and to the network structure. In structured populations, punishment can backfire unless costs are low. For prediction, this suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games may be negative or mixed if punishment is costly relative to its impact, especially in networked or heterogeneous settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task.

- source: Zhu, W. Q., Pan, Q. H., Song, S., & He, M. F. (2023). Effects of exposure-based reward and punishment on the evolution of cooperation in prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an infinite well-mixed population, exposure-based punishment (and especially the combination of reward and punishment) can promote the evolution of cooperation, but only if the cost of exposure is less than the punishment for defection. Rewarding exposed cooperators alone is insufficient to promote cooperation. There are threshold and nonlinear effects: simultaneous reward and punishment is most effective, and there are optimal values for exposure cost and reward/punishment strength. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation can be sustained, but all results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment (and especially combined with reward) can increase cooperation rates in a prisoner's dilemma setting with exposure-based mechanisms. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and focuses on cooperation rates, so its main value is as indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation under certain cost/benefit conditions. It does not provide quantitative predictions for efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Jia, C., Zhang, R. X., & Wang, D. (2023). Evolutionary game of cooperative behavior among social capitals in PPP projects: A complex network perspective. *Ain Shams Engineering Journal*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that increasing the default penalty (punishment) leads to higher rates of cooperation among social capitals in a networked prisoner's dilemma setting. Higher network density reduces cooperation, and initial cooperation probability can promote cooperation only when the temptation to defect (betrayal income) is not too high. The results are based on simulation of evolutionary dynamics, not empirical data. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that cooperation is important for the efficiency of PPP projects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that introducing or increasing punishment in a networked prisoner's dilemma can increase cooperation rates, which the authors argue is beneficial for project efficiency. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report results in terms of efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for understanding the behavioral mechanism (cooperation) rather than direct prediction of efficiency outcomes. The model's focus on network structure, punishment, and cooperation may inform qualitative expectations about the direction of efficiency effects when punishment is enabled, but does not provide direct predictive data for the downstream task.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Female cleaner fish cooperate more with unfamiliar males. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that female cleaner fish behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) when paired with unfamiliar males, who are more aggressive in punishing cheating than familiar males. Preventing male punishment reduces female cooperation. The effect of punishment on female cheating is short-lived, with no significant long-term effect. Male cooperative behavior is not affected by partner familiarity, likely because females do not punish males. The results suggest that in asymmetric interactions, harsher punishment from unfamiliar partners can induce greater cooperation from subordinates, even when familiarity would otherwise be expected to promote cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral proxies for cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and severity of punishment can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric dyadic interactions, even when the punisher is unfamiliar. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment increases cooperation, especially in settings with asymmetric power and punishment ability. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that enabling punishment (especially effective, low-cost punishment by dominants) can increase cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the impact on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Hu, K. P., Tao, Y. W., Ma, Y. J., & Shi, L. (2021). Peer pressure induced punishment resolves social dilemma on interdependent networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a peer-pressure-like punishment mechanism—where players with inconsistent strategies across interdependent network layers have their payoffs reduced—can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment maximizes cooperation, while too little or too much punishment is less effective. The effect is robust to changes in network structure (lattice, small-world, random) and the degree of payoff interdependence between layers. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; the main focus is on cooperation rates and the spatial distribution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, especially when the punishment is moderate. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that moderate punishment is likely to be most effective for promoting cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. The design dimensions most relevant here are the punishment cost (modeled as a reduction in payoff for inconsistent pairs) and the degree of interdependence between players' payoffs. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, X., & Cao, L. (2009). Largest Laplacian eigenvalue predicts the emergence of costly punishment in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks, there is a critical value of the population's selfishness degree (the share of the resource claimed by the proposer) above which a fraction of players become pure costly punishers (always reject offers). This critical value is analytically predicted by the inverse of the largest Laplacian eigenvalue of the network. The emergence of costly punishment is thus determined by network topology. The study also shows that pure punishers can have a higher average payoff ratio than others, especially as selfishness increases, but group-level efficiency or welfare is not directly analyzed. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations on various network types (scale-free, small-world, real-world collaboration networks).
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it studies the ultimatum game (an adjacent but distinct paradigm) and focuses on the emergence of costly punishment rather than on group efficiency or total payoff. The main outcome is the fraction of pure punishers as a function of network structure and selfishness degree. While the paper provides insight into how network topology affects the threshold for costly punishment, it does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare in a public-goods-like environment. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to theoretical context and mechanism discussion.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., & Fu, F. (2018). Strategy intervention for the evolution of fairness. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of fairness in the Ultimatum Game is promoted by the presence of spiteful strategies (which reject low or unequal offers, i.e., costly punishment) and inhibited by altruistic strategies (which accept any offer). The introduction of non-monotonic rejection (rejecting both low and high offers) further helps fairness overcome selfishness. In group-structured populations, larger population size increases the frequency of altruism and decreases the frequency of selfishness and spitefulness, but does not change the dominance of fairness. Migration inhibits the evolution of selfishness and fairness, but promotes altruism and, with sufficient migration, spitefulness. Mutation has non-monotonic effects, with intermediate mutation maximizing fairness and selfishness, and minimizing spitefulness. The main dependent variable is the frequency of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of costly punishment (rejection) in promoting fairness in strategic environments similar to the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it supports the idea that the availability of costly punishment can increase the prevalence of fairness-oriented behavior, especially when non-monotonic punishment is possible. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment mechanisms affect the evolutionary stability of fairness, not for predicting group efficiency outcomes.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Pinto, A. I., Grutter, A. S., Wismer, S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Male cleaner wrasses adjust punishment of female partners according to the stakes. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that male cleaner wrasses adjust the severity of punishment towards female partners based on the stakes of the cheating event (client value) and the relative size of the female. More severe punishment is delivered when cheating is more costly to the male (high-value client) and when the female is similar in size to the male. In turn, females respond to more severe punishment by behaving more cooperatively (cheating less in subsequent opportunities). The results support the idea that punishment can be flexibly adjusted to circumstances and that such flexibility can influence cooperative behavior. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be flexibly and contextually applied in cooperative dilemmas, and that increased punishment can reduce cheating and increase cooperative behavior. However, because the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot directly inform quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms and moderators (e.g., cost of cheating, partner asymmetry) that may influence the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Traxler, C., & Winter, J. (2012). Survey evidence on conditional norm enforcement. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reputation can sustain norm compliance, but their roles differ depending on the distribution of social motivation in the population. When reputation effects are weak, higher levels of punishment are needed to enforce the norm. When reputation effects are strong, norms can be sustained with little or no punishment. The model shows two stable equilibria: one with high compliance (if initial compliance is above a critical threshold) and one with low compliance (if initial compliance is below the threshold). The probability of punishment increases as the benefit-cost ratio for punishers decreases (i.e., as the psychological cost of punishing decreases). The interaction of punishment and reputation provides a robust mechanism for norm stability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can increase norm compliance, especially when reputation effects are weak. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify the effects of game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or number of rounds. The model is adjacent to public goods games but is not directly parameterized in terms of efficiency. For prediction tasks, the main insight is that punishment is more effective at sustaining compliance when reputation incentives are weak, and that the two mechanisms can substitute for each other to some extent. The paper does not inform how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in public goods games with specific design features.

- source: Roy, S., Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Sar, G. K., Banerjee, J., Rakshit, B., Mali, P. C., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2023). Time delays shape the eco-evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the inclusion of punishment as a strategy can, depending on parameter values (notably the cost of punishment and the temptation to defect), lead to the coexistence of punishers and defectors, dominance of punishers, or extinction of all strategies. Time delay can induce oscillatory or chaotic dynamics, and the ecological 'free space' variable can support the survival of cooperation-related strategies. The fate of cooperation, punishment, and defection is highly sensitive to parameter values and initial conditions. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the population dynamics of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment, its cost, and related parameters affect the prevalence of cooperation and punishment in a prisoner's dilemma context with eco-evolutionary feedbacks and time delay. However, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the conditions under which punishment can suppress defection or support cooperation, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, H. Y. (2017). Evolutionary Game Analysis of Emergency Management of the Middle Route of South-to-North Water Diversion Project. *Water Resources Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Theoretical and numerical analysis show that: (1) Local governments are more likely to show strong willingness to implement emergency policies if the profit from doing so exceeds the increased cost; (2) To avoid all local governments selecting weak cooperation, the central authority (MWR) should increase penalties for weak willingness, making the penalty greater than the cost saved by weak cooperation. The model demonstrates how the strength of punishment and the cost/benefit structure affect the evolution of cooperation, but does not directly quantify efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment (penalties) for non-cooperation can increase the willingness to cooperate in multi-agent settings similar to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings are most useful for understanding the mechanism by which punishment can shift equilibrium behavior toward greater cooperation, which may be associated with higher efficiency in analogous settings. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that higher punishment effectiveness and costliness of non-cooperation can promote cooperative equilibria, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency change.

- source: Li, K., Szolnoki, A., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). The coevolution of overconfidence and bluffing in the resource competition game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that in the absence of punishment, both overconfidence and bluffing evolve to high levels, with bluffing generally higher than overconfidence. Introducing punishment (with probability p) for revealed deception reduces both overconfidence and bluffing, with overconfidence being more sensitive to punishment. The structure of the interaction network (degree, heterogeneity) modulates these effects: higher degree or more heterogeneous networks promote bluffing and can make punishment less effective. System bias (delta) that increases conflict frequency also enhances the effectiveness of punishment. Players with higher real ability tend to evolve higher overconfidence and bluffing. The paper provides some analysis of average payoff for punished vs. non-punished players, showing that those with lower ability and lower payoff are punished more often, but does not report group-level efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and probability of punishment can reduce the prevalence of overconfidence and bluffing in resource competition games, especially overconfidence. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (deception levels) and not group efficiency or total payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games is limited. The model is adjacent to PGGs: it is a resource competition game with pairwise contests, not a standard PGG. The punishment mechanism is modeled as a reduction in deception traits rather than direct payoff reduction. The paper does provide some subgroup payoff analysis, suggesting that punishment targets and reduces the payoff of lower-ability, higher-deception individuals, but does not quantify overall group efficiency. Thus, the main decision support is qualitative: punishment reduces harmful behavioral biases, and its effectiveness depends on network structure and conflict frequency, but the effect on group efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Molho, C., & Wu, J. H. (2021). Direct punishment and indirect reputation-based tactics to intervene against offences. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that both direct punishment and indirect, reputation-based tactics (gossip, ostracism) are important for maintaining cooperation and enforcing norms. Direct punishment is more effective at recalibrating offenders' behavior but is costly and risky, while indirect tactics are less costly and reduce retaliation risk but are less effective at changing behavior. The choice of tactic depends on factors such as the self-relevance of the offense, relationship to the offender, power dynamics, and emotional responses (anger vs. disgust). The paper highlights that indirect tactics are more prevalent in real-world settings than direct punishment as operationalized in laboratory public goods games. It also notes that punishment can sometimes reduce efficiency, and that the ecological validity of lab findings is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers theoretical context suggesting that the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment depend on its cost, the risk of retaliation, and the availability of alternative (indirect) sanctioning mechanisms. It cautions that lab-based punishment games may overestimate the prevalence and impact of direct punishment compared to real-world settings, where indirect tactics are more common. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of communication, relationship context, and the type of punishment available may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, but it does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2010). Self-Serving Punishment of a Common Enemy Creates a Public Good in Reef Fishes. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that (1) punishment by victim fish (chasing the blenny) reduces the likelihood of being attacked again, (2) in shoaling species, this punishment increases the probability that the blenny switches to another species, creating a public good for the group, and (3) blennies can learn to discriminate between punishers and nonpunishers, which selects against free riding. The findings are based on both field observations and controlled laboratory experiments using artificial plates to simulate fish. The outcomes are behavioral and do not include direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, but the results are relevant to understanding how punishment can create public goods in repeated natural interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, naturalistic interactions, self-serving punishment can create public good benefits for a group, and that the ability of the 'cheater' (blenny) to identify and target nonpunishers can sustain punishment even in the absence of kin structure. However, the study does not use a formal public goods game, does not manipulate standard game design dimensions (e.g., MPCR, player count, explicit payoff structure), and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms are informative for understanding the conditions under which punishment can sustain cooperation and public goods, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency in laboratory public goods games with or without punishment. The main value is in highlighting the importance of repeated interactions, individual identification, and self-serving motives in the emergence of public goods via punishment.

- source: Sripada, C. S. (2005). Punishment and the strategic structure of moral systems. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment is a more general and empirically plausible mechanism for sustaining compliance with moral norms than reciprocity. Punishment can support cooperation in large groups and can enforce arbitrary rules, not just those with a cooperative payoff structure. The author reviews theoretical models (e.g., Boyd and Richerson 1992) and ethnographic/experimental evidence to support the claim that punishment, including direct, exclusion-based, and reputation-based forms, is universal in human groups. The paper also discusses the problem of stabilizing costly punishment and the implications for understanding moral diversity. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the importance of punishment mechanisms in sustaining cooperation and compliance in public-goods-like environments, especially in large groups. It suggests that, in theory, enabling punishment should increase compliance and cooperation, and thus could improve efficiency, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes. The arguments are useful for understanding why punishment may be effective, but the paper does not supply empirical data or parameterized results that can be directly used for predicting efficiency changes in experimental public goods games. Its value is mainly in supporting the general plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation and norm compliance, especially in large groups.

- source: Fitouchi, L., & Singh, M. (2023). Punitive justice serves to restore reciprocal cooperation in three small-scale societies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three small-scale societies, punitive justice is primarily restorative and dyadic, not group-norm-enforcing. Third-party punishment is rare, and most interventions are aimed at mediating disputes and restoring relationships. Compensation to victims is common and often proportional to harm, especially among the Mentawai and Nuer. Kinship and interdependence reduce the likelihood or magnitude of punishment/compensation. When costs are not imposed on offenders, cooperation often breaks down and feuding may result. The paper does not use public goods games or measure efficiency, but provides evidence that punishment and compensation are more about restoring dyadic cooperation than increasing group-level efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the function of punishment in environments that are adjacent to public goods games: real-world small-scale societies with reciprocal obligations and norm violations. It suggests that, in such contexts, punishment is not primarily about increasing group efficiency or enforcing group-level cooperation, but about restoring dyadic relationships and preventing escalation. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment may depend on whether the context is more about dyadic reciprocity or group-level cooperation. However, because the paper does not use experimental PGGs or report efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGGs is limited.

- source: Phillips, T. (2018). The concepts of asymmetric and symmetric power can help resolve the puzzle of altruistic and cooperative behaviour. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness and function of punishment in promoting cooperation depend critically on the symmetry or asymmetry of power among individuals. In asymmetric power settings (e.g., dominance hierarchies), punishment is typically used by dominants to coerce subordinates, not to enforce reciprocity or group-beneficial cooperation. In symmetric power settings, punishment can be counteracted by retaliation, and cooperation may break down unless specific conditions are met (e.g., territoriality, exit options). The paper reviews evidence that punishment often reduces group payoffs in experimental public goods games, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is crucial for its effectiveness. However, it does not provide new quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments, especially regarding the role of power asymmetries. It suggests that punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency in settings with strong power asymmetries, as it is used for coercion rather than cooperation enforcement. In symmetric power settings, punishment may also fail to improve efficiency due to retaliation and the tragedy of the commons. The paper highlights the importance of considering power relations, cost-to-impact ratios, and the possibility of retaliation when predicting the efficiency effects of punishment. However, it does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes, so its value is mainly in informing the interpretation of empirical results and the design of experiments.

- source: Nakao, H., & Machery, E. (2012). The evolution of punishment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment is widespread in nature and often evolves for reasons other than inducing behavioral change in the punishee. In many cases (especially among plants and insects), punishment is a loss-cutting or cost-imposing strategy, not a behavior-modification strategy. Even among vertebrates, where behavioral modification is more common, the evolutionary benefits of punishment often do not depend on the punishee changing its behavior. The paper challenges the view that punishment primarily evolved to promote cooperation or efficiency, and instead highlights alternative evolutionary pathways.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative data relevant to predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. It does, however, offer theoretical context suggesting that the evolutionary function of punishment may not always be to increase cooperation or group efficiency, but could instead be to reduce harm to the punisher or its kin. For the prediction task, this paper suggests caution in assuming that punishment mechanisms will always improve efficiency or cooperation, especially in settings where behavioral modification is unlikely or where punishment serves other evolutionary functions. However, it does not provide empirical or experimental data to calibrate such predictions.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Li, Y., Fermin, A. S. R., Kanai, R., Takagishi, H., Matsumoto, Y., Kiyonari, T., & Sakagami, M. (2017). Behavioural Differences and Neural Substrates of Altruistic and Spiteful Punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals who engage in costly punishment in the TPPG (altruistic punishers) also behave more pro-socially in a variety of non-strategic games, while those who only reject unfair offers in the UG (spiteful punishers) behave more selfishly. The distinction is supported by neural data: spiteful punishers have larger left caudate nucleus and nucleus accumbens volumes. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in a public goods or social dilemma context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or social dilemma games. It does, however, suggest that the motivations behind punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) are heterogeneous and that not all punishment is associated with pro-social or efficiency-enhancing behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always increase efficiency, depending on the underlying motives of punishers, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Thielmann, I., & Hilbig, B. E. (2018). Daring dishonesty: On the role of sanctions for (un)ethical behavior. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that increasing the probability of being checked for dishonesty and increasing the monetary penalty for being caught both substantially reduce the rate of cheating in individual tasks. In Experiment 1, tripling the probability of being checked (from 16.7% to 50%) nearly halved the estimated cheating rate. In Experiment 2, increasing both the probability of being checked and the penalty for being caught further reduced cheating, with the lowest dishonesty observed when both risk and severity were high. The results support utility-based theories of dishonesty, showing that external costs (sanctions) deter unethical behavior. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a public goods game or a direct analog.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the risk and severity of monetary sanctions reduces individual cheating in private reporting tasks. While the findings are highly relevant for understanding the deterrent effect of punishment on norm violations, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. The results may be indirectly informative for predicting that punishment mechanisms with higher detection probability and higher penalty severity are more likely to reduce norm violations, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited by the lack of group interaction, contribution, or payoff aggregation in the study.

- source: Brüne, M., Scheele, D., Heinisch, C., Tas, C., Wischniewski, J., & Güntürkün, O. (2012). Empathy Moderates the Effect of Repetitive Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation of the Right Dorsolateral Prefrontal Cortex on Costly Punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases the amount of costly punishment assigned by third-party observers in a Dictator Game with punishment option. This effect is significantly moderated by individual differences in empathy: the increase in punishment is stronger for individuals with lower empathy scores. No significant effect is found for left DLPFC stimulation. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not use a public goods game or a direct variant, but rather a third-party punishment Dictator Game. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural and psychological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms that may underlie costly punishment, showing that punishment can be increased by disrupting cognitive control (right DLPFC) and that this effect is moderated by empathy. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or group payoff, this paper is only weakly relevant, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Deutchman, P., Bracic, M., Raihani, N., & McAuliffe, K. (2021). Punishment is strongly motivated by revenge and weakly motivated by inequity aversion. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment is primarily motivated by revenge (reciprocating losses), but disadvantageous inequity also motivates punishment, even in the absence of loss. The effect of inequity aversion is weaker than the effect of loss. The design manipulates whether the partner can steal from or augment the responder's payoff, and the resulting payoff differences. Punishment is most frequent when the partner steals, especially if this creates disadvantageous inequity. There is also some punishment when the partner does not augment, if this results in disadvantageous inequity. The study does not measure or report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological motivations for punishment in two-player games, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. However, it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used when losses or disadvantageous inequity are present, and that the cost and effectiveness of punishment can moderate its use. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not provide data on group efficiency or welfare with or without punishment.

- source: Kurz, T., Thomas, W. E., & Fonseca, M. A. (2014). A fine is a more effective financial deterrent when framed retributively and extracted publicly. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The field experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of financial punishment in deterring undesired behavior (lateness) depends strongly on both the framing (retributive vs. compensatory) and the public/private nature of the sanction. Retributive and public fines significantly increase punctuality compared to compensatory or private fines, which are no more effective than no fine. A follow-up scenario study shows that compensatory fines reduce the perceived immorality of transgression, while retributive fines do not. The results support a theory that the moral framing and public visibility of punishment are key moderators of its behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the framing and public administration of punishment can substantially moderate its behavioral effectiveness, even when the material cost is held constant. While the context is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in collective action settings, punishment will be more effective at promoting cooperation if it is framed as retributive (moral) and administered publicly. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible. The results are most useful for informing the design of punishment mechanisms (e.g., public vs. private, moral vs. compensatory framing) rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Price, M. E. (2005). Punitive sentiment among the Shuar and in industrialized societies: cross-cultural similarities. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that among the Shuar, as in industrialized societies, higher contributors to collective action (mingas) express stronger punitive sentiment toward free riders, and that only intentional absences (free riding) are considered deserving of punishment. The main dependent variable is the amount of fine each member thinks should be imposed for unexcused absences. There is a strong positive correlation between contribution (attendance) and punitive sentiment. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it provides cross-cultural evidence that punitive sentiment toward free riders is a robust feature of collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, support the generalizability of punitive sentiment toward free riders across cultures and suggests that the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment are similar in small-scale and industrialized societies. For prediction tasks, this paper is useful for understanding the universality of punitive motivations, but not for quantifying efficiency effects or moderating variables. It does not provide parameter values or outcome data for the key game design dimensions relevant to efficiency prediction.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Berger, J. (2016). The Sanctioning Dilemma: A Quasi-Experiment on Social Norm Enforcement in the Train. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that peer-sanctioning (negative sanctioning of a norm violator) occurs in about 50% of interventions. Contrary to the classic diffusion of responsibility hypothesis, the likelihood of sanctioning increases with the number of passengers present. Passengers who are closer to the norm breaker and those whose activities require silence are more likely to sanction. The likelihood of sanctioning decreases as the train approaches its destination. No significant difference in sanctioning rates is found between silent-area and non-silent-area train cars. The study does not measure or report any payoff, efficiency, or group welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it is conceptually adjacent—framing norm enforcement as a second-order public good and using volunteer's dilemma theory—the outcomes are strictly behavioral (sanctioning occurrence and timing). There is no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, nor any manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related game design dimensions. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, though it may provide some indirect, conceptual support for the importance of heterogeneity and group size in norm enforcement contexts.

- source: Rosati, A. G., DiNicola, L. M., & Buckholtz, J. W. (2018). Chimpanzee Cooperation Is Fast and Independent From Self-Control. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees show a fast response bias for prosocial actions (including punishment of theft), but the frequency of such actions is modest and varies across individuals. More prosocial individuals are faster to act in both helping and punishment contexts. There is no evidence that self-control (temporal or motoric) predicts prosocial or punishment behavior. The three measured domains of prosociality (donation, helping, punishment) are uncorrelated, suggesting distinct underlying mechanisms. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design is adjacent to PGGs, using individual-level donation, helping, and punishment tasks with chimpanzees, but not repeated group games or explicit payoff structures. The main relevance is in showing that punishment behavior exists and is associated with faster response times in chimpanzees, but not with self-control. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, weak support: it suggests that punishment can be a salient behavioral response, but does not inform whether it increases or decreases group efficiency.

- source: Mathew, S. (2017). How the Second-Order Free Rider Problem Is Solved in a Small-Scale Society. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana adults express strong disapproval toward second-order free riders (those who do not punish norm violators), as well as toward those who punish excessively, unjustly, or without proper group consensus. Punishment is seen as legitimate only when collectively decided and administered by a preordained group. These meta-norms help regulate peer punishment and may solve the second-order free rider problem in this society. However, the evidence is based on responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that strong social norms and meta-norms can support the legitimacy and regulation of peer punishment in a real-world society, potentially solving the second-order free rider problem. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual economic outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of meta-norms and collective legitimacy for the functioning of peer punishment systems.

- source: Dimant, E., & Gesche, T. (2023). Nudging enforcers: how norm perceptions and motives for lying shape sanctions. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment increases with the size of the lie and is harsher when the lie increases inequity or is for personal gain, compared to when it restores equity. Norm-nudges (empirical or normative information about others' behavior or approval) increase punishment, especially when preexisting norms are ambiguous. The effect of norm-nudges is strongest in scenarios where the social norm is less clear. These findings are robust across a behavioral experiment, a vignette experiment, and a norm elicitation study. However, the study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how punishment behavior is shaped by norm-nudges and the context of norm violations, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it shows that punishment is sensitive to context and can be increased by norm-nudges, especially when norms are ambiguous. However, it does not provide evidence on whether or how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Thus, it should be used as indirect evidence about the determinants of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Martin, J. W., Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., & Cushman, F. (2019). When do we punish people who don't?. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that people are more likely to punish (or endorse punishing) third-party observers who fail to punish norm violators than second-party victims who fail to punish. This pattern is robust across economic games and vignettes, and holds for both severe and non-criminal norm violations. The relationship between the third party and the victim modulates HOP: authorities are punished most for failing to punish, followed by friends, then strangers. The study does not examine or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its outcomes are exclusively about the social psychology of higher-order punishment—when people punish non-punishers—not about the consequences of punishment for cooperation, contribution, or efficiency. The economic game used is not a public goods game or a close variant, and no group-level payoff or efficiency measures are reported. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Eriksson, K., Andersson, P. A., & Strimling, P. (2016). Moderators of the disapproval of peer punishment. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment of norm violators is typically disapproved of by observers, especially when the punishment is severe or appears aggressive. This disapproval extends to viewing the punisher as a problem for the group and the act as inappropriate. Disapproval is moderated by the observer's own trait aggression (less disapproval among more aggressive raters), the severity of punishment (more severe = more disapproval), and whether the punishment is collective or individual (collective = slightly less disapproval). Economic sanctions are disapproved of less than physical ones, and unselfish economic sanctions (where the punisher does not profit) are disapproved of less than selfish ones. Verbal sanctions are also disapproved of, even when effective. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social approval and the perceived legitimacy of peer punishment, but not for predicting how punishment affects group efficiency or earnings. The design is adjacent to PGGs (common resource, norm violation, sanctioning), but the outcomes are purely attitudinal and perceptual. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency, though it may inform hypotheses about the social acceptability of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Gordon, D. S., Madden, J. R., & Lea, S. E. G. (2014). Both Loved and Feared: Third Party Punishers Are Viewed as Formidable and Likeable, but These Reputational Benefits May Only Be Open to Dominant Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that third party punishment is perceived as both a dominant and likable act, distinguishing it from other aggressive behaviors. However, only dominant individuals are seen as able to punish effectively and at low cost, with reduced risk of retaliation and greater ability to use non-violent (social) punishment. Subordinate individuals are perceived as less likely to succeed and at higher risk if they attempt punishment. The reputational benefits of punishment (increased likability and dominance) are thus mainly accessible to dominant individuals. These findings are based on observer judgments in hypothetical scenarios, not on actual behavior in economic games or real payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that heterogeneity in the cost and effectiveness of punishment (due to dominance/status) may be important moderators of the impact of punishment on cooperation and norm enforcement. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that the effectiveness and social acceptability of punishment may depend on the perceived status of the punisher, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., McAuliffe, W. H. B., Shah, Y., Tanaka, H., Ohtsubo, Y., & McCullough, M. E. (2020). When and Why Do Third Parties Punish Outside of the Lab? A Cross-Cultural Recall Study. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment in real-life conflicts is infrequent, especially when the third party does not value the welfare of the victim (i.e., low WTR). Intervention (helping the victim without punishing the transgressor) is more common than punishment. The main predictor of both punishment and intervention is the third party's WTR for the victim, with higher WTRs associated with greater likelihood of action. WTR for the transgressor predicts less anger but does not significantly predict intervention or punishment. The results are consistent across U.S. students, U.S. MTurkers, and Japanese students, though Japanese students intervene and punish less often. The study concludes that real-world third-party punishment is much less common than laboratory experiments suggest, likely due to the lack of anonymity, the availability of alternative responses, and the importance of social relationships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is highly informative about the external validity of laboratory punishment rates: it suggests that real-world third-party punishment is rare, especially among strangers, and that laboratory designs may overstate the prevalence and impact of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the view that punishment's effect on cooperation or efficiency in real-world or naturalistic settings may be much smaller than in lab-based PGGs, particularly when social distance is high and alternative responses are available. The findings highlight the importance of relationship context and available response options as moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Zinchenko, O. (2019). Brain responses to social punishment: a meta-analysis. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that social punishment tasks consistently activate the bilateral claustrum, right inferior frontal gyrus, and left superior frontal gyrus. These regions are associated with salience detection, executive function, and emotional empathy. The meta-analysis includes a range of paradigms (Ultimatum Game, Dictator Game, third-party punishment, social rejection), but does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or related economic outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural basis of social punishment but do not provide evidence on the effects of punishment on cooperation, efficiency, or payoff in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it confirms that social punishment is a robust neural phenomenon across several economic and social paradigms, it does not report or synthesize any data on group earnings, efficiency, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the impact of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments.

- source: Zhang, Y., & van der Schaar, M. (2013). Robust Reputation Protocol Design for Online Communities: A Stochastic Stability Analysis. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in a repeated random matching game (asymmetric gift-giving, not a standard PGG), a reputation protocol with probabilistic punishment (parameterized by punishment probability beta) can induce full cooperation and achieve maximal efficiency (social welfare = b-c) in the long run, provided the discount factor and punishment strength are within certain bounds. If punishment is too weak or too strong, efficiency is lower. Theoretical analysis is supported by numerical simulations showing that the optimal protocol achieves much higher average social welfare than direct reciprocity (tit-for-tat) or less robust reputation schemes. The protocol's design is robust to stochastic perturbations and ensures convergence to the efficient equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated random matching environments with binary contribution (gift-giving), the introduction of a well-calibrated reputation-based punishment protocol can move the system from zero efficiency (no cooperation) to full efficiency (all cooperate, social welfare maximized). The effect is robust to noise and initial conditions, provided the punishment probability is neither too low nor too high. While the game is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure and explicit efficiency analysis make the results highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The findings suggest that, when punishment is enabled via a reputation protocol and properly tuned, efficiency can increase dramatically compared to the no-punishment baseline.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., & Sääksvuori, L. (2012). Communication in intergroup conflicts. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated intergroup contest game (two groups of four, 20 rounds), the opportunity for within-group peer punishment (REST+PUN) does not reduce group conflict expenditures compared to restricted communication without punishment (REST). Both treatments result in much higher conflict expenditures (i.e., lower efficiency) than the no-communication baseline. Open communication between groups, in contrast, dramatically reduces conflict expenditures and increases efficiency. Punishment is rarely used when communication is available, and its presence does not significantly affect group payoffs or efficiency. The main mechanism for efficiency gains is open communication and turn-taking, not punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in intergroup contest environments with intra-group punishment and communication, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency relative to communication alone. If the control (no-punishment) game has restricted communication, adding punishment does not increase efficiency. If open communication is possible, efficiency is much higher regardless of punishment. Thus, for prediction, the presence of peer punishment in this design should not be expected to increase efficiency, and may even be associated with high waste. The findings are most relevant for contest-like games with group-level competition and intra-group punishment, rather than standard linear PGGs.

- source: Hooper, P. L., Kaplan, H. S., & Jaeggi, A. V. (2021). Gains to cooperation drive the evolution of egalitarianism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that punishment of dominance (hawkishness) by withholding cooperation (defection in the PD game) can be evolutionarily stable and lead to egalitarian outcomes when the future benefits of cooperation outweigh the short-term gains from dominance. The efficiency of the group increases as leveller strategies proliferate, reducing the frequency of costly contests and increasing resource sharing. Simulations show that an ecological shift increasing the gains to cooperation leads to a transition from dominance/defection equilibria to egalitarian/cooperative equilibria with higher payoffs. Empirical analysis of forager societies finds that greater reliance on cooperative hunting predicts a higher probability of egalitarianism, consistent with the model's predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment (here, the ability to withhold cooperation from dominant individuals) increases group efficiency and resource equality, especially when the marginal benefit of cooperation is high and the probability of future interaction is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games but not an exact match; however, the logic and payoff structure are highly relevant. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that in repeated, high-mpcr, high-future-interaction environments, enabling punishment (even in the form of exclusion or shunning) will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the benefits of cooperation are high relative to the benefits of dominance or defection.

- source: Li, J. M., & Jiang, S. S. (2023). How can governance strategies be developed for marine ecological environment pollution caused by sea-using enterprises?-A study based on evolutionary game theory. *Ocean & Coastal Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of strong governance mechanisms (punishment, compensation, or high ecological performance appraisal), all parties tend to adopt minimal effort strategies, resulting in low efficiency and poor environmental outcomes (prisoner's dilemma). Introducing punishment (penalties for non-compliance, ecological damage compensation), increasing the weight of ecological indicators in local government performance appraisal, and combining these tools can shift the system to a more efficient equilibrium, where local governments and enterprises fully implement and comply with environmental policies, even if central government supervision is weak. The effect of any single tool is limited, but their combination is necessary to achieve high efficiency in governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms (penalties, compensation for damage) and reward mechanisms (performance appraisal) are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from introducing punishment in public-goods-like governance settings. The results suggest that, in adjacent multi-agent settings, punishment alone may not be sufficient, but when combined with other governance tools, it can substantially increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment, reward, and their combination should be considered as key moderators of treatment efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to weak incentives.

- source: Li, Q., Pi, B., Feng, M. Y., & Kurths, J. (2024). Open Data in the Digital Economy: An Evolutionary Game Theory Perspective. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of regulatory rewards/penalties (F) can shift the system toward cooperative equilibria, where all parties choose the socially desirable strategies (high-quality data provision, data acquisition, and regulation). The model provides explicit conditions under which full cooperation, partial cooperation, or defection equilibria are stable. Increasing the effectiveness or magnitude of regulatory punishment/reward expands the parameter space in which cooperation is stable. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium strategy fractions, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment/reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in multi-stakeholder environments similar to public goods games, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the strength or effectiveness of regulatory punishment/reward (analogous to punishment_tech and punishment_cost) can promote cooperative behavior among agents in a multi-stakeholder environment. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is informative for predicting that stronger, more effective punishment mechanisms are likely to increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not directly analyzed, the paper should be used as indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency, with caution regarding transfer to standard PGG settings.

- source: Truskanov, N., Emery, Y., & Bshary, R. (2020). Juvenile cleaner fish can socially learn the consequences of cheating. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Juvenile cleaner fish, after observing adults interacting with model clients (plates) that respond negatively to cheating (either by fleeing or punishing), subsequently behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) and prefer partners that are tolerant to cheating. Social learning about the consequences of cheating shapes cooperative strategies, but juveniles do not simply copy arbitrary preferences of adults. The study provides evidence that social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation in a non-human animal, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observation of punishment or negative partner responses can increase cooperative behavior in a dyadic animal model. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that the presence of observable sanctions can bias individuals toward more cooperative choices, which in analogous human or multi-player games could translate to higher efficiency if similar mechanisms operate. However, the lack of direct payoff or efficiency measurement, and the dyadic (not group) structure, limit direct transfer to the prediction of efficiency in public goods games with punishment. The main value is mechanistic: social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation.

- source: Jin, Q., Wang, Z., Wang, Z., & Wang, Y. L. (2012). Strategy changing penalty promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
