  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a strategy changing penalty (a form of endogenous punishment for switching strategies) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. Both higher penalty rates and longer memory (the period after which the penalty is lifted if the player does not change strategy again) monotonically promote cooperation, with the effect saturating at high memory. The mechanism works by inducing heterogeneity in fitness coefficients, which helps cooperator clusters survive and expand. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that endogenous penalty mechanisms can increase cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment is not peer-to-peer but is an automatic penalty for changing strategy. Thus, while the findings suggest that certain forms of punishment can promote cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The main value is as adjacent, mechanistic support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, especially when combined with memory or forgiveness.

- source: Bi, Y., & Yang, H. (2023). Based on reputation consistent strategy times promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner?s dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based warning/punishment mechanism—where players are less likely to imitate neighbors who have persistently defected—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG simulation. Stronger punishment (higher discount factor) and more timely punishment (lower threshold for triggering the discount) both further increase cooperation rates. The effect is robust across a range of temptation-to-defect parameters. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.

However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding mechanisms that increase cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction, as higher cooperation does not always translate to higher group payoff depending on the cost structure of punishment and the payoff matrix.

- source: Helbing, D., & Johansson, A. (2010). Cooperation, Norms, and Revolutions: A Unified Game-Theoretical Approach. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the dynamics and outcomes of cooperation and norm establishment in multi-population games depend critically on the payoff structure (game type), the relative power of populations, and initial conditions. In multi-population prisoner's dilemma games, defection dominates regardless of power. In stag hunt games, shared norms can emerge, often set by the more powerful or initially larger group, but sometimes by the weaker group depending on initial conditions. In snowdrift games, polarization and abrupt 'revolutionary' transitions can occur. Costly punishment can transform a prisoner's dilemma into a stag hunt, supporting norm establishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral equilibria and norm dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, payoff structure, and group power shape the emergence of cooperation and norms in environments related to, but not identical with, public goods games. It suggests that punishment can promote norm establishment by changing the effective game structure, but does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment can shift equilibria toward more cooperative outcomes in some settings, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of game structure and group asymmetries as moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Li, Q., Iqbal, A., Perc, M., Chen, M. Y., & Abbott, D. (2013). Coevolution of Quantum and Classical Strategies on Evolving Random Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that when agents can punish by reducing link weights, cooperation can dominate for a wider range of temptation values, especially when the network structure is updated frequently. Stronger punishment (lower minimum link weight) increases the range where cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, quantum strategies eventually dominate. The number of neighbors (network degree) has little effect if all agents have equal degree. The network evolves to have higher clustering and small-world properties. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about strategy prevalence, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (reducing link weights) on the prevalence of cooperation in an evolving network Prisoner's Dilemma. It suggests that stronger punishment and more frequent network updating support cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation under some conditions, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. The game is not a public goods game, but a networked PD with quantum strategies, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Gingins, S., Werminghausen, J., Johnstone, R. A., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2013). Power and temptation cause shifts between exploitation and cooperation in a cleaner wrasse mutualism. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: Cleaner wrasse strategically adjust their foraging behavior in response to the threat of early termination (sanction) and temptation to cheat. When the threat of early termination is high and the temptation to cheat is low, cleaners behave more cooperatively (eat more less-preferred items, which is analogous to providing better service). When the temptation to cheat is high and the threat is low, cleaners exploit the situation (eat more preferred items). The non-cleaner control species does not adjust its behavior in response to these manipulations. The study provides evidence that simple partner control mechanisms (sanctions) can maintain cooperation in asymmetric interactions, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the presence of a sanction mechanism (threat of early termination) can shift behavior from exploitation to cooperation, especially when the temptation to cheat is low. However, the outcomes are behavioral and not directly about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, enabling a sanction mechanism may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and whether the sanction is costly or not. The study is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which sanctions can promote cooperation, but less directly informative for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Mameli, M. (2013). Meat made us moral: a hypothesis on the nature and evolution of moral judgment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of moral judgment is rooted in emotional dispositions that support the enforcement of group-beneficial norms through punishment and meta-punishment. It draws on ethnographic and evolutionary accounts of hunter-gatherer societies, where collective punishment of cheaters and bullies (those who disrupt cooperation and resource sharing) is common and is enforced through both direct and reputational means (e.g., gossip, ostracism, disapproval). The author suggests that these mechanisms enabled stable cooperation in small groups, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than empirical or quantitative. The paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and meta-punishment mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in small, egalitarian groups, especially in contexts analogous to public goods dilemmas (e.g., meat sharing after cooperative hunting). However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with or without punishment. Its relevance is mainly in supporting the plausibility of punishment as a stabilizer of cooperation, especially when punishment is collective, reputational, and embedded in group norms. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in small, highly interactive, reputation-sensitive groups, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance.

- source: Romano, A., Saral, A. S., & Wu, J. H. (2022). Direct and indirect reciprocity among individuals and groups. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that group membership, gossip, and third-party punishment promote prosocial behavior through psychological mechanisms related to direct and indirect reciprocity. Third-party punishment is framed as a tool for upregulating reputation and deterring mistreatment, which in turn increases prosocial behavior among others. The review highlights that reputational concern and anticipation of future interaction are key drivers of both prosocial and punitive behaviors. However, the review does not report or synthesize results on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and focuses on behavioral and psychological outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment (especially third-party punishment) can increase prosocial behavior, but it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., reputation, observability, group membership), but does not inform direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as background on psychological mechanisms and as a pointer to empirical work, rather than as a source of quantitative or design-specific evidence.

- source: Barker, J. L., & Barclay, P. (2016). Local competition increases people's willingness to harm others. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that when competition is local (i.e., participants compete for prizes within small groups), people are more willing to pay a cost to harm others, resulting in lower group and individual payoffs (efficiency). In contrast, under global competition (competing against all participants), less money is spent on harming, and payoffs are higher. This effect holds whether endowments are earned or given. The findings are robust across three studies, including one where participants could harm both ingroup and outgroup members: under local competition, harming is targeted at ingroup members (the direct competitors), further reducing within-group efficiency. The results are interpreted as a 'tragedy of the commons' driven by local competition, where wasteful harming reduces overall welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in economic games where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs (money-burning), the structure of competition (local vs global) is a critical moderator of efficiency. When competition is local, efficiency is much lower due to increased costly harming, even when the cost-effectiveness of harming is high (burning $1 to reduce $4). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local competition (small group contests) should be expected to strongly reduce efficiency when punishment/harming is possible, compared to global competition. The results are robust to whether endowments are earned or given, and to whether harming is targeted at ingroup or outgroup members. However, the game is not a standard public goods game or punishment game: the main action is costly harming, not punishment for norm violation or contribution decisions. Still, the efficiency implications are directly relevant for settings where costly sanctions are possible.

- source: Bolton, G., Greiner, B., & Ockenfels, A. (2018). Dispute Resolution or Escalation? The Strategic Gaming of Feedback Withdrawal Options in Online Markets. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In laboratory markets with two-sided moral hazard (both buyer and seller can defect), the introduction of a mutual feedback withdrawal option leads to more strategic negative feedback, less honest feedback, and lower efficiency (measured as realized gains from trade divided by maximum possible gains). The feedback system becomes less informative, and trust/trustworthiness are reduced. In one-sided markets (only seller can defect), the feedback withdrawal option does not significantly affect efficiency or the informativeness of feedback. The negative effect of FBW on efficiency is thus conditional on the presence of two-sided moral hazard and mutual withdrawal. The paper also finds that some buyers engage in 'altruistic punishment' by refusing to withdraw negative feedback even at a cost to themselves, partially mitigating the detrimental effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with two-sided moral hazard and mutual punishment/retaliation options, introducing a mutual feedback withdrawal (punishment-like) mechanism reduces efficiency relative to a control without such a mechanism. The effect is not observed in one-sided environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of mutual, bilateral punishment/retaliation options in two-sided games is likely to reduce efficiency, even if the control game is already inefficient. The effect is mediated by strategic retaliation and the breakdown of reputation as an incentive for cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar two-sided, bilateral punishment/retaliation structures.

- source: Glover, J., & Kim, E. (2021). Optimal Team Composition: Diversity to Foster Implicit Team Incentives. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated team production, the efficiency of implicit team incentives (mutual monitoring and punishment for free-riding) depends critically on team composition and the expected duration of the relationship (discount factor). Diverse teams (with productive complementarity) always have a monotonic relationship between tenure and incentive efficiency: as tenure increases, the cost of providing incentives decreases, and collusion is not a concern. Specialized teams (with productive substitutability) can initially benefit from longer tenure (lower incentive costs), but as tenure increases further, collusion becomes a problem, raising the cost of incentives and reducing efficiency. The model provides explicit formulas and parameter thresholds for when each team type is optimal. The main efficiency outcome is the principal's expected payoff, which is maximized by choosing the team composition that best balances productive efficiency and incentive efficiency, given the risk of collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms (mutual monitoring, implicit threats) in repeated team production environments, especially as a function of team composition (diversity vs. specialization), the discount factor (tenure), and the production technology (complementarity vs. substitutability). While not a public goods game per se, the model is adjacent and highly relevant for settings where group payoff depends on repeated cooperation and the threat of future punishment. The results show that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether collusion is possible and on the structure of the team. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment (mutual monitoring) generally increases efficiency in diverse teams and in specialized teams with short tenure, but may reduce efficiency in specialized teams with long tenure due to collusion. The explicit formulas and parameter thresholds can inform quantitative predictions if the relevant parameters are known.

- source: Frean, M. R., & Abraham, E. R. (2004). Adaptation and enslavement in endosymbiont-host associations - art. no. 051913. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The main finding is that in a two-species repeated cooperation game with differential adaptation rates, the slower-adapting species (host) evolves to provide just enough cooperation to keep the faster-adapting species (symbiont) maximally cooperative. This 'miser-slave' dynamic leads to highly asymmetric payoffs: hosts receive nearly all the benefit, while symbionts receive very little, even though they are highly cooperative. The total group payoff (efficiency) is much less than the maximum possible (full mutual cooperation), because the host withholds cooperation. The model provides a theoretical explanation for observed asymmetries in natural symbioses, where hosts benefit much more than symbionts. The findings are robust across a range of cost-benefit ratios and are supported by both analytic and simulation results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical mechanism showing that, in repeated cooperation games with contingent strategies and differential adaptation rates, the introduction of a contingent response (analogous to punishment or reward) by one party can lead to highly asymmetric efficiency outcomes. Specifically, if the 'punishing' party adapts more slowly, it can extract maximal cooperation from the other party while contributing minimally itself, resulting in low overall efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the structure of contingent responses and adaptation rates can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, the model is not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implemented as a contingent reward/withholding rather than explicit costly punishment. Thus, while the findings are informative about the potential for punishment/contingency to reduce efficiency via asymmetry, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs with explicit punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Fonseca, M. A., & Peters, K. (2021). Is it costly to deceive? People are adept at detecting gossipers' lies but may not reward honesty. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Dishonest gossip reduces efficiency in trust-based interactions: participants who act on dishonest gossip (especially positive lies about untrustworthy agents or negative lies about trustworthy agents) earn lower payoffs than those who act on honest gossip. Participants can detect some dishonest gossip, especially after interacting with the target, and are more likely to trust gossipers they believe to be honest. However, the tendency to socially sanction dishonest gossipers (by reducing trust) is inconsistent and influenced by the valence of the gossip: gossipers who share negative information, even if honest, are trusted less. Thus, while social sanctions for dishonesty exist, they are imperfect and do not always align with actual honesty, limiting their effectiveness in promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the presence of dishonest communication in trust-based games reduces efficiency, as measured by participant payoffs. While not a public goods game, the trust game with gossip is adjacent and the mechanism—misleading information undermining cooperation—transfers to PGG-like settings. The findings suggest that, in environments where social sanctions for dishonesty are weak or inconsistently applied, the efficiency gains from reputation mechanisms (like gossip) are limited by the prevalence and detectability of dishonesty. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment or sanction mechanisms in improving efficiency depends not only on their presence but also on their alignment with actual dishonest behavior and the social context (e.g., whether negative but honest information is socially penalized). The paper does not manipulate classic PGG punishment dimensions (cost, tech, etc.), but it does show that the ability to sanction dishonesty is necessary but not sufficient for efficiency gains.

- source: Oberholzer-Gee, F., Waldfogel, J., & White, M. W. (2010). FRIEND OR FOE? COOPERATION AND LEARNING IN HIGH-STAKES GAMES. *Review of Economics and Statistics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study documents that, over two seasons of the game show, players shift from unconditional to conditional cooperation based on observable opponent characteristics (e.g., gender, race, age). In the first season, cooperation rates are unrelated to opponent attributes; in the second season, players are more likely to defect (choose 'foe') against opponents whose attributes predict less cooperation. This learning process leads to a substantial decline in overall efficiency: the average share of stakes converted to winnings drops from 39% in season 1 to 30% in season 2. The decline is especially pronounced for players with attributes associated with low cooperation (e.g., men, black players, younger players), whose average efficiency falls sharply. In contrast, players with more cooperative-associated attributes (e.g., women, white players) maintain their efficiency. The findings suggest that conditional cooperation, when based on imperfect signals, can reduce group efficiency in one-shot, high-stakes settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in one-shot, simultaneous-move games structurally similar to the prisoner's dilemma, the emergence of conditional cooperation based on observable attributes can reduce overall efficiency, especially when players use imperfect signals to predict others' behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling mechanisms that allow players to condition on opponent characteristics (e.g., through history, reputation, or observable traits) may decrease efficiency relative to baseline, particularly if the environment is not anonymous and players can stereotype. The findings are most relevant for games with binary (all-or-nothing) choices, no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and high stakes. The results caution that learning and conditional strategies do not always improve efficiency and may exacerbate payoff inequality.

- source: Al-Dhanhani, A., Mizouni, R., Otrok, H., & Al-Rubaie, A. (2014). A game theoretical model for collaborative groups in social applications. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of reciprocity and punishment strategies in repeated social dilemmas (modeled as asynchronous iterated prisoner's dilemma) has a strong effect on group efficiency and the distribution of gains between cooperative and selfish (free riding) users. Classical Tit for Tat leads to deadlock and declining payoffs. Generous Tit for Tat avoids deadlock and increases group gain, but allows free riders to benefit disproportionately. Reputation-based Tit for Tat reduces free rider gain but can also reduce cooperative user gain, especially if the reputation threshold is set high or the initialization phase is short. Group reputation-based Tit for Tat (indirect reciprocity) achieves the highest cooperative user gain and lowest free rider gain, thus maximizing group efficiency and survivability. The presence of free riders always reduces group efficiency, but the right punishment/reciprocity mechanism can mitigate this loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment/reciprocity mechanisms (especially those based on group reputation and indirect reciprocity) can significantly improve group efficiency in repeated social dilemma environments, even in the presence of free riders. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related and the efficiency outcomes are directly comparable. The findings suggest that enabling punishment or exclusion based on group-level reputation (rather than only direct reciprocity) is likely to increase efficiency relative to no punishment or to simple direct reciprocity. The results are most relevant for settings with repeated interactions, observable histories, and the ability to track and act on group-level reputation. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to all design dimensions, but the qualitative direction is clear: more sophisticated, group-based punishment/reciprocity mechanisms are more effective at sustaining efficiency.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhu, Y. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2020). Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems on Multiple Community Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Systems Man Cybernetics-systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the interaction network (community structure and interaction probabilities) and the payoff matrix jointly determine which strategies dominate and what equilibrium payoffs are achieved. In dominating games (like the prisoner's dilemma), the system converges to full defection or cooperation, mirroring well-mixed results. In coexistence games (like the snowdrift game), the system can reach an interior equilibrium with mixed strategies, provided certain conditions on the interaction matrix are met. In coordination games (like the stag-hunt), multiple stable equilibria are possible, and the final state depends on initial conditions and network structure. The paper provides sufficient conditions for the stability of equilibria and shows that higher payoff strategies tend to dominate. However, it does not analyze the effect of punishment, reward, or other institutional interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and payoff matrices affect equilibrium efficiency and welfare in multiagent evolutionary games. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it model public goods games directly. Its results are most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in structured populations without institutional interventions. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from punishment or reward mechanisms, but it does clarify how efficiency depends on game type and network structure in the absence of such mechanisms.

- source: Wang, R. W., & Shi, L. (2010). The evolution of cooperation in asymmetric systems. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric cooperative systems, the net benefit (efficiency) to the recipient depends critically on the intrinsic growth rates of benefit and cost, the strength of density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors, and the availability of common resources. Stronger density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors and higher initial relatedness (intrinsic contribution ratio) increase the probability of maintaining cooperation (positive efficiency). However, changes in these parameters or resource saturation can lead to a transition from cooperation to conflict, with net benefit becoming negative. The model also shows that recipient-enforced discrimination (punishing cheaters, rewarding cooperators) can increase initial relatedness and favor cooperation, but does not guarantee global stability. The system can exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics between cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how efficiency in public-goods-like asymmetric systems is affected by the number of actors, resource saturation, and density-dependent competition. It suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation are not guaranteed by punishment or sanctions alone, but depend on ecological and structural parameters such as the strength of interference competition and resource availability. For prediction tasks, the model implies that enabling punishment (in the form of recipient-enforced discrimination) can increase efficiency only when it effectively increases initial relatedness and when density-dependent competition among actors is strong enough to prevent resource overexploitation. However, efficiency can decrease or oscillate if these conditions are not met. The findings are most relevant for systems with asymmetric roles and may be less directly transferable to standard symmetric PGG lab games.

- source: Szabó, G., & Bunth, G. (2018). Social dilemmas in multistrategy evolutionary potential games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in symmetric potential games, efficiency (measured as average payoff or contentment) is maximized when the symmetric part of the payoff matrix dominates, aligning individual and collective interests. The introduction of antisymmetric (deluding) components can create social dilemmas, leading to inefficient Nash equilibria and lower average payoffs. The likelihood of inefficiency increases with the number of strategies. However, if the antisymmetric component supports the same equilibrium as the symmetric part, it can help maintain efficiency, especially under noise. The paper provides explicit mappings of parameter regions where efficiency is high or low, but does not study punishment or public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the structure of payoff matrices in multi-strategy evolutionary games affects efficiency, especially the role of antisymmetric (deluding) components in creating or resolving social dilemmas. While it does not study public goods games or punishment directly, it offers a framework for predicting when efficiency will be high or low based on the alignment of individual and collective incentives. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that efficiency is sensitive to the presence of payoff components that create conflicts between individual and group interests, and that the probability of inefficiency increases with the number of available strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or specific public goods game design dimensions.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). When agreement-accepting free-riders are a necessary evil for the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot games, the coexistence of proposers and acceptors can be necessary to keep defectors out of the population, especially when the cost of commitment must be paid before knowing if the other will accept (prior commitments). In repeated games, proposers alone can sustain cooperation, and the presence of acceptors can actually reduce cooperation by making it easier for defectors to invade. The introduction of an apology-forgiveness mechanism (where apologies after defection can restore cooperation) can be exploited by acceptors if the apology cost is too low, leading to lower cooperation. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and the stationary distribution of strategies, with average payoff as the fitness criterion, but do not directly report group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does offer adjacent theoretical insights about how commitment mechanisms (which can be seen as a form of pre-emptive contract or compensation for defection) affect the prevalence of cooperation and the exclusion of defectors. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (timing of costs, ability to recognize intentions, presence of apology-forgiveness) can strongly moderate the success of cooperation-promoting strategies and thus the average payoff in the population. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma and not a public goods game, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning as in PGGs, its direct predictive value for the downstream task is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding how alternative mechanisms to punishment (commitments, compensation, apology) might affect cooperation and payoff in related social dilemmas.

- source: Han, T. A., & Tran-Thanh, L. (2018). Cost-effective external interference for promoting the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: This work provides analytical and numerical results showing that an external decision-maker can promote high levels of cooperation in a population of self-interested agents by using a cost-efficient, state-dependent rewarding scheme. Rather than always rewarding cooperators, it is more cost-effective to reward only when the number of cooperators is below a threshold. The optimal threshold depends on the per-individual reward cost, the intensity of selection, and the desired cooperation level. For a wide range of parameters, this targeted approach achieves the same or higher cooperation at a lower total cost than always-on institutional incentives. The model does not directly analyze group payoff or efficiency in the public goods sense, but the results are closely related to maximizing cooperation frequency while minimizing resource expenditure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for predicting the effect of reward-based interventions (not punishment) on cooperation and cost-efficiency in Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. It suggests that targeted, conditional incentives can be more cost-effective than unconditional ones, especially under weak selection. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not analyze punishment, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency with punishment are not possible. The findings support the idea that the design of incentive schemes (e.g., when and whom to reward) strongly moderates the cost-effectiveness of cooperation promotion, and that always-on incentives may be suboptimal. For downstream prediction, the results are most informative for settings with external, state-dependent rewards, and less so for punishment or standard PGGs.

- source: Fulker, Z., Forber, P., Smead, R., & Riedl, C. (2021). Spite is contagious in dynamic networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic networks where agents can choose interaction partners and update their strategies by imitating higher-payoff individuals, spiteful (costly harmful) behavior can reliably invade and fixate in the population. This occurs even when the overall network does not exhibit strong anti-correlation, due to local and transient anti-correlated interactions. The spread of spite is facilitated by reinforcement learning and imitation based on average payoff per interaction. When spiteful behavior dominates, average payoffs are lower than in populations of only 'Social' agents, indicating a reduction in group efficiency. The findings challenge classic conditions for the evolution of costly harm and suggest that dynamic partner choice is a powerful mechanism for the emergence of antisocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is primarily theoretical and does not directly study public goods games or experimental punishment interventions. However, it provides indirect evidence that dynamic partner choice and imitation based on payoffs can enable the spread of costly harmful behaviors, which reduce group efficiency. In environments where agents can choose partners and update strategies based on payoffs, the introduction of punishment-like costly harm may decrease efficiency, especially if such behaviors can spread by imitation. The findings suggest caution in predicting positive efficiency effects from punishment in dynamic or endogenous network settings, as costly harm can become self-reinforcing and reduce welfare. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not include a cooperative contribution stage, so direct quantitative prediction is limited, but the qualitative mechanism is relevant for understanding the risks of antisocial punishment or spite in flexible social environments.

- source: Wu, J. H., Számadó, S., Barclay, P., Beersma, B., Cruz, T. D. D., Lo Iacono, S., Nieper, A. S., Peters, K., Przepiorka, W., Tiokhin, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Honesty and dishonesty in gossip strategies: a fitness interdependence analysis. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the honesty of gossip is determined by the gossiper's fitness interdependence with the receiver and target, and by the marginal cost/benefit structure of the underlying game between receiver and target. A simple matching rule is proposed: gossipers are honest when their fitness interdependence aligns with the effect of honest gossip, and dishonest when it mismatches. The model is applied to four game types, including a punishment game, but the focus is on the strategic choice of gossip honesty rather than on direct efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests that honest gossip can support cooperation, but that dishonest gossip can undermine reputation-based cooperation, especially when fitness interdependence is negative or mixed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information transmission (gossip) can act as a low-cost reputational punishment mechanism in social dilemmas, including punishment games. However, it does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze the standard public goods game dimensions. Its main contribution is to clarify when gossip will be honest or dishonest, which can indirectly affect cooperation and efficiency, but the paper does not quantify these effects. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the importance of information accuracy and fitness interdependence in the effectiveness of reputational sanctions.

- source: Nakashima, N. A., Halali, E., & Halevy, N. (2017). Third parties promote cooperative norms in repeated interactions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party intervention in repeated adversarial interactions (modeled as a Peacemaker Game) increases cooperation rates among adversaries. Early intervention is more effective at establishing persistent cooperative norms than late intervention. Third parties are more likely to intervene when adversaries have a history of cooperation, and adversaries are more likely to cooperate following third party intervention. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and intervention rates), not efficiency or group payoff. The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Halevy, N., & Halali, E. (2015). Selfish third parties act as peacemakers by transforming conflicts and promoting cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) The possibility and higher probability of third-party intervention increase cooperation rates among disputants in both interpersonal and intergroup contexts. (2) Third parties' willingness to intervene is highly sensitive to the risk and potential rewards of intervention; reducing risk or increasing potential gain increases intervention rates. (3) Despite the potential for increased collective gains, third parties often intervene less frequently than would be optimal, leading to missed opportunities for higher group payoffs. (4) The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as primary outcomes, but discusses them in the context of missed opportunities and the structure of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the introduction of a punishment/reward mechanism (via third-party intervention) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma-like settings, even when the underlying game is not a standard public goods game. However, because the primary outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/intervention rates) and not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms (including the risk and incentive structure for enforcers) is critical for their effectiveness. The evidence is most relevant for adjacent settings where third-party intervention is possible, but less directly transferable to standard PGGs with endogenous peer punishment.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Mui, V. L. (2014). Coordinating Resistance Through Communication and Repeated Interaction. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that repetition alone only modestly reduces leader DAC (transgression) rates, as leaders can punish beneficiaries who resist, deterring future resistance. Communication between responders, even when non-binding and private, substantially increases the rate of coordinated resistance and reduces leader DAC. The combination of repetition and communication is most effective at reducing DAC. The study also shows that hiding responder identities from the leader (so the leader cannot target challenging beneficiaries) increases resistance. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total surplus, or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The results are most relevant for understanding how communication and repeated interaction affect coordination in social dilemmas with endogenous roles and targeted punishment, rather than standard public goods games with peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the importance of communication and repeated interaction for coordinated resistance in social dilemmas with endogenous roles. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that communication between potential punishers (or resisters) can be as important as, or more important than, repetition for deterring exploitative behavior. The results highlight that the ability to target punishers (e.g., identifying who punished) can undermine the effectiveness of resistance. For prediction tasks in public goods games, the main lesson is that communication channels and anonymity of punishment/reward can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes, but it does suggest that communication is a powerful moderator.

- source: Garrett, J. B., Livingston, J. A., Tayler, W. B., Cade, N. L., & McVay, S. E. (2019). Controls and Cooperation in Interactive and Non-Interactive Settings. *Contemporary Accounting Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a control (audit with bonus) in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases subsequent cooperative behavior (measured as investment in a trust game) for both participants who directly experienced the control and those who only observed it, but the effect is significantly larger for those who directly benefited from cooperation. The effect is mediated by observed cooperation in the initial game. The results suggest that controls can foster trust and reciprocity, and that these effects are stronger in interactive settings. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the main outcome is behavioral (investment/cooperation), not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that control mechanisms (akin to monitoring or incentive-based controls) can increase cooperative behavior in subsequent social dilemmas, especially in interactive settings. However, because the main outcome is investment in a trust game (a behavioral measure) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not involve explicit punishment or sanctions, but rather a positive incentive (bonus for high contribution). The results suggest that mechanisms increasing observed cooperation can have positive spillovers on future cooperation, but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Samu, F., & Takács, K. (2021). Evaluating mechanisms that could support credible reputations and cooperation: cross-checking and social bonding. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment found that allowing for cross-checking of gossip or opportunities for social bonding did not significantly improve cooperation rates compared to a control. High competition for reputation increased cooperation, but overall cooperation declined over time in all conditions. Gossip was generally honest but became more negative about rivals under high competition. Reputation scores and gossip valence influenced conditional cooperation, but the mechanisms tested did not create a reliable path to sustained high cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of communication, reputation, and competition on cooperation in repeated dyadic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not include explicit costly punishment or group-level payoff analysis. Findings suggest that mechanisms to improve the credibility of social information (cross-checking, social bonding) may not be sufficient to sustain cooperation, and that competition for reputation can increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper offers only contextual or indirect guidance.

- source: Bednarik, P., Fehl, K., & Semmann, D. (2014). Costs for switching partners reduce network dynamics but not cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing costs for forming new links in dynamic networks reduces the rate at which participants break links (i.e., switch partners). However, cooperation rates in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma remain high across all dynamic treatments, regardless of the cost level, and are significantly higher than in static networks where no partner switching is allowed. The ability to break links—even if rarely used—appears to be enough to maintain high cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of these dynamics on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with dynamic partner networks, the introduction of costs for switching partners reduces network dynamism but does not reduce cooperation rates. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment or partner-switching mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that the mere threat of partner switching can sustain cooperation, but the efficiency implications (e.g., total group payoff relative to maximum possible) remain unaddressed.

- source: Guo, H., Chu, C., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2018). Reputation-based coevolution of link weights promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based coevolution of link weights in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game promotes cooperation. When link weights are allowed to vary (parameterized by delta), higher heterogeneity in link weights leads to higher levels of cooperation. Punishment is implemented as a reduction in link weight to the lowest-reputation neighbor, and reward as an increase to the highest-reputation neighbor. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The effect of punishment and reward is to facilitate the formation of cooperative clusters, which resist invasion by defectors. The effect is stronger for larger delta (greater possible link weight variation).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemma settings. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game), and the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of network structure, reputation, and coevolving relationships in promoting cooperation, but do not provide quantitative guidance for efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Zhen, S. S., & Yu, R. J. (2016). Tend to Compare and Tend to Be Fair: The Relationship between Social Comparison Sensitivity and Justice Sensitivity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Mancini, A., Betti, V., Panasiti, M. S., Pavone, E. F., & Aglioti, S. M. (2011). Suffering Makes You Egoist: Acute Pain Increases Acceptance Rates and Reduces Fairness during a Bilateral Ultimatum Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that acute pain leads to higher acceptance rates of unfair offers and lower fairness in offers made, suggesting that pain induces more self-interested behavior in both roles of the Ultimatum Game. The effect is specific to pain (not just heat) and is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The intensity of pain predicts acceptance rates for these offers. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to PGGs, and focuses on the effect of pain (a state manipulation) rather than institutional punishment. The outcomes are behavioral (acceptance, offer size) rather than payoff-based. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited.

- source: Brevers, D., Noël, X., Ermer, E., Dabiri, D., Verbanck, P., & Kornreich, C. (2013). Unfairness sensitivity and social decision-making in individuals with alcohol dependence: A preliminary study. *Drug and Alcohol Dependence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Alcohol-dependent individuals are more likely to reject unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game compared to healthy controls, suggesting greater emotional reactivity to unfairness and reduced ability to make self-advantageous decisions in social contexts involving frustration. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and the design does not include a punishment phase or a repeated public goods structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to the target environment, as rejection of unfair offers can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment, but the context is one-shot, dyadic, and does not involve group cooperation or repeated interactions. The findings may inform understanding of individual differences in punishment sensitivity, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or the effect of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen. This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2020). Self-regulation versus social influence for promoting cooperation on networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that strong self-regulation (relative to social influence from neighbors) can lead to full cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games, while weak self-regulation leads to defection. For intermediate self-regulation, complex behaviors such as partial cooperation, reciprocity, and anti-reciprocity emerge. Network topology matters: scale-free networks foster higher average cooperation than Erdös-Rényi or regular networks, especially when self-regulation is moderate. The model suggests that social diversity and heterogeneity in network structure can promote cooperation, even when the 'cost' of self-regulation is not high. However, the study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with self-regulation (an internal, not externally imposed, mechanism) and analyzes cooperation levels, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no manipulation of punishment, punishment cost, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how intrinsic motivation or network structure can affect cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Mellers, B. A., Haselhuhn, M. P., Tetlock, P. E., Silva, J. C., & Isen, A. M. (2010). Predicting Behavior in Economic Games by Looking Through the Eyes of the Players. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Players' self-defined want and ought motives predict their offers in ultimatum and dictator games; (2) in the ultimatum game, more players act according to what they believe is fair (ought), while in the dictator game, want and ought are equally influential; (3) positive mood induction increases the likelihood of making equal offers in the dictator game; (4) when given an exit option, players experiencing greater conflict between want and ought are more likely to exit. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in public goods or repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment institutions, marginal per-capita return, or group efficiency. Its findings on rejections in the ultimatum game and exit behavior in the dictator game are only loosely related to the prediction task.   findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of the asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, showing that the frequency of cooperation increases with greater asymmetry between players (i.e., when one player is much stronger or more dominant than the other). The model also shows that a lower cost-to-benefit ratio of cooperation increases cooperation frequency. The discussion links these findings to real-world systems where dominant individuals can punish non-cooperators, suggesting that credible punishment and asymmetry can stabilize cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the probability of cooperation and conflict.

Its relevance to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited, as it analyzes a two-player Hawk-Dove game rather than a multi-player public goods game, and focuses on cooperation frequency rather than efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual, not formalized as a game design dimension. Thus, while the paper suggests that punishment and asymmetry can facilitate cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

  findings: The study documents that in Enga customary courts, norm violations are addressed primarily through restorative justice: compensation to victims, reintegration of wrongdoers, and broad third-party participation in settlements. Punitive sanctions (punishment) are rare and not institutionally emphasized. Third parties contribute to compensation both during and outside court hearings, motivated by kinship, reputation, and community harmony. Repeated offenders and free riders receive less support over time. The system is designed to maintain cooperation and social cohesion, not to maximize punishment or deterrence. The paper argues that such restorative systems are more effective for sustaining cooperation in small-scale societies than third-party punishment, but does not provide quantitative efficiency or payoff data.

While it does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment is not the primary or most effective means of sustaining cooperation in such environments. For prediction tasks involving public goods games or efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly informative for understanding the limits of punishment's effectiveness and the importance of alternative, restorative mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence for how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental settings.

  findings: The paper reviews the literature on indirect reciprocity, emphasizing how the complexity of social norms and strategies affects the evolution and maintenance of cooperation. It synthesizes theoretical and computational results showing that: (1) more complex social norms can, in principle, sustain higher cooperation, but are harder for individuals to implement; (2) the costliness of reputation spreading, the observability of interactions, and empathy are key moderators of cooperation under indirect reciprocity; (3) when complexity costs are introduced, simpler norms may outperform more complex ones in sustaining cooperation. The paper provides a proof-of-concept simulation showing that cooperation rates under different social norms decline as the cost of implementing complex strategies increases. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or direct welfare outcomes, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment in a public goods context.

For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides only indirect, contextual background, not quantitative or directly transferable evidence.

Theoretical results show that, under certain conditions (notably negative frequency dependence in deception detection), cooperation and deception can coexist at equilibrium. Comparative analysis in primates supports the prediction that more cooperative species exhibit more tactical deception. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters.

While it discusses punishment as one of several conditional cooperation mechanisms, it does not analyze the quantitative impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the idea that enforcement mechanisms can have complex evolutionary consequences, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation evolved in two major transitions: first, among Pleistocene foragers, where cooperation was individually advantageous and stabilized by mutualism, reciprocation, and punishment of freeriders (both shirkers and bullies); and second, in the Holocene, where larger, more hierarchical societies required new mechanisms (including group selection and intergroup conflict) to maintain cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a key mechanism for controlling freeriding and maintaining cooperation, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than experimental or quantitative. The paper references experimental public goods games and their findings (e.g., punishment stabilizes cooperation), but only as background for theoretical arguments.

It does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. However, it supports the idea that punishment is more likely to stabilize cooperation (and thus efficiency) in small, transparent, repeated-interaction groups, and that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on social structure, group size, and transparency. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as background for mechanism hypotheses rather than as a source of direct predictive evidence.

  findings: The paper finds that requiring agents to achieve a minimum payoff threshold (environmental pressure) in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games can lead to the emergence of cooperation, even when the payoff for mutual defection is not very harsh. The fraction of cooperators increases with the threshold up to a point, and the addition of a Pavlovian update rule (win-stay, lose-shift) further increases cooperation and leads to power-law cluster size distributions in some parameter regions. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms beyond the standard PD payoffs.

There is no analysis of group efficiency or total payoff, and no manipulation of punishment or sanctioning technology. The findings may be of some indirect relevance for understanding how environmental constraints can promote cooperation, but do not provide evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.
