The strength of the reputation effect and the reputation adjustment factor both positively influence cooperation rates. However, all reported outcomes are in terms of cooperation frequency and network structure, not group payoff or efficiency.

While it models a form of punishment (link-type strategists defecting against defectors) and shows that this increases cooperation rates, it does not report on efficiency, welfare, or group payoff. The model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment mechanism is endogenous to strategy choice rather than an explicit costly punishment option. Thus, the paper may provide some indirect support for the idea that targeted sanctions can promote cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment costs or technology, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms (partner choice, punishment) are relevant, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The main value is in supporting the importance of partner choice and the robustness of punishment as a behavioral response.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2021). Cooperation in large-scale human societies-What, if anything, makes it unique, and how did it evolve?. *Evolutionary Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/A | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the key to sustaining cooperation in large-scale societies is the development of new institutional rules (social interaction mechanisms) that make prosocial behavior payoff-compatible for self-interested individuals, rather than a shift to decision-making mechanisms that are insensitive to payoff (e.g., strong conformity or prestige bias). It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that, in both small- and large-scale societies, humans are generally sensitive to material payoffs in social dilemmas, and that institutions such as monitoring, sanctioning, and reputation systems evolve to support cooperation. The paper highlights the importance of distinguishing between cooperative (payoff-increasing for all) and altruistic (payoff-decreasing for the actor) prosocial behaviors, and suggests that in large-scale societies, cooperation among non-relatives is typically cooperative, not altruistic, due to institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that the effect of punishment and institutional rules on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on whether those rules make cooperation payoff-compatible for self-interested agents. It suggests that, in predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, one should focus on the design of the social interaction mechanism (e.g., the structure and effectiveness of punishment, monitoring, and reward systems) and the degree to which these mechanisms align individual incentives with group welfare. However, the paper does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative relationships between game design dimensions and efficiency outcomes. Its value is in framing the importance of institutional design and payoff-sensitivity, rather than in providing parameter-specific predictions.

- source: Artavia-Mora, L., Bedi, A. S., & Rieger, M. (2017). Intuitive help and punishment in the field. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that time delay (deliberation) reduces the likelihood of helping strangers and norm violators in the field, with helping rates dropping by 18-27 percentage points depending on the scenario. However, time delay does not significantly affect the rate of direct punishment of norm violators. The study also finds that time delay increases self-reported fear of retaliation for punishing. The design is a one-shot, non-strategic, individual-level field experiment, not a public goods game or its direct variant, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar strategic environments. Its design lacks group interaction, repeated rounds, contribution choices, or payoff structures analogous to PGGs. The main outcomes are binary helping or punishment actions in response to norm violations, not group payoffs or efficiency. While the findings may be contextually informative about the psychological underpinnings of prosocial and punitive behavior, they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. The study is best used as contextual or mechanistic background, not as direct input for efficiency prediction models.

- source: Kaltwasser, L., Hildebrandt, A., Wilhelm, O., & Sommer, W. (2016). Behavioral and neuronal determinants of negative reciprocity in the ultimatum game. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both prosociality (as measured by social value orientation) and assertiveness predict the likelihood of rejecting unfair offers in the ultimatum game, supporting both the strong reciprocity and status defense models of negative reciprocity. Neural data (feedback negativity) show that individuals with higher negative reciprocity have stronger neural responses to proposer fairness. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. The findings are limited to the behavioral and neural correlates of negative reciprocity in the UG, not to efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is an ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and the main outcome is the rejection rate of unfair offers (a form of costly punishment), not group payoff or efficiency. While the study is relevant for understanding individual motivations for costly punishment (negative reciprocity), it does not inform how punishment affects group efficiency or payoff under different game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be weighted strongly for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes.

- source: de Melo, C. M., Terada, K., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Emotion expressions shape human social norms and reputations. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that participants cooperate more with counterparts who have positive or unknown reputations, use generous strategies, and display cooperative emotion expressions. Reputation, strategy, and emotion all independently and interactively affect cooperation rates and how reputations are updated. Emotion expressions can help forgive defectors with good reputations or rehabilitate those with bad reputations if they behave generously and express cooperative emotions. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses an iterated prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and focuses on cooperation rates and reputation rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the sense of costly punishment; instead, the study manipulates emotion expressions and reputation. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited. It may be useful for understanding how nonverbal cues and reputation affect cooperation, but not for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Singh, M., & Garfield, Z. H. (2022). Evidence for third-party mediation but not punishment in Mentawai justice. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in the Mentawai context, third-party punishment is absent: no cases were found where third parties (outside the victim, their close kin, or spouse) directly punished or demanded penalties from transgressors. Indirect or second-order sanctions by third parties are also not observed. Instead, penalties (tulou) are negotiated and paid primarily to restore dyadic cooperation, with kinship playing a major role in the likelihood and size of payments. Mediation by third parties (especially shamans and elders) is common, particularly in more severe or between-clan disputes, but the mediator's role is to facilitate negotiation, not to enforce punishment. The study suggests that, in this small-scale society, institutionalized penalties serve to repair relationships rather than to enforce group-level norms through punishment. The findings are based on retrospective interviews and ethnographic observation of 302 reliable cases of transgression and response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The studied context is not a PGG or close variant, and the outcomes are not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in small-scale, kin-based societies, third-party punishment is rare or absent, and penalties serve to restore dyadic cooperation rather than to enforce group norms or increase group efficiency. If extrapolating to PGG-like settings, the results would suggest that the presence or absence of third-party punishment may depend on the social structure and the nature of cooperation (private vs. public goods), but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Thus, it is of limited use for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that punishment mechanisms may not always function as assumed in small-scale, kin-based societies.

- source: Price, M. E. (2006). Judgments about cooperators and freeriders on a Shuar work team: An evolutionary psychological perspective. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Shuar villagers overwhelmingly judge higher contributors to a collective work project more positively (more respect, less punishment) than lower contributors, regardless of how much the worker benefits from the project. When contribution is held constant and benefit varies, those who participate more in collective actions (mainly men) are more likely to favor 'altruists' (high contributors, low benefit) and disfavor 'freeriders' (low contributors, high benefit), while low-participation subjects (mainly women) tend to favor those who benefit more, regardless of contribution. The study does not measure actual group payoffs, efficiency, or the effects of real punishment or reward options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that social judgments (including support for punishment) are strongly influenced by perceived contribution, and that individuals who participate more in collective actions are more likely to support punishing freeriders and favoring cooperators. The findings suggest that in environments where social judgment or informal sanctions are possible, support for punishing low contributors may be widespread, especially among frequent cooperators. However, because the study does not implement real punishment, measure actual payoffs, or use a public goods game design, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited and indirect.

- source: Voorhees, B., Read, D., & Gabora, L. (2020). Identity, Kinship, and the Evolution of Cooperation. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The authors propose that human cooperation is uniquely enabled by the evolution of cultural idea systems and kinship-based social identities, which prescribe cooperative behavior and motivate punishment of norm violators as a defense of social identity. They argue that this top-down, culturally mediated mechanism overcomes the second-order free rider problem and stabilizes cooperation in human groups. The paper does not present empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but offers a conceptual account of how cooperation and punishment are maintained in human societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. Its value for the prediction task is limited to high-level theoretical context: it suggests that the presence and strength of culturally mediated identities and norms may be important moderators of cooperation and punishment, but it does not specify how game design dimensions or control efficiency would quantitatively affect efficiency when punishment is enabled. The paper does not address or analyze any of the target game design dimensions in a way that would inform direct prediction.

- source: Balliet, D., Molho, C., Columbus, S., & Cruz, T. D. D. (2022). Prosocial and punishment behaviors in everyday life. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial and punishment behaviors in daily life are highly context-dependent, varying with relationship quality, power dynamics, and the nature of the interaction. Direct punishment (as seen in lab games) is rare in daily life, especially among strangers; indirect forms like gossip and avoidance are more common. The review emphasizes that laboratory paradigms (such as public goods games with punishment) capture only a small subset of real-world social behavior, and that field methods reveal a broader range of strategies and contexts. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It is primarily a theoretical and methodological review, arguing that lab-based findings may not generalize to real-world contexts. The paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze the game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in highlighting the limitations of laboratory paradigms and the importance of context, but it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency effects from punishment in controlled games.

- source: Roberts, G. (2020). Honest signaling of cooperative intentions. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that costly signaling of cooperative intent can evolve as an honest signal when the long-term benefits of repeated cooperation outweigh the costs of signaling. In such environments, agents who signal are more likely to be chosen as partners, and those who are chosen are more likely to cooperate, leading to higher payoffs for both signalers and choosers. The model predicts that efficiency (total payoff) is maximized when signaling costs are moderate and the number of repeated interactions is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and is based on dyadic rather than group interactions, but the findings are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can support efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms allowing individuals to signal cooperative intent and choose partners based on such signals can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the model is closely related and suggests that, in environments where reputation-building is possible and partner choice is allowed, efficiency will be higher than in environments without these mechanisms. The absence of punishment in the model means it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of costly signaling and partner choice as alternative or complementary mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling reputation-building and partner choice (analogous to some forms of communication or signaling in PGGs) can increase efficiency, especially when the cost of signaling is not prohibitive and the benefits of cooperation are high.

- source: van Leeuwen, B., Schram, A., & Offerman, T. (2020). COMPETITION FOR STATUS CREATES SUPERSTARS: AN EXPERIMENT ON PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION AND NETWORK FORMATION. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that competition for status (via status rents for incoming links) is a powerful mechanism for achieving efficient public good provision in repeated network games. When status rents are high, groups tend to form stable star or superstar networks, with a single core player providing the public good at or above the efficient level, and periphery players free-riding. Efficiency (measured as group earnings or welfare relative to the efficient benchmark) is much higher with status rents, especially in larger groups. In contrast, without status rents, groups fail to coordinate on efficient outcomes, and efficiency is low. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It is not suitable for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as punishment is not present in any treatment. However, it does provide high-quality evidence on how other mechanisms (status rents, endogenous network formation, group size) affect efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms that can increase efficiency in repeated public goods environments.

- source: Panchanathan, K., Frankenhuis, W. E., & Silk, J. B. (2013). The bystander effect in an N-person dictator game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds a strong bystander effect in an N-person dictator game: when there is only one dictator, recipients receive significantly more than when there are two or three dictators. The average transfer as a fraction of the 'equal share' drops from 57% (one dictator) to 36% (two or three dictators). Recipient payoffs are about 50% higher in the one-dictator condition than in the two- or three-dictator conditions. This effect persists even when uncertainty about others' actions is removed (strategy method) and when dictators can communicate and coordinate (bargaining method). The results suggest that most participants transform the situation into a prisoner's dilemma, leading to low transfers in groups. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but discusses the possibility that introducing punishment could alleviate the bystander effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the number of potential contributors in a many-to-one helping game (adjacent to PGG) reduces efficiency/welfare, even when communication or strategy methods are used. The absence of punishment or sanctions means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does establish a robust baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency declines with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar games without punishment, efficiency will be lower as group size increases. It also suggests that communication alone may not be sufficient to restore efficiency. However, since punishment is not present, the paper cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, only to set a control baseline.

- source: McClung, J. S., Placì, S., Bangerter, A., Clément, F., & Bshary, R. (2017). The language of cooperation: shared intentionality drives variation in helping as a function of group membership. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper introduces a joint foraging game (the 'egg hunt') in which pairs of participants can help each other at a cost to themselves, with helping not explicitly instructed. The design manipulates group membership (in-group vs out-group) and the ability to talk (talking vs no talking). Both in-group membership and the ability to talk significantly increased costly helping and overall group success (total screws collected, which determined monetary payoffs). Shared intentionality in conversation was identified as the key psychological mechanism mediating the effect of group membership on helping. Costly helping was costly to the helper but increased total group payoff. There were no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The game is adjacent to public goods or prisoner's dilemma games, but differs in that helping is not explicitly structured and there is no punishment or reward system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling communication (chat) and making group membership salient can increase efficiency (total group payoff) in joint action games where helping is possible but not enforced. The effect is mediated by shared intentionality in communication. While the game is not a standard public goods game and does not include punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games where communication and group identity are manipulated. The absence of punishment means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that communication alone can have a strong positive effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of chat/communication and group identity, but does not provide evidence about punishment, punishment cost, or related dimensions.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2019). Nonconformity of cooperators promotes the emergence of pure altruism in tag-based multi-agent networked systems. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in tag-based multi-agent systems, the combination of spatial structure (small-world networks) and the presence of nonconforming (free) altruists, together with conformity-biased transmission of other strategies, is necessary for the stable dominance of pure altruism (unconditional cooperation). Conformity alone, or spatial structure alone, is insufficient to produce this outcome. The model shows that even a small fraction (>1%) of nonconforming altruists can drive the system toward near-complete altruism, resulting in higher mean fitness and group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are robust across a range of network parameters and conversion sensitivities. The results are supported by both agent-based simulations and mathematical analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about the role of conformity, spatial structure, and unconditional cooperation in evolutionary games with tag-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does provide a strong theoretical and computational baseline for understanding how other mechanisms (conformity, spatial structure, nonconformity) can promote efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment. If a model or prediction task involves tag-based cooperation, conformity, or spatial structure, the findings here suggest that these factors can be powerful moderators of efficiency, but the effect of punishment would need to be assessed from other sources.

- source: Chiang, Y. S. (2015). Good Samaritans in Networks: An Experiment on How Networks Influence Egalitarian Sharing and the Evolution of Inequality. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that voluntary sharing in networks can reduce inequality, but the extent depends on network topology. When networks connect the very rich and very poor (Lattice_Hetero, SF_Negative), local inequality is salient and motivates more redistribution, leading to significant reductions in the Gini coefficient. In other network structures (Full, Lattice_Homo, SF_Positive), inequality is not significantly reduced. The study does not involve public goods provision, punishment, or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency as the ratio of group payoff to the fully cooperative benchmark. The findings are most relevant to how network structure moderates voluntary redistribution and the evolution of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in the sense required for the downstream prediction task. The findings are about voluntary redistribution and inequality in networked settings, which is adjacent but not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The paper may provide some contextual insight into how network structure affects redistribution, but it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Molleman, L., Pen, I., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Effects of Conformism on the Cultural Evolution of Social Behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of conformism on the evolution of cooperation depends on the game structure. In social dilemmas, conformism can make cooperation a stable equilibrium if it is already common, but in group-structured populations, conformism hinders the spread of cooperation because it prevents rare cooperative strategies from invading. In evasion games, conformism erodes polymorphic equilibria and leads to fixation of a single strategy. The main outcomes are fixation probabilities and average payoffs at equilibrium, which are related to efficiency. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms, and does not directly model public goods games, but the results are relevant for understanding how social learning biases affect group-level cooperation and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how social learning rules (payoff-based vs. conformist) affect the spread and stability of cooperation and group payoffs in two-strategy games, including social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that mechanisms which stabilize cooperation (such as conformism) may not always increase group efficiency, especially in group-structured populations. However, since punishment is not modeled, and the game structure is not a PGG or its direct variant, this paper should be considered only for background or mechanistic context, not for quantitative prediction.

- source: Ye, W. X., & Fan, S. H. (2017). Evolutionary snowdrift game with rational selection based on radical evaluation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a rational selection mechanism, where players preferentially imitate neighbors with superior strategies as determined by radical evaluation, can significantly enhance the frequency of cooperation in the evolutionary snowdrift game, especially on regular networks with large neighborhood size and on scale-free networks. The effect of rational selection depends on network structure and the cost-to-benefit ratio. However, the study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. Its findings about rational selection and cooperation in the snowdrift game may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how imitation or reference mechanisms affect cooperation, but are not informative for the specific prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Brodbeck, F. C., Kugler, K. G., Reif, J. A. M., & Maier, M. A. (2013). Morals Matter in Economic Games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across four lab experiments, participants induced to adopt a Unity moral motive (via framing or subliminal priming) allocated more money to another participant in a one-shot risk-sharing game than those induced to adopt a Proportionality motive. This effect was only present in interpersonal (DSG) and not solitary (SIG) settings. The study does not use a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about the psychological determinants of solidarity behavior (unconditional giving) in risk-sharing contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that psychological framing (moral motives) can strongly affect other-regarding behavior in one-shot risk-sharing games. The design is not a PGG, and the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for the prediction task is limited.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Rule Following Mitigates Collaborative Cheating and Facilitates the Spreading of Honesty Within Groups. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individuals who are more likely to follow arbitrary, costly rules are also more likely to behave honestly in situations where cheating is possible and profitable. In dyadic and four-person group die-rolling tasks, groups with more high rule-followers exhibit less collaborative cheating (i.e., lower rates of reporting doubles and lower average reported die-rolls). The presence of even a single high rule-follower in a group can reduce collaborative cheating among low rule-followers. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or any formal enforcement mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes beyond the behavioral proxies for dishonesty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related enforcement mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. Its findings are relevant to understanding the role of individual differences in rule-following and the dynamics of collaborative cheating in the absence of punishment, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Li, J. Q., Zhang, C. Y., Sun, Q. L., Chen, Z. Q., & Zhang, J. L. (2017). Changing the Intensity of Interaction Based on Individual Behavior in the Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Ieee Transactions on Evolutionary Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that dynamically adjusting the intensity of interaction based on individual reputation and evaluation level can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial iterated prisoner's dilemma. For low or moderate temptation to defect, increasing the evaluation level monotonically increases cooperation; for higher temptation, there is an optimal evaluation level. The mechanism works by creating positive feedback between reputation and effective neighbors, facilitating the formation and expansion of cooperator clusters. However, the study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.

  findings: The study finds that participants are more likely to help ingroup than outgroup members, and more likely to help when decisions are public (i.e., reputational concerns are present). The effect of public/private decision setting is stronger in loss contexts than gain contexts, but the pattern of ingroup favouritism is similar in both. The cost/benefit (net expected value) of helping influences overall helping rates, but does not moderate the effect of group membership or decision setting. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.

- source: Capraro, V., & Marcelletti, A. (2014). Do good actions inspire good actions in others?. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that good actions (altruism or benevolence) do not inspire further good actions in anonymous, one-shot economic games. Specifically, being the recipient of a good action (either a split in a Dictator Game or a benevolent act in a Benevolence Game) does not significantly increase the probability of cooperating in a subsequent Prisoner's Dilemma or splitting in a subsequent Dictator Game. The results are robust to exclusion of participants who did not believe the other player was real or failed to provide a valid reason for their choice. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. - source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2013). Cooperation during cultural group formation promotes trust towards members of out-groups. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the degree of cooperation required during group formation (cooperative vs. independent production tasks) causally affects trust and reciprocity in subsequent trust games. Specifically, participants from cooperative production groups (CP) trust and reciprocate with out-group members at similar rates as with in-group members, showing little parochial altruism. In contrast, participants from independent production groups (IP) display significant in-group favouritism and out-group discrimination. These effects persist even after negative experiences (betrayal) in the trust game. The results are explained by differences in beliefs about out-group trustworthiness, shaped by the group formation process. No efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that the cooperative context of group formation can influence trust and reciprocity, but not in a payoff or efficiency framework. It should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2019). Collective Action for War and for Peace: A Case Study among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that collective action for war is often easier to mobilize than for peace, due to emotional triggers, private benefits for some individuals (especially young men), and the contagiousness of anger and revenge. Peacemaking, by contrast, requires overcoming internal rifts, mistrust, and the lack of unifying emotions, but is facilitated by strong leadership, compensation payments, and the involvement of broader social networks (including women and affinal kin). The development of rules and institutions for peacemaking (e.g., compensation, mediation) reduced the costs and destructiveness of war, promoted social complexity, and shifted incentives toward peace. However, the breakdown of traditional norms (e.g., with the introduction of guns) led to more destructive wars and greater reluctance to participate. Sanctions against free riders in war are rare due to the importance of brotherhood, but in peacemaking, non-contributors can be gradually reintegrated. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in a formal sense, but describes the social and material costs and benefits of war and peace.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It offers contextual and theoretical insights into how collective action, norm enforcement, and compensation (a form of sanction) operate in a real-world society, and how these mechanisms affect the costs and benefits of group action. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in a way that can be mapped to PGG outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in illustrating the complexity of sanctioning, the importance of leadership and social structure, and the potential for compensation to restore balance after conflict. It should be used as contextual background rather than as direct input for quantitative prediction.

The paper finds some small sex differences (women are sometimes more dyadic), and minor associations with age and relationship status, but the main result is the predominance of dyads. The discussion connects this to theories of reciprocity, coordination, and social exclusion, but does not empirically address cooperation, punishment, or efficiency in economic games.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kouchaki, M. (2021). Virtuous victims. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that people perceive victims of immorality as more moral and trustworthy than nonvictims, even when their behavior is identical. This effect is specific to moral traits and does not extend to nonmoral traits or to victims of accidents. The effect is explained by the Justice Restoration Hypothesis: seeing victims as virtuous motivates people to help them and punish perpetrators. The effect disappears when there are disincentives for justice-restorative action. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in economic games, but does show that perceived victim virtue increases willingness to punish and help in hypothetical contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or measure group payoff, efficiency, or actual economic outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that willingness to punish is motivated by perceptions of victim virtue, and that social incentives can modulate this willingness. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually relevant for understanding psychological motivations behind punishment and justice-restorative actions.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The study provides strong field evidence that upstream indirect reciprocity substantially increases prosocial behavior in one-shot interactions among strangers. Drivers who were helped by another driver were 32.1% likely to help a third party, compared to 14.6% in the baseline (a 119% increase). This effect is robust to gender, age, social class, presence of co-passengers, traffic congestion, and the physical distance (time elapsed) since being helped. The authors discuss that such increased prosociality could reduce waiting times and improve social welfare, especially during busy periods, but do not directly measure efficiency or payoff outcomes.

However, the design, context, and outcome measures are not directly transferable to public goods games with or without punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the behavioral drivers of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

  findings: The study finds that participants are more likely to lie for self-gain when facing an unfavorable outcome, but this tendency is reduced when their reputation is at risk (i.e., when the opponent will know about their lies). The presence of the opponent in the same room does not significantly affect lying rates. Personality traits, particularly reward dependence and social desirability, predict the degree to which reputation risk reduces lying. Manipulativeness and moral disengagement are associated with less sensitivity to reputation risk. The study does not involve punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings are limited to the behavioral determinants of lying under reputation risk and personality variation in a dyadic deception game. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Kardas, M., Shaw, A., & Caruso, E. M. (2018). How to Give Away Your Cake and Eat It Too: Relinquishing Control Prompts Reciprocal Generosity. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that when one person abdicates a resource allocation decision to another, the recipient is more likely to respond generously (give the better resource to the abdicator) than in a baseline where no abdication occurs. This effect is robust across hypothetical and real-stakes settings, and is mediated in part by perceptions of the abdicator's generosity. The studies are all dyadic, with two individuals and a binary allocation (better/worse resource), and do not involve repeated interaction, group cooperation, or any form of punishment or sanction. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design, outcomes, and mechanisms studied (abdication, reciprocity, perceived generosity) are not related to punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes in group cooperation games. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game-like settings.

- source: Capraro, V., Perc, M., & Vilone, D. (2020). Lying on networks: The role of structure and topology in promoting honesty. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that network structure has a nontrivial effect on the evolution of honesty and lying in the sender-receiver game. For black lies and altruistic white lies, honesty is more likely to evolve in small-world networks and, to a lesser extent, in the one-dimensional ring, compared to well-mixed populations. For Pareto white lies, lying is more likely to evolve in small-world networks. For spiteful lies, honesty always prevails regardless of network. The outcomes are reported in terms of the densities of liars and believers, not in terms of payoffs or efficiency. The sender-receiver game is fundamentally different from public goods games and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not study public goods games, does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on network structure and the evolution of honesty in the sender-receiver game are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGGs.

- source: Manesi, Z., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Pollet, T. V. (2015). Butterfly Eyespots: Their Potential Influence on Aesthetic Preferences and Conservation Attitudes. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that minimal eye-like patterns (eyespots) on butterflies increase their perceived attractiveness and elicit more positive attitudes toward their conservation. In some cases, eyespots also increase the likelihood of actual conservation behavior (e.g., donations), though this effect is mixed. The effect of eyespots on conservation attitudes is mediated by increased aesthetic preference. The study does not address economic games, punishment, or group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games, nor does it report payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are not transferable to the prediction task at hand.

- source: Cai, W., Huang, X. Q., Wu, S., & Kou, Y. (2015). Dishonest behavior is not affected by an image of watching eyes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that implicit reputation cues (images of watching eyes) do not reduce dishonest behavior in laboratory settings, even when dishonesty leads to higher monetary payoffs or reputational benefits. This null effect was consistent across different tasks and motivational framings. The study also finds that higher moral identity predicts less dishonesty, but the eyes image does not affect this. The pilot experiment replicates the known effect of eyes images on generosity in a dictator game, but the main experiments focus on dishonesty, not cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. The findings about implicit reputation cues and dishonesty may be of tangential interest for understanding the limits of non-punitive interventions, but do not inform the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.
